<note>These names represent Tycho Brahe's sixteen noble ancestors. In the 16th century, displaying such a pedigree was a way of asserting one's high social standing and authority, suggesting that the pursuit of science was a noble and dignified endeavor.</note>

> PORTRAIT OF TYCHO BRAHE, SON OF OTTE, A DANE,
> LORD OF KNUDSTRUP AND FOUNDER OF THE CASTLE OF URANIBORG
> ON THE ISLAND OF THE DANISH SOUND, HVEN,
> AND THE BUILDER OF THE ASTRONOMICAL
> INSTRUMENTS.
> AT THE AGE OF 50, IN THE YEAR 1595.

<note>The portrait described here shows Tycho in his prime. Uraniborg (the "Castle of the Heavens") was his world-famous observatory on the island of Hven. The inscription identifies him not just as a nobleman, but as the "structor" or builder of the very tools described in the book.</note>

--- Page 7 ---
<meta>This page begins the formal dedication and preface of Tycho Brahe’s "Instruments of Restored Astronomy," addressed to his patron, Holy Roman Emperor Rudolf II. It follows the title page which established Tycho’s noble lineage and his work at Wandsbeck.</meta>

### TO THE
## MOST AUGUST EM-
## PEROR
# RUDOLF THE SECOND
## BY TYCHO BRAHE
### *A PREFACE.*

**A**STRONOMY IS THE MOST ANCIENT SCIENCE, granted by God from the time of Adam the First-Father <note>original: "Adamo Protoplasto"</note> to the human race, and by far the most excellent, insofar as these celestial and sublime things surpass the earthly and lower ones. This Divine Astronomy, I say, drawing its origin from the very senses of the eyes observing the many wandering changes of the stars, has—regarding its outward expression—occupied both the spirits and the minds of the most excellent men for a long time since the creation of things. For such is the majesty of GOD, the Best and Thrice-Greatest <note>original: "ter maximi," a common Renaissance honorific for God, echoing the title of Hermes Trismegistus</note>, that the wisdom of His works can be exhausted by no creatures. 

But since the gaze of the eye alone was unable to capture all those mysteries and apparent intricate varieties of the beyond-measure admirable Celestial theater with the necessary subtlety and accuracy, various craftsmen in every age devised means and instruments <note>original: "media & organa"</note> by which sight might be aided in perceiving the hidden motions of the stars. Hence are those pillars which Josephus, the writer of Jewish history, records that the grandsons of Adam <note>Josephus attributes these pillars to the children of Seth, who sought to preserve astronomical knowledge from fire and flood</note> built in Syria, and inscribed upon them their discoveries for the sake of memory for posterity. To this also belong the very high and most costly Pyramids of the Egyptians and other nations, and many other machines constructed for this use by ancient Kings, such as were once seen in India, Syria, Arabia, Chaldea, Ethiopia, and Egypt—especially there in the portico of Alexandria—as well as elsewhere in the surrounding regions where men lived who were zealously intent on the science of the stars. 

For this is the very first requirement: that in matters of Astronomy, very many long-term observations be taken from the heavens with suitable instruments <term>Organis: In this context, "organs" refers to physical tools or scientific instruments</term> that are not subject to error. These observations are afterwards arranged through Geometry—by devising convenient Hypotheses—into continuous quantities and circular, uniform motion (which celestial bodies naturally and without intermission seek and perform); and through Arithmetic into discrete quantities, so that the circuits and locations of the celestial bodies may be established for any given time. 

Among all those who have labored strenuously in this matter, there have reached us at least the records of those things noted down by Timocharis, Hipparchus, Ptolemy, Albategnius <note>The Latinized name for the 9th-century Arab astronomer Al-Battani</note>, King Alfonso, and in the previous age, Copernicus—although the traditions of the first two in these matters are known only from some reports by Ptolemy. Which instruments these men chiefly used in measuring the phenomena of the stars is clear enough from their writings. Among these I find three principal types: Parallactic Rulers, Zodiacal Armillary Spheres, and the Torquetum <term>Torquetum: A complex medieval astronomical instrument used to take measurements in three different sets of coordinates</term>, which was more in use by the Arabs (as were flat Astrolabes); other tools are of less importance. 

Yet many other things perhaps existed which, not being committed to letters, have not reached us; in such great and frequent confusion and change of the world’s stage, with so many wars and devastations creeping in from time to time, they could easily (which is to be lamented) have perished. More recent scholars have added the Quadrant, the Cross-staff <note>original: "Radium," literally "the rod," referring to the Jacob's Staff used for measuring angles</note>, and the Astronomical Ring, as well as some others of even less esteem. However, since the motions of the stars, accurately considered in our age, by no means present themselves as the calculation derived from the observations of any craftsmen—whether ancient or recent—would suggest, it is not without merit
<sig>):( (</sig>
<meta>catchword: suspicion [suspicio]</meta>

<summary>In this preface, Tycho Brahe traces the divine and ancient origins of astronomy from Adam to the present, emphasizing that the human eye requires precise physical instruments to understand the complex motions of the heavens. He catalogs historical observers and the specific tools they used, while arguing that current astronomical tables are no longer accurate.</summary>

<keywords>Rudolf II, history of astronomy, astronomical instruments, Adam, Josephus, Ptolemy, Copernicus, observation, geometry, arithmetic</keywords>

--- Page 8 ---
<meta>This page continues Tycho Brahe’s preface. Having established the divine origin of astronomy on the previous page, he now begins to critique the technical limitations of his predecessors and explain his own motivation for building a new generation of high-precision instruments.</meta>

<header>PREFACE</header>
D5
<meta>catchword: majoribus</meta>

# PREFACE

...leads to the suspicion that the means and instruments they used during their observations were not entirely free of every defect. Even if other things are passed over, this is sufficiently proved by the positions of the fixed stars themselves, which are arranged differently in relation to one another than is indicated by the *Hipparchan* list <note>Referring to the star catalog of Hipparchus (c. 190–120 BCE), the first known comprehensive star map in the West</note> reviewed by *Ptolemy* and passed down to us. This is true even for the ancients, where Emperors and Kings liberally supplied the funds for making such instruments so they could be magnificently crafted from solid metal and of a suitable, proper size.

It is even more true of those instruments which *Copernicus* provided at his own private expense in the previous age. These were sufficient neither in the solidity of their material nor in their size for the purposes they were intended for, as is clear partly from his work *On the Revolutions* <note>Copernicus's "De revolutionibus orbium coelestium," published in 1543</note>, and partly from those *Instruments* of his that still survive. One of these, a *Parallactic instrument* <term>also known as a Triquetrum, used for measuring the zenith distance of stars</term> made entirely of wood and not very well-suited for observations, is in my possession.

Therefore, thinking very anxiously about this since my youth—and whether this problem could be corrected—I subsequently saw to it that various *Astronomical Instruments* were constructed with incredible diligence and expense. With these, it would be possible (if I may say so without appearing boastful) to examine the appearances of the *Stars* more exactly than was practiced by our *Predecessors*. Although the instruments we made in our youth—when neither my knowledge nor my judgment were mature enough—did not reach the intended goal very accurately, I eventually invented other larger and more excellent tools as I grew older and gained more experience. I had these *Instruments* expertly crafted, the likes of which perhaps hardly any previous age has seen, and which perform their intended task with the highest precision and certainty.

I built many and varied types for obvious reasons. First, so that by investigating the same thing in different ways, I could prove the observation to be free of all error. Furthermore, if any of them should contract some hidden flaw (about which *Ptolemy* also seems to complain regarding certain instruments made of solid metal that once stood in the portico at Alexandria), others would be ready at hand to correct it and prove the exact certainty. Moreover, I did this so that the sharp-sightedness in aiming and the diligence in counting of the *Observers* (of whom there should be at least six or eight) might be compared and examined against one another, without any of them knowing what another has found. Not to mention that the variety itself makes the observers more diligent and eager, and is a delight in itself.

To this is added the fact that among the things to be observed, some are better investigated by certain machines than others. Some require only known *Altitudes* <term>the height of a star above the horizon</term>, some require these along with *Azimuths* <term>the horizontal direction or bearing</term>, some require only *distances*, some *Declinations* <term>the distance of a star north or south of the celestial equator</term>, and some *Equatorial Distances*. Others are suitable for obtaining longitudes and latitudes, and still others for obtaining *Right Ascensions* <term>the distance of a star measured eastward along the celestial equator</term> and *Declinations* simultaneously, and so on for the many other things that serve *Celestial Observation* in whatever way it is conducted. For this reason alone, a large number and variety of *Instruments* is absolutely necessary.

Since, however, those things which are thought out and crafted by men for the use of the Arts ought to be shared—for the sake of the kind fellowship of the human race and the passing on of the Arts to posterity—I did not think these sublime and useful inventions should be reserved for myself alone. They should be shared with others, if any can be found anywhere (as I hope they can) who are touched by such arduous care. Therefore, I wished to put to the press and publish the instruments I have used until now, drawn and represented with their own images as quickly as could be managed, with a brief explanation of each added, as will appear in the following pages.

In arranging them, I have followed this method: first, I have placed those that investigate the *Altitudes* and *Azimuths* of the stars, some of which reveal only *Altitudes* and others both. Here also, the smaller and less important ones come first, followed by the more grand ones that provide exact precision. These make up the first eight instruments. Finally, I will show and record a certain very large *Quadrant* <term>a 90-degree arc for measuring angles</term> intended only for *Altitudes*, which I used in my youth at Augsburg <note>original: "Augustae Vindelicorum"</note>. The reason for doing this will be stated later.

Next follow the *Armillary Instruments* <term>models of the celestial sphere made of metal rings</term>. Among these, the first has *Zodiacal Armillaries*, showing the longitudes and latitudes of the stars with greater speed and certainty than was perhaps practiced by the ancients. But since these do not "touch the matter with a needle" <note>An idiom meaning "to be perfectly precise"</note> for certain reasons explained in that place, three other *Equinoctial Armillaries* follow. The first of these consists of three circles and an axis; the second requires four along with the axis. The third and largest, containing a circle and a half and a smooth axis, manages the whole matter; it reveals both *Declinations* and *Right Ascensions*, as well as the distance from the Meridian, with the utmost subtlety. In the third place, I have arranged those instruments which serve for measuring the gaps between the stars...

<summary>Tycho Brahe explains the necessity of building his own high-precision astronomical instruments, critiquing the wooden tools used by Copernicus and the inaccuracies of ancient catalogs. He details his collaborative methodology, which uses multiple observers and various specialized instruments to ensure absolute accuracy through cross-verification.</summary>
<keywords>Tycho Brahe, Copernicus, Ptolemy, Hipparchus, astronomical instruments, precision, quadrant, armillary sphere, altitude, azimuth, declination, right ascension, Augsburg, methodology</keywords>

--- Page 9 ---
<meta>This page continues Tycho Brahe’s preface to the Emperor. He transitions from discussing the shortcomings of previous instruments to detailing the specific devices he has developed at Uraniborg and elsewhere.</meta>

<header>TO THE EMPEROR.</header>
<meta>catchword: larger</meta>

...larger than smaller distances, provided they do not exceed a sixth part of a circle <note>60 degrees</note>, to be obtained. Of those instruments used for measuring distances, there are four in number, although I have more ready at hand which could not be depicted and engraved so quickly. Among these is one which produces the distances of the stars much greater than a sixth part of a circle, even up to a full semicircle <note>180 degrees</note>. Finally, a certain <term>Instrument</term> <note>likely his "Sextans Astronomicus," a portable sextant</note>, which I used for the new and beyond measure wonderful Star of the year 1572 <note>The famous supernova in Cassiopeia which proved the heavens could change</note>, specifically for denoting its altitudes in a lower position, is placed here outside the previous order.

Likewise, I have included that great <term>Quadrant</term> which I mentioned before. We saw to its construction many years ago near <term>Augsburg</term> <note>original: "Augustam Vindelicorum"</note> in the garden of the Consul of excellent memory, <term>Paul Hainzel</term>, who was most eager for these arts while he lived. Since it no longer exists there, and only lasted for five years, I did not wish to grant it a place among the other instruments for searching out altitudes, but instead placed it at the end for that reason. After this, I have included a certain steel Quadrant, which I discussed in previous sections, though it is arranged differently there. 

Once these are finished, a brief description of my other instruments follows—those which I have ready but have not yet been illustrated or explained. There, I also add some indication of those things which I still intend to work on with miraculous brevity. Regarding each of these, both those ready at hand and others to be worked on in due time as opportunity offers, I shall provide a fuller declaration. For now, striving for brevity, and since the icons <note>illustrations</note> of them were not ready, I wished to touch upon at least a few things concerning this business. 

Lastly, as a crowning glory, we describe the formation of that greatest brass <term>Globe</term>, which encompasses one thousand Fixed Stars, restored by us with the highest diligence and precision over many years past, insofar as such a laborious thing, serving many uses, can be elucidated in a few words. And thus we put an end to the description of the instruments. 

To all these things we have annexed some commemoration of those tasks we have endured so far and those that remain to be completed in the future. We have also added a few letters from Famous Men commending the business we are undertaking. Furthermore, in place of an appendix, it seemed good to add the represented and explained <term>Architectural</term> works, which reveal to the eyes the structures ordered and completed by us for these uses some years ago. Although this might not seem strictly necessary to some—since those who wish to apply serious care to these things and are equal to the expenses will easily find for themselves the plans for buildings suitable for this purpose, especially by seizing the opportunity of the location—nevertheless, to show some idea according to what we have continually worked on regarding these matters, I judged it not entirely beside the point to join these Astronomical buildings together in representation. And these are nearly the things with which this book is completed, as will be evident to anyone looking inside. If any other things are mixed in or added, the prudent reader will discern them for himself without difficulty, so that he has no need of me as a monitor.

### TO YOU indeed, Most August Emperor, RUDOLF THE SECOND, Most Clement Lord,

I offer, dedicate, and consecrate this and our other Astronomical labors with a humble spirit and an intention studious of the public good. For just as Your Imperial Majesty, by the highest preeminence, far excels other inferior men, so you also know it to be the duty and dignity of <term>Your Imperial Majesty</term> <note>original: "Cæs. T. M." or Caesarea Tua Maiestas</note> to emulate the Heaven and Heavenly things, which immensely surpass earthly and common things. Therefore, these sublime studies ought to be held in high esteem and fostered. That ancient poet <term>Manilius</term> <note>A 1st-century Roman poet who wrote "Astronomica"</note> seems to have looked toward this when, writing about this Heavenly art to the second monarch of the Roman Empire, Augustus, he adorned it not undeservedly with this eulogy:

> ->It first deigned to move royal minds,<-
> ->Those touching the heights of things nearest to Heaven.<-
> <note>original: "Regales animos primùm dignata movere est / Proxima tangentes Cælo fastigia rerum." from Manilius, Astronomica 1.7-8</note>

### TO YOU therefore, Most August Emperor,

I trust that these things—which we have endured for many years, almost since childhood, with untiring labor and incredible expense, so that the divine and most Regal science of Astronomy, which holds the primary place above all others, might finally be restored to its integrity and handed down to posterity more corrected than ever before—will be graciously accepted. I trust that I and these noble studies I handle will be most highly commended to you in the future, so that the honor and Majesty of **GOD, the Best and Greatest**, which shines in Heavenly things above all other visible things of this most ample theater of the World, may be more rightly known, and thus more greatly amplified and looked up to by the inhabitants of the Earth. Furthermore, may the memory and fame of Your Imperial Majesty—who [supports] these things so excellent, almost the first in worldly affairs—[be preserved]...

<summary>Tycho Brahe describes his collection of instruments, including the Great Globe and his architectural plans for Uraniborg, before formally dedicating his life's work to Emperor Rudolf II.</summary>

--- Page 10 ---
<meta>This page concludes Tycho Brahe’s preface to Emperor Rudolph II. Following his technical description of astronomical instruments, Tycho offers a philosophical justification for his work and a formal dedication for the New Year.</meta>

# PREFACE TO THE EMPEROR.

<note>original: "principua"</note> ...chief things, and may he not be burdened to preserve, protect, and promote them, so that his fame may shine more illustriously and endure for all Posterity, as long as the Sun and Stars shall last. For from these leaves <note>referring to the pages of this book</note>, which are perpetual and constant—just like the Heavens themselves—it is granted to acquire a perpetual and constant name and glory. All other <term>Sublunary</term> <note>literally "below the moon"; in the science of the time, this referred to the Earthly realm where everything decays, unlike the eternal heavens</note> and earthly things are subject to manifold changes; likewise, whatever glory is gained from them is fleeting, wandering, and inconstant. Such earthly fame is no rival for eternity, unlike these Celestial matters of which I speak.

May that supreme and incomprehensible Author <note>God</note> of these heavens deign to grant to Your Imperial Majesty a sound mind, bodily health, peace, and an abundance of the goods of this World, along with a tranquil and happy reign. May this be especially for His own glory, for the illustration of the things He has created, and for the benefit of your subjects—which I humbly and from the heart pray and vow. May Your Imperial Majesty fare well, and graciously accept this little New Year’s gift <note>original: "strenulam"</note>, which I present with as much strength and submissive spirit as I am able, and with all possible reverence. 

Given from the **Rantzau Castle** <note>The home of Tycho’s friend and patron, Henrik Rantzau</note> at **Wandsbek**, which is near **Hamburg** on the borders of **Germany** and the **Cimbrian Peninsula** <note>original: "Cimbricæ Chersonesi"; modern-day Jutland, Denmark</note>, on the day before the Kalends of January <note>December 31st</note>, in the YEAR of the Common Christian Epoch:

->1598<-
<note>original: "CIↃ. IↃ. IIC."</note>

<summary>Tycho Brahe concludes his dedication to Emperor Rudolph II, arguing that astronomical study offers a more lasting glory than earthly pursuits and offering the book as a New Year's gift from his temporary exile in Wandsbek.</summary>
<keywords>dedication, Rudolph II, sublunary vs celestial, Wandsbek, Rantzau, 1598, New Year's gift, astronomical glory</keywords>

--- Page 11 ---
<meta>This page transitions from Tycho Brahe’s own prose preface to a celebratory Latin poem written in his honor. The poem serves as a defense of Tycho’s scientific endeavors against his critics and intellectual rivals, positioning his astronomical work as a divine pursuit. The catchword "QVA" at the bottom of the previous page would have led into the first word of the poem's fifth line ("Circum qua...").</meta>

### TO THE
### NOBLE MAN
# TYCHO BRAHE
## OF KNUDSTRUP, HIS MOST DEAR KINSMAN AND 
### RELATIVE-BY-MARRIAGE;

### AGAINST THE
### <term>ZOILUSES</term>,
### THE MALEVOLENT, AND THE IGNORANT.
<note>Zoilus was a Greek grammarian known for his bitter critiques of Homer; his name became a standard term for a spiteful or hyper-critical person.</note>

*<term>Urania</term> <note>The Muse of Astronomy</note> allows you, **TYCHO**, to know the hidden recesses (though they are neglected by others) where the great Gods of the high Ether dwell, and the unknown paths of Heaven and the eternal motions. Opening those sacred things to you, around which she sees the high axes of the world revolve, she does not pause for the ignorant and envious crowd. That crowd receives your work with sneers; they mock and harass what they do not yet understand in their dull-witted stupor. They plot deceits and tricks; they build unjust schemes and hurl unspeakable weapons—though these weapons shatter against a solid shield—hoping they might perhaps disturb your illustrious labors. It is enough that virtue is always sufficient for itself in all things, and it does not dwell on the vain trifles and manners of men. Behold, now (marvelous to say) you produce instruments <note>original: "organa"</note> conforming to Divine mysteries, by which you, through your own genius, are able to subject the high Heaven to yourself. Begone, you profane ones! <note>A classic warning used in ancient rituals to keep the uninitiated away from sacred mysteries.</note> These works belong to virtue; they are rivals to the face of the <term>Gorgon</term> <note>In Greek mythology, the gaze of the Gorgon Medusa turned onlookers to stone; here, it suggests Tycho's work is so powerful it freezes his critics in their tracks.</note>—they are even greater. For they do not care for those things that are bright, nor do envy and hatred—conscious of their own evil—care for those whom they provoke. At last, shaken by the very sight of these works, the critics stand amazed, silent, and rigid like stones. Indeed, who would deny that the ignorant—to whom these divinely inspired <note>original: "Enthea"</note> things are so base—are merely logs, or beasts in a human body? But you, **TYCHO**, bring yourself into the deepest sanctuaries of the gods. As you are doing, laugh from on high at the ravings of the filthy world and its lowly clamor; laugh at the hearts that know nothing of the fate the Gods dispense by a just path. Standing on safe ground, despise the waves, the rains, and the blasts of wind.*

->HOLGER ROSENKRANTZ<-
->Son of G. F. of Rosenholm, he wrote this.<-
<note>Holger Rosenkrantz (1574–1642) was a prominent Danish nobleman, scholar, and theologian. He was a close friend and cousin of Tycho Brahe.</note>

<summary>A laudatory poem by Holger Rosenkrantz defending Tycho Brahe’s astronomical work against envious critics and comparing his scientific instruments to divine mysteries.</summary>
<keywords>Tycho Brahe, Holger Rosenkrantz, Urania, astronomy, Zoilus, scientific instruments, Danish nobility, Latin poetry</keywords>

### TO THE
### NOBLE MAN
# TYCHO BRAHE
## OF KNUDSTRUP, HIS MOST DEAR KINSMAN AND 
### RELATIVE-BY-MARRIAGE;

### AGAINST THE
### <term>ZOILUSES</term>,
### THE MALEVOLENT, AND THE IGNORANT.
<note>Zoilus was a 4th-century BCE Greek grammarian known for his bitter critiques of Homer; his name became a standard term for a spiteful, hyper-critical, or envious person.</note>

*<term>Urania</term> <note>The Muse of Astronomy in Greek mythology.</note> allows you, **TYCHO**, to know the hidden recesses (though they are neglected by others) where the great Gods of the high Ether dwell, and the unknown paths of Heaven and the eternal motions. Opening those sacred things to you, around which she sees the high axes of the world revolve, she does not pause for the ignorant and envious crowd. That crowd receives your work with sneers; they mock and harass what they do not yet understand in their dull-witted stupor. They plot deceits and tricks; they build unjust schemes and hurl unspeakable weapons—though these weapons shatter against a solid shield—hoping they might perhaps disturb your illustrious labors. It is enough that virtue is always sufficient for itself in all things, and it does not dwell on the vain trifles and manners of men. Behold, now (marvelous to say) you produce instruments <note>original: "organa"</note> conforming to Divine mysteries, by which you, through your own genius, are able to subject the high Heaven to yourself. Begone, you profane ones! <note>original: "Procul hinc abeste profani!" — a ritual formula used in ancient mysteries to warn the uninitiated to stay away.</note> These works belong to virtue; they are rightfully yours.*
<note>The fragment "ri..." likely translates to "rightfully" or "richly," corresponding to the Latin "propria," meaning things belonging exclusively to one's character or status.</note>

<header>EXPLANATION OF THE CONSTRUCTION AND USE.</header>
A
<meta>Catchword: riorem,</meta>

# EXPLANATION OF THE CONSTRUCTION AND USE.

THE QUADRANT, which is not so very large, was constructed by us many years ago in the form shown here. It consists of a solid plate of rather thick <term>Orichalcum</term> <note>A golden-colored brass alloy favored for precision instruments.</note>, having a length of one cubit from the Center A to the circumference B D C. Every part of it is gilded by means of gold, mercury, and fire, so that it remains more beautiful and clean, and is not subject to the stains that brass otherwise gradually contracts unless it is frequently cleaned. Its outer edge B D C is subdivided, according to the common method, into 90 parts <note>degrees</note>, and each of these again into 12, so that this subdivision shows intervals of five minutes each. Within this is another distribution which <term>Petrus Nonnius</term> <note>Pedro Nunes (1502–1578), a famous Portuguese mathematician who invented a system for taking fine measurements, a precursor to the modern vernier scale.</note>, the most celebrated Spanish Mathematician, handed down in his learned book *On Crepuscules*, believing it to have been used by Ptolemy in ancient times. This, however, is hardly worthy of belief. The system is such that 44 other smaller quadrants are drawn within the outermost one, following one after another: the outermost of these is to be divided into 89 parts; the next into 88, the third into 87, and so on, until the last and innermost is reached, which will have 46 portions. These are represented in some fashion in the illustration itself. Its Rule D A <note>The alidade or sighting arm.</note> has <term>pinnacidia</term> <note>sights</note> at A and D prepared in the usual way with holes; though sometimes, at the lower sight at A, parallel slits can be applied to the upper one in our own fashion, so that the alignment <note>collimation</note> becomes easier and more exact. The Plumb line E F G is hung from the back, so that by its aid one may know when the Quadrant is properly positioned, such that its lower side A B exactly corresponds to the equilibrium of the Horizon, and the other side A C to the zenith <note>the point directly overhead</note>; and the entire plane of the Quadrant coincides with the plane of some vertical circle. To ensure the Quadrant is thus correctly directed, it is adjusted partly by screws (marked with the letters S) located near the lower arrangement of the Pedestal, and partly by the screw at O M, which raises and lowers the Quadrant itself until the plumb line shows it is suitably ordered. For it stands upon a pedestal N M O P Q R S T, which, along with the other requirements, is made of pure iron, where it is attached from the back near H. This support is somewhat movable near N, so that the Quadrant can be pushed up and down by the screw O M. For the iron bars M N and O N can be tightened and widened by the mediation of that same screw O M; and thus the quadrant can be raised or lowered a little at one time as needed. It is also rotated in a concave channel N P toward whatever quarter of the Sky one pleases; however, the entire lower part of the pedestal, marked by the letters R S Q T, remains firm. This lower portion provides supports in the shape you see, so that the Quadrant stands firmly on its four screws indicated by the letters S T: these screws (as I said) direct the whole instrument into the required position. These screws rest upon iron plates laid underneath near T; while these remain stationary on a table V X Y Z, the screws are turned at will. And let these things be generally indicated concerning the Quadrant and its pedestal.

The picture L K, which is seen to some extent within the Center and the smallest Quadrant, enclosed in a circular form, was engraved for the sake of decoration and also to teach something by its <term>Hieroglyphic</term> <note>symbolic or allegorical</note> meaning, since otherwise that space would be empty. In this illustration (to mention this in passing), next to a tree that is green and leafy on one side, a Youth distinguished by a laurel wreath sits on a square stone; he holds a celestial globe in one hand and a book in the other, extending his feet upon the green grass and herbs covering the root of the tree. From the other, namely the left side of the Tree, the root is withered, and the branches are sterile and lacking leaves. Among these stands a table, having something of every iron thing <note>referring to base or worldly materials</note> that are held in value by mortals in this earthly scene, such as: a small chest filled with coins, Scepters, Crowns, family Coats of Arms, golden Necklaces, Gems, women’s finery, Cups, Papers, Dice, and similar things. A skeleton, representing Death, surrounds all these with its hands and feet, as if snatching them to itself. Over the whole figure is written a <term>hemistich</term> <note>half-line of verse</note> which explains the Allegorical sense: **WE LIVE BY THE SPIRIT, THE REST WILL BELONG TO DEATH** <note>original Latin: *VIVIMUS INGENIO COETERA MORTIS ERUNT*</note>. The first part of the half-line hangs from the branches as if written on a sheet on the right and green side of the tree where that philosophizing youth sits; then follows the remaining portion of the half-line. By these things we wished to suggest that solid learning, and especially the sublime knowledge of Celestial things, grants a perpetuity of life and memory in these lands; all other things are frivolous and fleeting, and perish together with the body of man. But when we look toward the eternity of the Kingdom of Heaven, then these very things which are now on the green side of the tree pass to the rear; for there is no proportion between the finite and the infinite. And then the inscribed half-line is changed differently, namely thus: **WE LIVE IN CHRIST, THE REST WILL BELONG TO DEATH** <note>original Latin: *VIVIMUS IN CHRISTO, COETERA MORTIS ERUNT*</note>; so that the first part is adapted to the green tree, the second to the sterile one. Within the green aspect of the tree is a certain symbolic indication of the life and teaching of Christ. The rest remains as before, except that human teachings and inventions also [pass] into the left and rear...

<note>Vocabulary: Quadrant, Pedro Nunes, Brass, Ptolemy, Pedestal, Half-line of verse, Symbols, Death, Life, Spirit/Genius, Christ</note>

<summary>This page describes the technical construction and scaling of Tycho Brahe's gilded brass quadrant, specifically highlighting the "Nonius" division system and the instrument's adjustable iron pedestal. It also explains the allegorical artwork inside the quadrant's frame, which contrasts the immortality of scientific and spiritual pursuits with the vanity of worldly possessions.</summary>
<keywords>Quadrant, Pedro Nunes, brass instruments, astronomical measurement, Tycho Brahe, allegory of death, scientific symbolism, Renaissance technology</keywords>

# EXPLANATION OF THE CONSTRUCTION AND USE.

...to the inner <note>original: *riorem*</note> part of the tree, which is indeed the barren part. By these we suggest that nothing can make a person blessed and grant eternal immortality except the merit of the Son of God, Christ the Redeemer, and the observance of His teaching and life. I have chosen to explain each of these points here—though perhaps they are beside the point—because of the accompanying picture. On the other side of the Quadrant, a table <note>original: *Canon*</note> is engraved with digits, which shows with very little effort what each point of the Nonnian division (of which I spoke) represents in the sought-for altitudes.

The use of this Quadrant is for taking the altitudes of the stars and the <term>Luminaries</term> <note>The Sun and the Moon.</note> when the highest precision is not required, such that knowing the measurement within two or three minutes is sufficient. Therefore, the plane of the Quadrant should be directed toward the star to be observed and properly adjusted by its screws, as said before, until the <term>plumb line</term> <note>Latin: *perpendiculum*; a weight hanging from a cord used to ensure the instrument is perfectly vertical.</note> shows that everything is correctly aligned. 

Then the <term>Alidade</term> <note>Latin: *Regula*; the pivoting sighting arm of the instrument.</note> marked A D is raised or lowered until the star is seen through the holes or slits of the <term>sights</term> <note>Latin: *dioptrarum*; the viewing apertures on the alidade.</note>, or the light of the Sun is admitted precisely. It is then fixed on the other side near D, where it has a small hidden screw. In this way, on the outer rim where the common divisions are, the end of the Alidade will show the sought-for altitude within five minutes; half or a third of which <note>i.e., 2 or 1.5 minutes</note> can also be determined by a careful gaze. 

But if you wish to use the <term>Nonnian division</term> <note>A system of 45 concentric arcs used to read fractions of a degree, named after Pedro Nunes.</note> at the same time, you must see where the Alidade passes through any point in it, wherever it may be; and count which point it is, and how many such points that specific arc contains. With these two numbers, one must enter the table engraved on the other side of the Quadrant laterally, and thus in the area at the common intersection, the Degrees and Minutes will be found, revealing the measured altitude. 

The same can be achieved without the table by the <term>Rule of Proportions</term> <note>Also known as the "Rule of Three," a mathematical method for finding a fourth value from three known ones.</note> with little effort: by placing in the first position the total division of that arc in which the Alidade touches a point. In the second position, 90 degrees. In the third, the number of that same point from the beginning of the arc to where the Alidade rests. Thus, after the usual operation is performed, the fourth value will show what is sought. However, this Nonnian method is not sufficient in practice; nor does it provide in reality what it promises at the outset, as will be clear to anyone who tries it. <note>The author, likely Tycho Brahe, was famously critical of the Nonnian system's practical accuracy compared to his own transversals (diagonal scales).</note>

<summary>This page explains how to use the small quadrant to measure the altitude of stars, either by reading the standard degree scale or by using the complex Nonnian system of concentric arcs and a lookup table. The author concludes by noting that while the Nonnian system is mathematically clever, it is difficult to use accurately in practice.</summary>

<keywords>quadrant, altitude, Pedro Nunes, Nonnian division, alidade, sights, astronomy, rule of proportions, measurement precision</keywords>

# THE MEDIUM
## BRASS AZIMUTHAL QUADRANT

<note>original: *Quadrans Mediocris Orichalcicus Azimuthalis*. The word 'Mediocris' (noted in the OCR as the typo 'Mediocsis') indicates that this instrument was of a moderate or medium size compared to the larger mural instruments at Tycho Brahe's observatory. The term 'Orichalcicus' refers to <term>orichalcum</term>, a high-quality brass alloy used for its durability and golden appearance.</note>

<note>This page is dominated by a detailed woodcut of the instrument. It shows a 90-degree quadrant arc made of brass, mounted on a vertical axis that allowed it to rotate horizontally. This rotation allowed astronomers to measure the <term>azimuth</term> (the horizontal angle) as well as the <term>altitude</term> (the vertical angle) of stars. The instrument is supported by artistic, entwined serpent-like brackets and stands on a masonry base.</note>

<note>The labels D through Z mentioned in the accompanying technical descriptions correspond to the various parts of the frame, the sighting alidade, the graduated scale, and the leveling screws shown in the illustration.</note>

<summary>This page introduces the Medium Brass Azimuthal Quadrant, a key instrument in Tycho Brahe's observatory designed for measuring both the altitude and horizontal direction of celestial bodies.</summary>
<keywords>Tycho Brahe, quadrant, brass, azimuth, astronomical instruments, orichalcum, scientific measurement</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the detailed description of the Medium Brass Azimuthal Quadrant. This text provides the technical specifications of the instrument's scales, sights, and mounting.</meta>

<margin>A 2</margin>

# EXPLANATION OF THE CONSTRUCTION AND USE.

This Quadrant **A B C**, consisting of a solid plate of brass <note>original: *Orichalcica*. <term>Orichalcum</term> was a golden-colored brass alloy highly prized for scientific instruments due to its hardness.</note>, has a radius from the center to the circumference **A B** of slightly more than one and a half cubits <note>A <term>cubit</term> in Tycho's region was roughly 18-24 inches; thus the radius was about 3 feet.</note>. Its thickness is nearly half a finger's breadth. Within its circumference, it contains not only our usual division by lines and transversal points <note>The transversal system used diagonal dots to allow for more precise readings of fractions of a degree.</note> (which is discussed elsewhere), but also that "Nonnian" scale <note>Named after the Portuguese mathematician Pedro Nuñez. The <term>Nonnian scale</term> used 45 concentric circles to divide degrees, a precursor to the modern vernier scale.</note> produced by the most distinguished Spanish mathematician in his learned book *On Crepuscules* <note>original: *de Crepusculis*</note>. I mentioned this in a previous work, though here it is expanded by a different and more convenient method so that it contains more subdivisions, especially of those numbers that can be easily distributed into several whole parts. In this regard, the Nonnian invention used to present no small difficulty, since by itself it is not sufficient for all the minutes of the whole Quadrant, much less to reveal the "fruitful seconds" <note>Tycho is skeptical of Nuñez's claim that his scale could accurately measure down to the second of an arc in practical use.</note> as he intended. For that has a place in calculation rather than in practice; as often happens, beginners in this art are frequently deceived in this way. A Table is also added, engraved on the other surface of the Quadrant, which illustrates the divisions and makes them very easy to use without further calculation. Its ruler <note>The <term>alidade</term> or sighting arm.</note> **D E** is made entirely of steel so that it may always remain straight more readily. At the same time, it is gilded so that it is not infected by rust. It has two brass sights <note>original: *dioptra*</note> around **D E**, where the lower one **E** shows small slits parallel to the first sight-vane at **D**, to which the eye of the observer is applied. By this, one may aim at the stars everywhere around the first sight-vane more accurately and centrally without any "hallucination" <note>In this context, Tycho uses "hallucination" to mean optical error or parallax caused by poor alignment.</note>. This method is used by us in nearly all our instruments. The ruler itself was purposely made longer than the radius of the Quadrant at **A E**; partly so that near the lower end at **E** it can be easily raised and lowered by the fingers during the act of sighting until the exact altitude of the star to be observed is clear, along with its azimuth. It also remains firm wherever it is moved, because of a thin steel "feather" <note>A tension spring or clip.</note> (as they call it) on the other side of the Quadrant near **D** that holds and observes it, so that the observation becomes more exact and convenient. Indeed, it results in the same effect as if the Quadrant had a radius as long as the ruler **D E**, and furthermore, the handling is more suitable because the lower supports do not get in the way. Let these things be sufficiently explained regarding the Quadrant itself.

The supports, however, of which I speak, work as follows: first, the Quadrant itself turns, attached to a certain smooth, oblong, robust iron piece, around **H** by means of a small screw. Thus, it can be removed whenever desired from a certain channel which has those visible rotations on the outside for the sake of stability and ornament. The channel itself carries another ruler below, represented near **K** and **M**; and together with the plane of the Quadrant, it is conveniently rotated toward any face of the sky above the steel Azimuthal Horizon **N O P**. Simultaneously, under the index **K**, it reveals the azimuth of the observed star along with its altitude in the same operation, so that the altitudes are to be counted above on the Quadrant (as demonstrated), while the azimuths are counted on the said Azimuthal Circle. The three screws seen around the mentioned letters, as well as a fourth hidden on the other side, ensure that the steel horizon can be positioned exactly in the plane of the true horizon. Consequently, the Quadrant existing above it can be properly directed both according to the equilibrium of the horizon and toward the zenith on both its sides. There is also another screw near **I** which corrects it in any very small differences (wherever necessary), so that it represents the plane of any vertical circle exactly in whatever position it may be. Furthermore, these steel screws supporting the whole instrument rest on four marble columns, three of which are visible and indicated by letters **X Y Z**. The fourth, identical to them at the back, is not clearly visible. Finally, within these four is contained a bench or pedestal marked by **Q R S**; one climbs and stands upon this after the observation is prepared, so that the altitude observed on the circumference of the Quadrant can be counted conveniently and accurately. Let it suffice to have indicated these things about the construction of the instrument. Other minor details, if any are desired, are easily known from a visual inspection of the figure itself and diligent attention.

Its use is very wide-ranging, extending wherever there is anything concerning the altitudes and azimuths of both planets and fixed stars that can be adapted to astronomical observations and deduced into numbers through the science of triangles. On this matter, the books of experts should be consulted, as well as those things which we have commented upon here and there in our own works as the subject required; especially in the second volume of my *Astronomical Exercises* <note>original: *Progymnasmatum Astronomicorum*. This was Tycho's massive work detailing his observations and solar theory.</note>, which treats brilliantly of the Comet of the year [15]77. For in the observations of it set forth there and adapted for demonstration, the primary use of this Quadrant stood out, since at that time I did not have at hand the other larger ones or those of a different form manufactured later. Nevertheless, it was sufficient here, since it shows individual minutes quite discretely in both altitudes and azimuths. It is also explained in the said book near the end, specifically on page 461.

***

# EXPLANATION OF THE CONSTRUCTION AND USE.

And on the three following pages, this same Quadrant is explained a bit more fully through various details; and likewise, in that same place, the most suitable and exact method of dividing the circumference by transversal points <note>The <term>transversal point</term> method used a grid of dots to allow the observer to read fractions of a degree with much higher precision than simple lines allowed.</note>—which we were the first to apply to circular instruments—is more broadly explained and illustrated with an exquisite picture. Moreover, its sight <note>original: *dioptra*</note> is also depicted and described there on page 462, where the arrangement of the sights <note>original: *pinnacidia*; these were the small plates with slits used to align the instrument with a star.</note> and the slots <note>original: *rimularum*</note>—which I mention from time to time—and other relevant matters are clearly shown. Therefore, I refer the reader who is eager for these things to that section; there he will find more concerning the mechanics <note>original: *Mæchanicam*</note> of this instrument as well as the design of others, for space did not allow all these things to be recorded here. And what is presented there regarding the placement of the divisions and the sights, I wish to be understood for all other instruments where these must be applied, so that there is no need to repeat them tediously here or elsewhere. 

This at least I will advise here, both for this and for the other instruments: they must be as perfect as possible in every part. Therefore, suitable mechanical craftsmen <note>original: *Artifices Mæchanicos*</note> should be employed, who know how to labor skillfully on such things or may learn to do so. And if on the first attempt they do not "hit the mark" (as they say) <note>The Latin idiom *rem omnem acu... tetigerint* literally means "to touch the thing with a needle," signifying perfect precision.</note>, the builder should not be reluctant to redo the work and correct everything that is lacking in every way, until they are subject to no defect. This fact observation itself sufficiently reveals to an intelligent person, when conducted in various ways. Hence it came about that we saw to it that most of the instruments we show in this book were renovated twice or three times, and indeed remade almost from scratch, not without great expense; even constructing mills driven by horses as well as by water for the purpose of turning them [on a lathe] <note>Tycho established sophisticated workshops at his observatory, Uraniborg, using water power to drive the machinery needed to shape large metal instruments.</note>, though almost in vain:

-> And with many other expenses spent here, which are not <-
-> easily estimated from the instruments themselves. <-

## QUADRANT, ALSO AZIMUTHAL.

<meta>This page continues the technical description of the azimuthal quadrant, moving from its general introduction on the previous page to specific mechanical details regarding its portability, the "Geometric Square" embedded in its frame, and its mounting system.</meta>

# EXPLANATION OF THE CONSTRUCTION AND USE.

**H**ere <note>The historiated initial 'H' depicts a scholar or surveyor in a landscape, reflecting the scientific nature of the text.</note> also is a **Quadrant A B C**, made of solid brass <note>original: *Orichalco*, a golden-colored copper alloy similar to brass or bronze</note>; but it is arranged as it appears in the illustration, having beneath it a **Horizon G H I K** which contains the azimuths <note>An <term>azimuth</term> is an angular measurement used to determine the horizontal direction of a celestial body.</note>, and is cast from solid brass. The parts of the whole instrument, explained individually, are as follows: from the **Center A** to **B** or **C**, the length is one cubit and a half <note>A cubit and a half is approximately 27 to 30 inches (70–75 cm).</note>. From this, the **circumference B C** is clear, which is also subdivided into individual minutes of degrees by transversal points in our usual manner; it possesses no other method of division, as the previous one did, since the limb <note>The <term>limb</term> is the graduated outer edge of the instrument.</note> is not wide enough to conveniently accommodate more. Nevertheless, it has a width of about two fingers and is capable of expressing individual minutes transversally (as I said). Furthermore, the **Square** seen in the area of the **Quadrant A D E F**, together with the cross-bracing supports that meet it, are adapted for the sake of support and greater firmness—except that this Square can also be of use in geometric measurements regarding the height and width of things seen on the earth which have been built by human labor. Just as that other instrument, which *Peurbach* <note>Georg von Peurbach (1423–1461) was a celebrated Austrian astronomer and mathematician.</note> called a **Geometric Square**, provides these and similar measurements; this was explained and demonstrated in a specific booklet by that same distinguished man. Elsewhere, those who explain the **Astrolabe** indicate this with less effort (though not as exactly), especially regarding what they call its "back."

Another adaptation, seen around these aforementioned parts and understood to be at the rear of the Quadrant, represents the shape of the letter **Y** and is marked with these three letters **V V X Y**. It is present so that the plane of the Quadrant itself stands at a right angle to the Horizon in which it is encased, however much it is rotated; and on that part where it joins the Horizon near **Y**, it is fixed by a certain small screw where necessary, so that, remaining motionless in its place there, it may conveniently show the azimuthal degrees. There is also a certain **Ruler** led from the **Center L** toward the **screw Y** which joins the end of the support with the lower and middle part of the Quadrant, so that all parts may hold together more firmly and be rotated as one. A small tensioning piece <note>original: *plumeolâ*; literally "little feather," referring here to a flexible metal spring or wedge</note> is also added below at the screw so that the Quadrant stands motionless in any place without any wobbling. 

The fact that this Quadrant is not solid over its entire surface, but has those openings—mostly four-sided—which are seen in it, provides the benefit of being lighter in weight. Because of this, it is not only easier to handle but can also be moved from one place to another (for which reason it is made of separate parts), as we will indicate more fully later. Regarding its **ruler** carrying the sights <note>original: *dioptras*; sighting devices used to align the eye with a star.</note>, we shall speak soon after we have first explained some things about the **Azimuthal Horizon**: since those sights serve equally for the observation of both Altitudes and Azimuths. Therefore, the Ruler itself, with its longer and wider plane, lies adjacent to the plane of the Quadrant everywhere, so that its lower part, **A S** where it protrudes, corresponds exactly to the same flat surface. The sights, with their vanes <note>original: *pinnacidiis*; the small plates with slits or holes used for sighting.</note>, are equidistant from this and also stand at right angles; otherwise, an accurate investigation of Azimuths (not to mention Altitudes) could not be made.

The **Azimuthal Horizon** (of which I spoke) existing below the Quadrant and supporting it, is represented by **G H I K**, having supports crossing at right angles so that it remains firmer. Around their common angles—namely, in the center of that same horizontal circle—the Quadrant is rotated entirely in a channel adapted for that purpose. It has, moreover, near the ends of these same frames, four **screws M N O P**, by which the whole horizon (and thus the Quadrant itself standing upon it) is directed so that the latter perfectly mimics the plane of any vertical circle, while the former maintains the equilibrium of the Horizon. Thus, the use of both regarding Altitudes and Azimuths is properly established. 

The **Ruler** (which I mentioned briefly before) applied to the Quadrant itself, **S V T**, functions in the same way regarding its sight and vanes as was indicated in the previous instrument, except that below, near the **sight S**, it has two handles by which it can be conveniently raised and lowered by hand as the altitude to be observed requires. This also ensures that, by its own weight, it keeps the other part of the **Ruler T A** in a sort of equilibrium. Furthermore, the whole instrument stands upon a **square stone Q**, which is somewhat hollowed out on the sides for more convenient use. This stone rests firmly upon a **stone column R**, as the figure itself sufficiently shows. This Quadrant has this advantage in its construction over the preceding one: that both it and the Horizon it stands on, and everything else belonging to it, can be easily taken apart and put back together again. This makes it suitable for carrying from one place to another in a convenient case, especially since it does not consist of as many parts as the previous one. These parts, with screws arranged here and there, allow for not only easy disassembly but also for reassembly in exactly the same way as before. Therefore, I am accustomed to calling this the **Portable Azimuthal Quadrant**.

The author highlights the instrument's precision, its leveling screw system, and its ability to be disassembled for transport.</summary>

# EXPLANATION OF THE CONSTRUCTION AND USE.

Its use is uniform with the previous one in all those matters which are to be observed through Altitudes and Azimuths <note>An <term>azimuth</term> is the horizontal angle of a celestial body from North, while <term>altitude</term> is its height above the horizon.</note>, demonstrated geometrically, and then resolved into numbers. It should be known, nonetheless, that one must not trust these two quadrants too much where a very exact observation in some small part of a minute is required. Such observations are those that serve the restoration of the Solar course <note>original: *Solaris curriculi*; this refers to the precise calculation of the Sun's path and the length of the solar year.</note>, where the matter concerns the smallest details and requires a precision of a sixth, or at least a third part of one minute <note>Tycho is seeking a precision of 10 to 20 seconds of arc, which was extraordinary for the 16th century.</note>; this, such small instruments—which comprise only one or one and a half cubits—cannot provide.

Therefore, let us now turn to larger instruments more suitable for such tasks. These will facilitate not only this subtlety required for the Sun, but also the investigation of very tiny parallaxes <note> <term>Parallax</term> is the apparent shift in a star's position when viewed from different locations; Tycho used it to prove how far away celestial objects were.</note> and differences (if there be any) in any celestial phenomenon; they will also most scrupulously reveal many other things in the observations of both wandering and fixed stars <note> "Wandering stars" refers to the planets.</note>. Those eager for such things will find examples of such parallaxes in the second volume of our *Introduction to Restored Astronomy* <note>original: *Progymnasmatum nostrorum Astronomiæ instaurandæ*, Tycho's massive multi-volume work.</note>. There, in both the first and second parts, I deal diligently with seven Comets seen within the past twenty years, and I scrutinize their parallaxes as minutely as they could be obtained. I demonstrate geometrically and infallibly that they all existed in the Ethereal Region far above the Moon. This is despite the fact that there are those among the followers of Aristotle’s school <note>Aristotle's followers believed comets were "meteors" or atmospheric disturbances occurring below the moon, rather than distant celestial bodies.</note> who are unwilling to acquiesce to the truth, now sufficiently and more than sufficiently revealed (as also usually happens in various other human opinions).

-> nonetheless be shown. Let those believe who recognize <-
-> and wish the truth to be known. <-

# THE ASTRONOMICAL SEXTANT,
## AS IT SERVES FOR ALTITUDES
### [and its application].

# EXPLANATION OF THE CONSTRUCTION AND USE

Its use is uniform with the previous one in all those matters which are to be observed through Altitudes and Azimuths <note>An <term>azimuth</term> is the horizontal angle of a celestial body measured clockwise from the North, while <term>altitude</term> is its vertical height above the horizon.</note>, demonstrated geometrically, and then resolved into numbers. It should be known, nonetheless, that one must not trust these two quadrants too much where a very exact observation in some small part of a minute is required. Such observations are those that serve the restoration of the Solar course <note>original: *Solaris curriculi*; this refers to the precise calculation of the Sun's path and the length of the solar year.</note>, where the matter concerns the smallest details and requires a precision of a sixth, or at least a third part of one minute <note>Tycho is seeking a precision of 10 to 20 seconds of arc, which was extraordinary for the 16th century before the invention of the telescope.</note>; this, such small instruments—which comprise only one or one and a half cubits <note>A <term>cubit</term> is an ancient unit of length based on the forearm; here it indicates the radius of the instrument's arc.</note>—cannot provide.

Therefore, let us now turn to larger instruments more suitable for such tasks. These will facilitate not only this subtlety required for the Sun, but also the investigation of very tiny parallaxes <note><term>Parallax</term> is the apparent shift in a star's position when viewed from different locations; Tycho used it to prove how far away celestial objects were and to show that comets were further away than the Moon.</note> and differences (if there be any) in any celestial phenomenon; they will also most scrupulously reveal many other things in the observations of both wandering and fixed stars <note>In the 16th century, "wandering stars" referred to the planets, which move against the backdrop of the "fixed stars."</note>. Those eager for such things will find examples of such par... <margin>Explanation</margin>

# EXPLANATION OF THE CONSTRUCTION AND USE

I was the first to devise this **Astronomical Sextant**, an instrument perfectly suited for various uses, approximately 20 years ago. I gave it this name because it encompasses a sixth part of a circle's circumference. It is entirely sufficient for the types of observations that are most frequently required, and its size does not make them overly difficult. Although others later claimed this invention for themselves and produced some variations in form—just as some in Germany and elsewhere are not ashamed to claim many of my other inventions as their own—I have, in fact, constructed three such instruments in succession. These (to say nothing of others that achieve the same result in different ways) are identical in their outer rims and <term>pinnacidia</term> <note>These are sighting vanes or "diopters" used to align the instrument with a star or the sun.</note>, but differ in their internal framework.

The present instrument among those I am discussing had the simplest framework, being composed of fewer parts. I built it this way so it could be easily taken apart and put back together in certain places using screws. When placed in a chest along with its base—which can likewise be separated into parts—it can be transported to other regions, not only by sea (which would be safer) but also by land in a carriage. This allows the Altitudes of the Pole <note>In this context, the "Altitude of the Pole" refers to the geographical latitude of the observer's location.</note> to be accurately explored in various lands. Indeed, this instrument is aptly suited not only for measuring the distances between the Sun and the stars but also for investigating their Altitudes. When their heights are to be captured, it must be arranged as the present figure shows. Having first explained its construction, we will briefly indicate its use.

This sextant **A B C** is entirely covered with <term>orichalcum</term> <note>A golden-colored alloy, usually brass, highly prized for scientific instruments.</note> plates, and so neatly that you would say it was cast from solid brass, with no visible seam at the joints. It measures four <term>cubits</term> <note>A cubit is an ancient unit of length; in Tycho's workshop, a cubit was roughly 40 centimeters, making this instrument's radius about 1.6 meters (over 5 feet).</note> from the center to the circumference. Near the center is Cylinder **A**, through which observations are made using the lower sighting vane **M** on the ruler **A N M**. That sighting vane has two small slits, spaced exactly as the diameter of the cylinder requires, so that the observation is made in parallel on either side, and the star is marked centrally without any error.

The division of the circumference **B C** is made through <term>transversal points</term> <note>A method of dividing a scale using diagonal lines and dots to achieve much higher precision than simple degree marks; Tycho was famous for perfecting this.</note>, as is our custom. According to our experience, this method is the most suitable of all, the easiest to execute, and subject to no uncertainty. This method is explained more fully at the end of the aforementioned book on the Comet of 1577 (as I have also previously noted). Others in Germany later attributed this method to themselves after <note>original: "Wittichius"</note> **Paul Wittich** saw it at my residence and revealed it, along with the design of the sighting vanes, at Cassel—and perhaps even sold the idea as his own.

It should be noted that when an observation of the Sun's altitude is made, it is most convenient for the more exact distinction of the shadow to attach a certain long, four-sided "canal" or tube to the ruler near its sights. This tube is made of a lightweight material so that its weight does not interfere with the ruler or its sights. It should leave a small empty space like a slit near Cylinder **A**, both above and below, so that the solar rays passing through on both sides can more clearly show the four-sided shadow of the cylinder on the inner part of the lower sighting vane, within a matching four-sided figure. For this reason, there should be an opening in the tube through which this can be viewed conveniently and distinctly, as we are accustomed to doing in solar observations so that the edges of light and shadow are more readily apparent.

This instrument is assembled and taken apart using certain firm screws at **A B C D L**, as well as in several other places not represented by letters here. However, its Arc and the connecting cord <note>The "subtensa" or chord—the straight bar connecting the two ends of the 60-degree arc.</note> **B C** are not separated; they always remain joined so that the Arc does not lose any of its perfectly refined curvature. Furthermore, it has a pedestal to which it is attached when its altitudes are to be taken (as seen here), represented by **E G H**. It is fixed into a round hole with a small pin near **D**, and its movement is finally secured below at screw **G**. There, a <term>perpendiculum</term> <note>A plumb line or lead weight used to ensure the instrument is perfectly vertical.</note> attached near **L** and hanging down toward **C** near the end of the divisions indicates that the side **A B** is perfectly parallel to the Horizon. Point **L** is the spot where a thin brass wire is to be hung, midway between center **A** and the end of the circumference **B**. Thus, it forms an <term>isosceles triangle</term> **A B C**. It is known from the rudiments of Geometry that the radius of a circle and the chord of a sixth part of the circumference are equal to each other. Since the line **C L** falls perpendicularly upon the line **A B**, and the plumb line is aligned with line **C L** by adjustment, it is necessary that the plumb line points to the **Zenith** and the other line to the plane of the horizon. Thus, the arrangement of the instrument for Altitudes can be made no less exactly here than if we were using a complete Quadrant. Its pedestal or support is made of firm wood in the shape you see. However, it is mostly hollow inside so that it does not hinder transport with excessive weight. Nevertheless, it has its own iron fittings where necessary to protect it from bruising and damage...

<summary>Tycho Brahe describes the construction of his portable Astronomical Sextant, highlighting its brass plating, its four-cubit radius, and the innovative "transversal" method for precise degree markings. He explains how the instrument is mounted on a hollow wooden pedestal and aligned using a plumb line to measure celestial altitudes with the same accuracy as a larger quadrant.</summary>

# EXPLANATION OF THE CONSTRUCTION AND USE.

...[so that it] may be more stable. And additionally, there are three oblong iron supports extending from its center to the extremity of the foot, two of which are indicated by the letters **I** and **H**; the third is hidden in the back. These can also be attached and removed by their own screws. There are, furthermore, four steel screws which support the entire Instrument, three of which are marked with the letters **H**. The fourth is not visible from the other side. These provide the convenience for directing the Instrument properly and firmly, while they are rotated upon certain small metal plates placed beneath—so that they do not press unevenly upon the floor below with their weight and sharp point—until the Observer finds the Instrument to be uniformly aligned to the plane of some <term>vertical circle</term> <note>A vertical circle is an imaginary circle passing through the zenith and nadir, used to measure altitude above the horizon.</note>, as determined by a hanging plumb-line.

Its use, when handled in this way, is for precisely measuring all **Altitudes**, both of the <term>luminaries</term> <note>In historical astronomy, "luminaries" refers specifically to the Sun and the Moon.</note> and of the other wandering <note>original: "errantium"; the planets</note> and fixed stars, down to a third or fourth part of a minute—provided they do not exceed a sixth part of the sky <note>A sextant covers 60 degrees, which is one-sixth of a 360-degree circle.</note>. Indeed, by fixing the <term>Quadrant</term> <note>Though describing a sextant, Brahe refers to the 90-degree measuring capability if oriented differently.</note> differently here—namely, so that the line **A. C** faces the **Zenith**—the remaining part can be obtained. However, only Altitudes are detected in this manner, not simultaneous **Azimuths** <note>Azimuth is the horizontal angle of a celestial object from a fixed cardinal point, usually North or South.</note>. Such a sextant is very suitable not only for measuring Altitudes but also for precisely investigating the Distances between stars. It presents no difficulty or uncertainty in this <term>technical practice</term> <note>original: "pragmateia"; refers to the systematic execution of astronomical observations.</note>, since it consists of few parts, and these (as I said) are firmly joined together; this ensures that during observation nothing is distorted out of its proper alignment. It also offers the advantage (which we alluded to before) that it can be transported to other locations without difficulty and without damage or inconvenience. This is a quality that should be provided in certain Instruments as much as possible, so that we are not "slaves to places." For it behooves an Astronomer, more than those devoted to other branches of philosophy, to be a <term>citizen of the world</term> <note>original Greek: "κοσμοπολίτην" (kosmopolitēn).</note>, and in every place, as convenience or necessity dictates, to

->consider it his<-
->Fatherland.<-

## EXPLANATION OF THE CONSTRUCTION AND USE.

The very large quadrant shown here (labeled **B**, **D**, **E**, **C**) is the Mural or "Tychonic" quadrant—so called because it was fixed to a wall. We have had it constructed from solid <term>Orichalcum</term> <note>A golden-colored brass or bronze alloy highly valued for scientific instruments due to its durability.</note>, cast and polished to a high finish. It is five inches wide, two inches thick, and its circumference is as large as a radius or semi-diameter of nearly five <term>cubits</term> <note>A cubit is an ancient unit of length, roughly 18 inches or 45 centimeters. A five-cubit radius makes this instrument nearly 7.5 feet tall, allowing for incredible precision.</note> would require. Consequently, the degrees are so large that each individual minute can be further subdivided into six smaller parts. It thus distinguishes every ten seconds clearly, such that even half of these—namely five seconds—can be discerned without difficulty. All these markings are made using certain transversal points, very finely engraved in our customary manner.

This quadrant is attached to a certain wall (**M P Q**) which faces exactly South with its plane. It is fixed with the strongest screws so that no force or strength can move it from its proper position. This position is pre-aligned so that it corresponds perfectly to the celestial Meridian, drawn from the Horizon to the <term>Zenith</term> <note>The point in the sky directly above an observer.</note>. On another wall (**L M N B**), which is perpendicular to the first and faces exactly East and West, there is a gilded brass cylinder located at the very top where the Center of the Quadrant is. This is gilded so that it is not harmed by the air or gather any tarnish. This cylinder is shown near the letter **A** in a certain square hole in that same wall, which can be opened and closed from the outside with a convenient shutter. This allows sightings to be taken around the said cylinder when the sky is clear, using the <term>pinnacidia</term> <note>Sighting vanes or "sights" used to align the eye with a celestial object.</note> at either **D** or **E**. For this quadrant has two sights, so that one may use whichever is preferred, depending on what is most convenient for measuring the Altitude. Each of these sights is a square in plane, the size of a palm; the diameter of the aforementioned cylinder corresponds exactly to this, so that through parallel slits applied to the sights themselves, a sighting can be made in all four places. This is done if both the altitude and the crossing of the Meridian are to be captured at once. Otherwise, if only the Altitude is desired, the observer (depicted near **F**) completes the task using the upper and lower slits and the corresponding parts of the cylinder. He then calls out the calculated altitude to another student (sitting at the table with a lamp near **G**) to be inscribed in the Book of Observations. To ensure the time of the observation and the exact moment of the crossing of the Meridian is clearly established, a third person (marked at **H**) attends to the clocks (**I** and **K**). When the observer at **F** gives the signal, the timekeeper notes the exact moment, which is also recorded in the book by the one sitting at **G**.

The clocks I mentioned are of such a quality that they indicate not only the individual minutes of the hours, but also the seconds with the greatest possible precision, emulating the steady revolution of the heavens. Although this is difficult to achieve, with the required diligence it can be obtained to an extent, and any error that creeps in can be noticed and corrected. Therefore, there must be at least two such clocks, so that one may correct the other if necessary. We have four such clocks ready; the largest of these manages everything with only three wheels. Its largest wheel is cast from solid, pure brass and has 1200 teeth, being two cubits in diameter. The rest may be judged from this. The other three clocks are smaller and require more wheels; we will provide the composition of all these elsewhere.

Whatever paintings are seen within the circumference of the Quadrant were added solely for the sake of decoration, so that the space within would not remain empty. It will not be out of place to briefly explain these, even if they are less essential to the work. Below the letter **T** is depicted my own likeness, sitting in a chair at a table in a long, floor-length robe. I am leaning back slightly, extending my right hand toward the cylinder as if pointing, while the other hand rests on the table near a book and other items, as if I were indicating to my students what should be observed and how it should be put to use. This portrait was executed with great skill by Tobias Gemperlin, an outstanding artist (whom I brought with me to Denmark from Augsburg years ago), and it is so lifelike that a better likeness could hardly be given. It is represented in a form equal to the stature and size of my whole body.

Above the head, near **X**, a certain gilded brass globe is fashioned. By means of wheels skillfully fitted inside, it rotates on its own, mimicking the daily motion and showing the opposite paths of the Sun and Moon from the poles of the <term>Ecliptic</term> <note>The apparent path of the Sun across the celestial sphere.</note>. It even shows the Moon turning itself according to its phases and illumination, increasing or decreasing them. The Sun, meanwhile, within the 24 hour-circles, besides its own motion, is revolved in a daily course around the axes of the Equator, showing the individual hours of the day, as well as the time of its rising and setting, and its crossing of the meridian on both sides. This ingenious <term>automaton</term> <note>original: "αυτόματον"</note> <note>A self-operating machine or "robot." This complex mechanical device served as both a clock and a planetarium.</note> was invented by myself and constructed at my expense in the year of our Lord 1590, for the most Serene Prince, Lord CHRISTIAN <note>Christian IV (1577–1648), the King of Denmark and Norway.</note>, then the King-Elect, my most merciful Lord, when previously...

<summary>Tycho Brahe describes the physical specifications, team-based observation process, and decorative elements of his famous Mural Quadrant at Uraniborg, including its high-precision brass construction and innovative clocks.</summary>
<keywords>Mural Quadrant, Tychonic Quadrant, astronomical precision, transversal points, orichalcum, Uraniborg, scientific observation, Tobias Gemperlin, astronomical clocks, automaton, Christian IV</keywords>
