<meta>This page continues the description of the mural quadrant, specifically detailing the narrative painting found within the arc of the instrument and the technical application of the device. The previous page ended while describing the center cylinder of the quadrant.</meta>

# EXPLANATION OF THE CONSTRUCTION AND USE.

...namely at the age of 7, and again around the 14th year of his age, he did not disdain to graciously visit me at <term>Uraniborg</term> <note>Tycho's famous observatory on the island of Hven, meaning "The Castle of Urania."</note> on the Island of Hven. He was accompanied, besides the rest of the court nobility, by three primary Senators of the Kingdom who then presided over the government. I humbly gave him a gift which he still preserves today. In return, the Most Serene King-Elect graciously presented me with a gold chain of peculiar workmanship—the kind he was then accustomed to wear—beautifully crafted and decorated with his own likeness. Above the Globe itself, which we have just mentioned, at the letter **V**, some part of our Library is represented. At **Y** and **Z**, there hang two portraits formed within a round frame; one is of the most serene and powerful Frederick II, King of the Danes, of most lauded memory; the other is of the most serene Queen Sophia, his most illustrious consort, both of whom always supported me and my studies with Royal and merciful favor. 

Furthermore, regarding what is seen in the lower painting: first, at the top near the numbers **1, 2, 3, 4**, some of my instruments are depicted there. Then, below this floor, the <term>Museum</term> <note>In the 16th century, a 'museum' referred to a place of study or a library rather than a public exhibition space.</note> is shown. Here, near **5** and **8**, are the tables where my astronomical students were accustomed to sit while calculating or performing other tasks related to this work. There were four such tables in the Museum itself. I was always accustomed to support at least six or eight—sometimes ten or twelve—such students, summoned from everywhere, in addition to the boys and younger disciples of that kind. Between these tables, at **6** and **7** behind the column placed in the center of the round Museum, is seen that greatest brass Globe, six feet in diameter, which we will later display and explain in its own place. Finally, below all these things near the numbers **9, 10, 11**, is seen my chemical laboratory, which was entirely subterranean and hidden; in it, I had equipped sixteen <term>pyronomic furnaces</term> <note>From the Greek 'pyr' (fire) and 'nomos' (law/rule); these were specialized chemical or alchemical furnaces designed for precise heat control.</note> of various kinds and forms. For I was intensely devoted to this study, even from my early youth, no less than to Astronomy; and I cultivated it with great diligence and at no small expense. Lastly, near my feet at number **12**, lies one of our hunting dogs, who was very faithful and wise. He is depicted in the form and size in which he was seen: not so much as a symbol of nobility, but as a <term>hieroglyphic</term> <note>Tycho uses this term in the Renaissance sense to mean a symbolic representation or emblem.</note> of sagacity and fidelity. Thus you have a brief explanation of the entire painting, insofar as it was possible to imitate it in such a small form; three different and excellent artists painted it for me. The painter from Augsburg whom I mentioned produced my likeness; the architectural parts and what is contained within them were fashioned by my architect, Johannes de Embda Stenvvichel. However, the parts depicted above those, resembling a description of regions and mountains where the Sun is seen to set, were added by Johannes of Antwerp, the Royal painter at <term>Kronborg</term> <note>The famous castle at Elsinore, Denmark.</note>. For each of these three artists excelled above the others in the specific work he performed. Finally, at the top near **R** and **S**, there is an inscription for the portrait and the whole painting, just as you see.

The use of this very large Quadrant is for investigating the altitudes of the stars with the greatest accuracy, within one-sixth part of a minute <note>Equivalent to 10 seconds of arc.</note>. This is done by making an alignment through the sights that project at the top and bottom near both circumferences of the cylinder, corresponding to these; and by counting the altitude itself on the exterior surface of the Quadrant at the position of that same sight. The moment of transit through the Meridian can also be obtained from both sides at once—both of the sights and of the cylinder—by means of the accurate clocks we have described. How the location of a star is established from a given altitude and the moment of time in which it passes the Meridian (with the motion of the Sun applied) is well known to Astronomers. I have relied most heavily on this quadrant for restoring the motion of the Sun based on its Meridian altitudes, by allowing the square shadow of the cylinder to fall onto the interior part of one of the sights, which receives and encompasses that shadow perfectly in a square shape. Nevertheless, I have also called other large 

->quadrants into counsel, so that a matter so subtle<-
->might be free from all suspicion<-
->of error.<-

# THE ROTATING QUADRANT
## AZIMUTHAL

<note>The Rotating Azimuthal Quadrant was a landmark instrument that allowed astronomers to measure both the height (altitude) and the horizontal position (azimuth) of a star at the same time by turning the entire frame on a vertical axis.</note>

# EXPLANATION OF THE CONSTRUCTION AND USE.

...specifically, at the age of seven, and once more around the fourteenth year of his age, he <note>The "he" refers to the young King Christian IV of Denmark (1577–1648).</note> did not think it beneath him to graciously visit me at <term>Uraniborg</term> <note>original Latin/Greek: "Uraniburgum"; Tycho’s premier observatory on the island of Hven, which translates to "The Castle of the Muse of Astronomy."</note> on the Island of Hven. He was accompanied, in addition to the other members of the courtly nobility, by three of the kingdom's primary <term>Senators</term> <note>These were the "Gubernatores" or Regents who ruled during the King's minority.</note> who were then at the head of the government. At that time, I humbly presented him with a gift which he still keeps in his possession today. In return, the Most Serene King-Elect graciously gave me a gold chain of unusual craftsmanship—of the sort he was then accustomed to wear—which was beautifully worked and adorned with his own likeness. 

Above the celestial globe itself, which we have just described, at the letter **V**, a portion of our library is shown. At **Y** and **Z**, two portraits are displayed within circular frames; one represents the most serene and powerful Frederick II, King of the Danes, of most honored memory; the other shows the most serene Queen Sophia, his most illustrious wife. Both of them always supported me and my scientific pursuits with royal and compassionate favor.

Furthermore, regarding the details visible in the lower section of the painting: first, at the top near the numbers **1, 2, 3, 4**, several of my astronomical instruments are shown. Below this level, the <term>Museum</term> <note>In the 16th century, a "museum" was a private place for study, contemplation, and scientific work, similar to a modern laboratory or library.</note> is depicted. Here, near the numbers **5** and **8**, are the tables where my students of astronomy were accustomed to sit while they were calculating or engaged in other tasks required by this work. There were four such tables in the Museum itself. I was always accustomed to su...

## EXPLANATION OF THE CONSTRUCTION AND USE.

**W**E have constructed a rotating <term>Quadrant</term> <note>A quadrant is an astronomical instrument used to measure angles up to 90 degrees, a quarter of a circle.</note> which would not only investigate Altitudes with precision (like the previously mentioned Sextant), but also reveal <term>Azimuths</term> <note>Azimuth refers to the horizontal direction of a celestial object, measured as an angle from the North or South point of the horizon.</note> exactly, in the form you see here. It has a radius from center A to the circumference at B or C of four <term>cubits</term> <note>A cubit is an ancient unit of measure, approximately 18 inches, based on the length of a forearm.</note>, and the circumference is so finely subdivided by <term>transversal points</term> <note>original: "puncta transversalia"; Tycho's innovative method of using diagonal dots to allow for much more precise readings than standard scale marks.</note> that it shows a fourth part of one minute to the sharp sighter when the eye is applied to the <term>pinnule</term> <note>original: "pinnacidium"; a small plate with a sighting hole or slit.</note> at K. The ruler A K is moved until the star being observed shines equally on both sides of the <term>Cylinder</term> <note>A central sighting post at the pivot point of the instrument.</note> near A. Then, the ruler shows the sought altitude where it crosses the divisions at K. Furthermore, the Index Q, which extends to the azimuthal circumference marked by R, reveals the exact moments of the Azimuths.

The structural frameworks seen within the area of the Quadrant are arranged in various ways for this purpose: to hold and preserve the instrument firmly both in its own plane and in its other necessary alignment. The <term>Limb</term> <note>The graduated edge or arc of the instrument.</note> where the divisions are located is covered everywhere with <term>brass</term> <note>original: "Orichalcicis"; a golden-colored alloy like brass or bronze used for its durability and ease of engraving.</note> plates; likewise, the remaining parts of the Quadrant are protected with encrustations partly of <term>steel</term> <note>original: "Chalybeis".</note> and partly of brass, as was necessary. This Quadrant is firmly attached to a strong, four-sided iron column, L M N, with which it also rotates very easily toward whatever part of the sky one pleases. For the said column is made rounded at the top and is firmly fitted into a hole in a thick iron piece, which is fixed crosswise into the round wall of the small tower or crypt in which the instrument was kept. At the bottom, where the same column ends in a rounded steel point near G, it rotates in a similarly pointed hole in a small steel plate. This plate can be pushed in any necessary direction by four externally placed small screws until both of the Quadrant's <term>plumb lines</term> <note>original: "perpendicula"; weighted strings used to ensure the instrument is perfectly vertical.</note>, starting at A and ending near S, direct both the plane of the Quadrant and its back so that, however it is rotated, it is found to represent any vertical circle exactly.

When this has been done once, and the arrangement of the Quadrant has been carefully provided for and fixed with the required screws toward various and diverse quarters of the world, there will be no need to attend to the plumb lines for a long time to see if the Quadrant has moved from its required position. Indeed, that cannot happen unless some great violence occurs unexpectedly; and then it can easily be restored in the manner mentioned before. For it stands so firmly on the round stone H—which, being solid, rests unmoved on another stone foundation deep in the earth—and the iron piece that stands crosswise above (resting, as I said, on the stable wall) does not allow the iron column to which the Quadrant is attached to wobble at all from its hole in the center.

**T**HIS round wall, which perfectly represents the circumference of a circle (whose diameter is about 6 cubits, providing enough space between the circumference of the Quadrant and the hollow of the crypt for the observer to walk around conveniently), has upon it a certain mechanical circle covered with brass plates. On this circle, it provides all the Azimuths of the entire Horizon (to which it corresponds in its equilibrium), subdivided into minutes. Together with it, the starting point of the Azimuths remains unmoved, which depends on the <term>meridian line</term>. This was sought with such care and precision that it does not deceive the observer by even a single minute. 

We discovered the meridian line by another and far more certain method than anyone has done until now, using the <term>Pole Star</term> <note>The "stella polaris" or North Star.</note> itself, especially when it causes the maximum angle of horizontal removal <note>Tycho is referring to the star's "greatest elongation," the point where its horizontal movement appears to stop momentarily before it turns back toward the meridian.</note> on either side of the pole; at that time, it seems to stay in the same point of Azimuth for a little while. This was a worthwhile effort, since few things in astronomical observations can be perfected without the most accurate knowledge of the meridian line.

The method of handling and observing with this instrument is as follows: it is directed toward whatever star is to be noted in the heavens by rotating the plane of the Quadrant and by raising or lowering its ruler with its sights, until that star is seen exactly through the slits of the pinnule near the eye—first from the upper slit to the upper edge of the cylinder, and then immediately the same is done through the lower slit to the lower edge. In this way, the ruler reveals the sought altitude of the star most exactly where it cuts the divisions at K. 

If, however, one intends to know the Azimuth at the same time, the Quadrant is rotated slightly back and forth on its column until the same star is seen simultaneously through both lateral slits along the flat surface of both sides of the cylinder. Then, the index Q on the horizontal circumference reveals the very Azimuth itself. Because these two things are not easily discerned at once due to the continuous and rapid motion of the heavens, it is better to fix the index Q at a certain Azimuth which the star is approaching, and wait until it is brought down by that universal motion, and in that same instant...

# EXPLANATION OF THE CONSTRUCTION AND USE.

...standing, the height is captured by moving the <term>sighting-ruler</term> <note>original: "Regulę dioptra"; the alidade or movable arm of the instrument equipped with sights.</note> up or down: which is managed with almost no effort.
The use of this <term>Instrument</term> <note>original: "Organi"</note> is the same as others that are operated by <term>Altitudes</term> and <term>Azimuths</term>. However, it holds this prerogative over others: that through it, everything may be obtained most exactly and safely, even within a quarter part of a minute, without labor. This was denied to earlier instruments, both because of their small size and also because their structure was not so refined or easy to use. Therefore, the <term>Astronomer</term> should hold this method of construction as highly recommended. Furthermore, so that the instrument may be used more conveniently both during observation and calculation, the **Steps** represented by **T** ascend all around, following almost the same curve as the <term>Quadrant</term> itself, so that one standing on the highest **Step** is able to observe a star positioned near the <term>Horizon</term>; whereas sitting on the lowest, one may observe those near the vertex <note>The zenith, or the point directly overhead.</note>, and likewise for those in between. 

Moreover, this instrument is covered with its own "hidden tower," a roof skillfully composed of smooth boards, which is attached below to a certain round **Ring** <note>original: "Armillæ"</note> made of strong wood, on the horizontal wall outside the <term>Azimuthal circle</term>. That **Ring** has, exactly within itself at four transverse locations, certain small wheels by which it can be rotated in any direction toward the circumference of the Horizon with the least effort. In this way, the oblong windows which the roof has (also made of boards), and which are opposite to one another, can easily be directed toward any star to be observed. These windows are closed once the observation is finished, so that the instrument remains immune from the damage of the air, rains, and winds. This is a precaution to be taken with other machines as well, by whatever means possible. For these cost a great deal and are easily rendered liable to damage unless they are kept safe from 
-> violence and elemental <-
-> contamination and changes. <-

# THE GREAT STEEL QUADRANT,
## ALSO ENCLOSED WITHIN A STEEL SQUARE
### enclosed and likewise <term>Azimu-</term>
### <term>thal.</term> <note>original: "Azimuthalis." An azimuthal instrument is one capable of rotating horizontally to measure the "azimuth"—the compass direction of a star—in addition to its height above the horizon.</note>

<note>This title page introduces the "Quadrans Magnus Chalibeus." In the 16th century, large instruments were often made of wood, which could warp or swell. By constructing this <term>Quadrant</term> (an instrument shaped like a quarter-circle) out of steel and mounting it within a square frame, Tycho Brahe achieved unprecedented stability and precision in his observations.</note>

<meta>catchword: EXPLI-</meta> <note>The catchword "EXPLI-" indicates that the following page begins with the word "Explicatio," or "Explanation."</note>

<keywords>Great Steel Quadrant, Tycho Brahe, azimuthal quadrant, astronomical instruments, 16th-century astronomy, Uraniborg</keywords>

<sig>B 4</sig>
<meta>catchword: Azimu-</meta>

## EXPLANATION OF THE CONSTRUCTION AND USE.

MOROVER, this Quadrant, consisting entirely of solid steel and further surrounded by a steel Square, performs the same functions as the previous one; indeed, it does so even more precisely, since it somewhat exceeds it in size. For it measures nearly five <term>cubits</term> <note>A cubit is an ancient unit of length; Tycho's "Danish cubit" was roughly 62 centimeters. This instrument's radius was therefore over 3 meters.</note> from the center to the circumference, not counting the Square—also made of solid steel—which encompasses it. The Quadrant itself, on the side where the markings are located, is so skillfully overlaid with a plate of <term>brass</term> <note>original: "Orichalcum." A golden-colored copper alloy preferred for scientific instruments because it is easier to engrave than steel.</note> that one might think it was fused to the iron. This was done because brass holds fine divisions more accurately than steel, no matter how well the latter is polished, and it is not as subject to rust. Each individual Degree is subdivided not only into single minutes, but each of these minutes is split into six smaller parts; thus, each part represents ten seconds, and this is achieved by a special method we commonly use elsewhere <note>Tycho is referring to his use of "transversal" dots, a system that allowed him to read fractions of a degree with high precision before the invention of the vernier scale.</note>.

The Square which surrounds the Quadrant is not overlaid with brass, because it is used less frequently; the Quadrant itself shows what is sought with sufficient precision. The Square serves as a support and allows for more convenient rotation, even if it had no other use. Nevertheless, even this Square is marked upon the steel itself as finely as possible, also using our special method, so that each of its sides—both the one facing the Horizon **D C** and the one facing the Zenith **B C**—contains the final number of the <term>table of sines</term> <note>original: "canonis sinuum." In the 16th century, sines were calculated as lengths rather than ratios, typically for a circle with a very large radius like 10,000,000.</note> with 6 <term>zeros</term> <note>original: "Ziphrarum." From the Arabic "sifr," meaning zero; Tycho is describing the decimal precision of his mathematical tables.</note>. A rule drawn from the center across the entire plane shows the altitude sought during observation on both the Quadrant and the Square: on the former by the equal divisions of Degrees, and on the latter according to the geometric ratio of sines and triangles; nonetheless, the result in both places comes to one and the same value.

By the Quadrant, one should understand **A G M E**; by the Square, **A B C D**. By the Rule, **A P Q**, which also has a <term>sight</term> <note>original: "pinnacidium & dioptra." These are the metal plates with small holes or slits used to align the eye with a star.</note> at **P**, as well as a cylinder near the center **A**, in the same manner as in the previous instruments. That part of the Rule, **P Q**, which extends beyond the Quadrant so far as to reach the furthest corner of the Square, can be added or removed as one pleases.

Furthermore, this instrument has its own supports and frameworks for bracing and stability, though fewer than the previous one, since it is entirely made of metal. To prevent it from becoming too burdened by their weight, the ribs themselves (marked by the letters **G H N O** and **E K L M**), which cross each other diagonally, are made of solid steel and firmly hold the entire Square, along with the Quadrant it contains, in its proper plane and arrangement. 

Moreover, this Quadrant, together with its Square, is attached to a very robust iron column near **X** and **Y**, with which it is also conveniently and very easily rotated toward all parts of the sky. For that column turns at the top in a round hole located in the middle of the iron fittings indicated by the letters **A**, which are fixed across the wall of the <term>crypt</term> <note>Tycho built many of his instruments in semi-submerged "crypts" at his observatory, Uraniborg, to shield them from the wind.</note>. At the bottom, it turns in an iron plate near **Y** in the same way as described for the previous instrument, so that by means of screws arranged in four directions, the entire instrument can be properly adjusted for use and held firmly in its rotation, lest it be pulled away from the required plane. 

But the plate I mentioned, with its screws, is fixed into a round stone column indicated by **B**, which supports the entire machine and stands deep in the earth upon another stone base. This same instrument also has its own Azimuthal Horizon **T V VV** on the smooth, circular wall of the crypt; this horizon is entirely overlaid with brass and positioned midway up the wall. Here, the observer stands on an upper step (for this instrument, due to its great size, requires its own masonry steps for ascending and descending as the observation demands) and can count the <term>azimuths</term> <note>The azimuth is the horizontal angle or compass direction of a celestial body.</note> shown by the pointers **R** and **S** on both sides. The diameter of this azimuthal horizon is so large that one may walk all the way around the outside of the Quadrant and Square, and even within it; thus, its diameter equals 9 cubits. 

The entire engine is in itself so firmly arranged and secured with screws that, once properly adjusted, it need not fear the force of human strength trying to move it from its place. Such is the construction and arrangement of this instrument; it is also entirely protected from the damage of the weather in the upper part of the wall by a neatly crafted roof. This roof has its own windows that can be opened, and it can be rotated on rollers toward any part of the world as the observation requires. It can also be closed while the instrument is being adjusted, lest the wind violently move its <term>plumb lines</term> <note>original: "perpendicula." These hanging weights were essential for ensuring the instrument was perfectly vertical.</note> near **D** from their natural path, and furthermore, when the instrument is not in use, the roof protects it from the damage of rain and wind. These roofing arrangements were also implemented for the previous quadrant, as we mentioned there.

Its use is the same as the previous one in capturing and counting both altitudes and...

# EXPLANATION OF THE CONSTRUCTION AND USE.

...Azimuths <note>The horizontal angle of a celestial object, measured clockwise from the North.</note>; and insofar as these serve Astronomy, as was indicated before. However, this Quadrant with its attached Square—since it is not only entirely metallic but also exceeds the previous one in size—reveals both Altitudes and Azimuths even more accurately than the former. Thus, it is possible to examine these measurements even to the sixth part of a minute <note>This equals ten seconds of arc, an incredible level of precision for the pre-telescopic era.</note>, particularly where such great precision is required, as is worthwhile in investigating the Solar motion from its <term>Altitude</term> <note>The height of a star or planet above the horizon.</note> and <term>Declination</term> <note>The angular distance of a celestial body north or south of the celestial equator.</note>.

I will also add this as a concluding point: that by using these two Quadrants—namely, the previous revolving one and this revolving steel one—together with the Great Brass Mural (or Tychonic) Quadrant (upon which we placed our greatest trust for investigating the Declinations of the Sun with precision <note>original: "ad amussim," literally "according to the carpenter's rule."</note>, so that its position and entire course might be restored without error). We did the same for subtly examining the Declinations of the Stars when they crossed the <term>Meridian</term> <note>The imaginary great circle in the sky that passes through the celestial poles and the observer's zenith.</note>, where the highest precision was necessary. For these three massive Instruments, properly arranged and adapted for use, yielded the same Altitude even down to the smallest fractions of a minute, and they agreed with one another in every respect. The Sextants and other instruments used alongside them also bore witness to this.

# THE GREAT SEMICIRCLE
## AZIMUTHAL. <note>Azimuth refers to the horizontal direction of a celestial object, measured as an angle around the horizon. A "Semicircle" (180 degrees) offered a wider field of measurement than the 90-degree Quadrants discussed previously.</note>

<note>This page is dominated by a large, intricate engraving of the "Great Azimuthal Semicircle," an instrument designed by Tycho Brahe. It features a vertical semicircular arc mounted on a complex base with leveling screws and a central sighting mechanism (an alidade). The figures at the top are likely allegorical or decorative elements common in high-status scientific publications of the 16th century.</note>

<meta>catchword: EXPLANATION</meta> <note>original: "EXPLI-"</note>

<summary>This page serves as a title and visual introduction to the Great Azimuthal Semicircle, one of the primary instruments used in the observatory. The accompanying engraving illustrates the device's complex mechanical design and scale.</summary>

<keywords>Great Azimuthal Semicircle, astronomical instruments, Tycho Brahe, scientific engraving, celestial measurement, azimuth</keywords>

--- Page 34 ---
<meta>This page continues the description of the "Great Azimuthal Semicircle" introduced on the previous page. The previous page provided a visual plate; this text now explains the mechanical construction, the geometric principles behind its scale, and the symbolic meaning of its decorative elements. The catchword "Usus" (Usage) connects to the title of this section.</meta>

# EXPLANATION OF THE CONSTRUCTION AND USE.

[E] we have also devised another certain instrument suitable for precisely taking Altitudes <note>The vertical angle of a celestial body above the horizon</note> along with Azimuths <note>The horizontal direction of a celestial body</note>, the form of which is indicated by the preceding figure. However, it pleased us to adopt a full Semicircle **A B C** here in place of a Quadrant, and nonetheless to establish the center of the divisions at **A**, near the Diameter **A C**. This was done so that the individual degrees on the edge of the Semicircle become twice as large as they would be if the partition were made from the center itself. For it is known from the *Elements* of Euclid <note>original: "Elementis Euclideis"</note> that an angle at the center of a circle is twice as large as one drawn from the circumference to that same arc, according to the twentieth proposition of the third book, and others that pertain to this. In the middle of this, from the center **D** up to the highest point **B**, there is a certain oblong square channel, within which a plumb line—whose lead weight hangs in the hole **D** with some portion of brass wire <note>original: "fili Orichalcici"</note>—shows when the instrument is erected perpendicularly, so that it corresponds exactly to the plane of the vertical semicircle. The framework structures, which are seen on either side in the form of Arches, are applied merely for the sake of stability, so that they might firmly hold this vast semicircle in its plane and circular path. Indeed, it measures six cubits <note>A cubit is an ancient unit of measurement; Tycho's "six cubits" here indicates a diameter of approximately 8 to 9 feet</note> along the Diameter **A C**.

The Rule <note>An alidade, or sighting rod</note> **Y A V** applied to it has a length from **A** to **V** equal to the diameter of the semicircle; additionally, it has a protrusion **A Y** that is slightly thicker, so that the Rule can be more conveniently raised and lowered as if in a state of balance. For this purpose, two handles made of solid metal were added at **Y**. On its other side, it has a locking mechanism with a screw near **X**, where it is secured in the small Quadrant **N O**, so that the Rule remains in a fixed place wherever one wishes until the altitude has been read, in whatever part of the edge of this semicircle it has then passed. This semicircular instrument is also covered everywhere with brass plates, as is necessary, and has three figures at the top, skillfully and artistically carved in solid wood, and so arranged on their pedestals there as appears in the figure. This was done as much for the sake of ornament as to advise something through their representation in the manner of hieroglyphs <note>Here used to mean "symbolic emblems"</note>.

The highest of all is the symbolic image of **Urania** <note>The Muse of Astronomy</note> herself. She is a graceful and beautiful Maiden, her face lifted toward the Heaven gazing at the Stars, lifting a Sphere of the celestial revolutions with her right hand, while with her left she takes those things which the two lower figures standing beneath, as if handmaidens, reach out to her. The color of her tunic is Azure, interwoven with small gold stars and silver fringes.

The figure standing on the left side is the Image of **Geometry**: holding a triangle in her right hand, which she lifts toward Astronomy; in the other, she holds a Compass. By these, she wishes to signal that she serves Astronomy through measurement and mechanical constructions, as well as through the learned science of Triangles. She directs her eyes toward the face of Urania and looks up at her with reverence. The color of her dress is dark brownish, interwoven with green, because she takes her name from earthly things <note>The word "Geometry" literally translates from Greek as "earth-measurement"</note>. On her head, she wears a laurel wreath, by which she shows that she endures forever and is capable of untainted truth.

On the other side, specifically the maiden standing on the right, represents **Arithmetic**; she also looks up at Astronomy, honoring and serving her. She holds chalk in one hand and a tablet in the other, signifying that she expresses the numbers which Astronomy needs to be open to knowledge; she resolves into discrete quantities those things which the former, Geometry, was working out through continuous measures. She also wears a laurel wreath above on her head, since she is no less eternal and true than Geometry. The color of her dress is white, representing paper or parchment, marked here and there with various ciphers <note>Numbers or digits</note>. All these things were done both to decorate the instrument and to indicate that Astronomy, the supreme Queen of all liberal arts, has chosen these two—Geometry and Arithmetic—as her ministers above all others (though she does not neglect the others).

Below this Semicircle is the **Azimuthal Horizon E F G H**, measuring four cubits in diameter, consisting of solid steel and suitably distributed into degrees and their smaller subdivisions on top. In the middle, it has two strong iron pieces intersecting each other crosswise at the center **D**, around which the entire instrument revolves. Iron supports are placed on the other side to keep it perpetually at right angles to the Horizon. Below the iron Horizon are five columns, of which the middle one, indicated by **Q**, stands in the center point below the middle; the remaining four, marked with the letters **P**, carry the circumference at equal distances in four directions. Furthermore, all five have certain special screws at the top, called "perpetual screws" <note>Worm screws used for fine adjustment</note>, marked with the letters **R**. This is also to be understood for the others; these are turned by small handles **S** and **T** on either side and are pushed up or down until the Azimuthal circle resting upon them exactly mimics the equilibrium of the Horizon, and the semicircle standing above matches the plane of some vertical circle. Indeed, these screws can lift or lower a very heavy weight with minimal effort, and they have many other conveniences in Mechanics.

# EXPLANATION OF THE CONSTRUCTION AND USE.

The use of the instrument is sufficiently clear from its very construction: and it is the same as those measurements which are otherwise collected by **Azimuths** <note>The horizontal direction or bearing of a celestial body</note> and **Altitudes** <note>The vertical angle of a celestial body above the horizon</note>; except that here, the same result is achieved and numbered in a **Semicircle** as is done elsewhere in **Quadrants** or **Azimuthal Rulers**, which we shall discuss later.

I will also point out this: whereas previously that steel Quadrant enclosed in a Square (which we discussed in the explanation of the immediately preceding figure) originally stood upon a **Steel Horizon** <term>Horizon</term> <note>original: "Chalybeo Horizonti"; here referring to the physical horizontal base-plate of the instrument</note> at its lower part and was rotated within it—just as we have taken care to represent in another figure, though not displayed here—it later seemed more advisable to rotate it while removed from this horizontal support (as the preceding figure shows). This was because, due to its excessive weight (since it is entirely made of steel, as has been said), it bore down too heavily and forcefully upon the Horizon. Therefore, it could only be turned with great difficulty without the whole machine being shaken or trembling. Nevertheless, its operation can also be set up in that way: and then no...

# THE PARALLACTIC
## INSTRUMENT OR
### The Rules.

<note>original: "Instrumentum Parallaticum / Regularum"; these terms refer to the Triquetrum, an ancient astronomical instrument used to measure the zenith distance of stars. It consists of three hinged "rules" or arms.</note>

# EXPLANATION OF THE CONSTRUCTION AND USE.

The use of the instrument is sufficiently clear from its very construction: and it is the same as those measurements which are otherwise collected by **Azimuths** <note>The horizontal direction or bearing of a celestial body, measured as an angle from the North</note> and **Altitudes** <note>The vertical angle or height of a celestial body above the horizon</note>; except that here, the same result is achieved and numbered in a **Semicircle** as is done elsewhere in **Quadrants** or **Azimuthal Rulers**, which we shall discuss later.

I will also point out this: whereas previously that steel Quadrant enclosed in a Square (which we discussed in the explanation of the immediately preceding figure) originally stood upon a **Steel Horizon** <term>Horizon</term> <note>original: "Chalybeo Horizonti"; here referring to the physical horizontal base-plate of the instrument rather than the skyline</note> at its lower part and was rotated within it—just as we have taken care to represent in another figure, though not displayed here—it later seemed more advisable to rotate it while removed from this horizontal support (as the preceding figure shows). This was because, due to its excessive weight (since it is entirely made of steel, as has been said), it bore down too heavily and forcefully upon the Horizon. Therefore, it could only be turned with great difficulty without the whole machine being shaken or trembling. Nevertheless, its operation can also be set up in that way: and then no...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Parallactic Instrument, also known as the Triquetrum. # EXPLANATION OF THE CONSTRUCTION AND USE.

**I** decided to construct this Parallactic instrument, which others call *Ptolemaic* Rules <term>Ptolemaic Rules</term> <note>Also known as the Triquetrum, an ancient instrument used to measure the "zenith distance" or how far a star is from the point directly overhead</note>, because it measures the distance of stars from the zenith, and thus their altitudes, succinctly with only three required rules. I received an instrument of this kind made entirely of wooden material, which had once belonged to the incomparable **Copernicus** <note>Nicolaus Copernicus (1473–1543), the astronomer who proposed the Sun-centered model of the universe</note>; it was said to have been crafted by his own hands. This was sent to me as a gift by Master **Johannes Hannovius**, a Canon of **Warmia** <note>A region in Poland where Copernicus lived and worked</note>, where Copernicus had previously lived.

This happened in the year 1584, when I sent one of my students who was assisting me in astronomical exercises to that location with a **Sextant** <note>An instrument for measuring angular distances</note>—modeled after the one explained in the third place above—so that he might measure the elevation of the Pole <note>The latitude of the observer's location</note> at Warmia with the greatest exactness using that device. For I suspected that nearly three minutes were missing in Copernicus's observation of this location; I was warned of this by the motion of the Sun and the maximum obliquity of the **Ecliptic** <note>The Sun's apparent path across the sky</note>, which behaved differently than his numbers suggested. Experience itself proved this to be the case. By using my own instrument for many observations of both the fixed stars and the Sun, the height of the Pole there was found to be 54 degrees and 22 ¼ minutes. This has been demonstrated more extensively by us in the first volume of our *Astronomical Exercises* <note>original: "Progymnasmatum Astronomicorum," Tycho's massive work on the stars</note> on page 34 and following, where I show the causes of Copernicus's error in the motion of the Sun, which he had not correctly recalculated.

Copernicus presupposed the latitude of that place from his own observation to be 54 degrees and 19 ½ minutes. Therefore, his measurement falls short of the exact standard by 3 minutes minus ¾ <note>i.e., 2 minutes and 15 seconds</note>, exactly as I had previously gathered from his own given data and the numbers written regarding the solar motion. When my student returned to me, he brought back not only the sextant I had entrusted to him, unharmed in every part, but also this other **Copernican Parallactic Instrument** sent to me as a gift by the Canon I mentioned.

As soon as I saw it, even though it was wooden and unsuitable for practical use, it pleased me so much because of the memory of such a great artist (by whom it was said to have been made) that I could not restrain myself from composing a Heroic Song, which can be found in the first volume of our *Astronomical Letters* on page 105 and following. Shortly thereafter, in imitation of it, I had another one made, covered in **Brass** <term>Orichalcum</term> plates, with a few things changed and added for more convenient use; this is the one represented here.

There are three rules: **DE**, **EF**, and **FD**. The first, **ED**, which is raised toward the **Zenith** <note>The point in the sky directly above the observer</note>, has another rule next to it at **D** which is joined to it and can rotate on a smooth brass fitting. Both are of the same length, namely four cubits each, provided the length of the upper one is taken from letter **D** to **F**. The part that is longer, namely **FG**, is added only so that it may contain and constrain another longer rule within itself, for it is bifurcated <note>split into two branches</note>.

The third rule, **EH**, which is longer and similarly joined to the first rule **DE** near **E**, is also rotatable there; it has the length required when the two sides **DE** and **EF** form a right angle: at which point the rule **DF** is raised so that it is level with the **Horizon**. The **Sights** <term>Dioptrae</term> <note>Devices used to align the instrument with a star</note> through which the sighting of the stars is performed are on the same rule near **H** and **I**. They accomplish the task by means of slits parallel to the forward sighting vane, both above and below, using a method we recently invented. In the **Copernican Instrument**, there were holes in these places, through which stars are seen only with great difficulty.

Another disadvantage is added: the forward hole in sighting vane **I** must be larger than the other if stars are to be seen through it conveniently. When this is the case, it necessarily occupies a certain fraction of a degree—at least an eighth or tenth part. It is impossible to know during observation whether the star is seen exactly in the center of that hole or not; thus, a loss of several minutes can occur. It is a wonder how not only **Copernicus**, but even the ancients who used such sights, could achieve any certainty even if everything else was correct.

Equal divisions are marked on the longer rule by **Transversal Points** <note>Tycho's innovative method for reading fractions of a degree</note>, as is our custom. There are enough of them to fulfill the final number of the **Table of Sines** <term>Canon sinuum</term> with the required six decimal places <term>zyphrarum</term> <note>literally "ciphers"</note> at the position where it matches the first two rules to form an equilateral triangle. It should also be noted that the rule **DK**, which looks toward the zenith, has a certain cavity inside it. In this cavity, a thin brass wire hangs from the upper part, with a small lead weight near **K** at the bottom, so that the wire it holds inside is not moved by the wind. Long little doors have been added to this cavity which can be opened and closed as needed for that purpose. This shows, by a convenient marking added there, when the side **DE** points exactly to the zenith. This can be adjusted by the lower screws at **A** on the pedestal, which are turned with small iron handles at **L**. These three rules are attached to the column **CB**, which stands on the said pedestal, so that by means of round pins...

# EXPLANATION OF THE CONSTRUCTION AND USE.

so that they may be rotated this way and that. I added this convenience to the instrument regarding the supports <term>supports</term> <note>original: "fulcra"</note>; for it was not present in the Copernican version. I also added a small lead weight <term>plummet</term> <note>original: "plumula"</note>, which is located near point **G**, which ensures that the rule **AF** remains stationary at any observed angle until the measurement has been completed on the longer side. I have also applied certain other features for the sake of more convenient use and handling; I shall refrain from mentioning each of them individually, lest I seem to diminish the inventions of my predecessors by over-correcting them.

The use of this Parallactic instrument is for taking the distances of stars from the vertex <note>The "vertex" or Zenith is the point in the sky directly above the observer</note>, just as **Ptolemy** <note>Claudius Ptolemy (c. 100–170 AD), the Greco-Egyptian astronomer whose work dominated the field for centuries</note> frequently did with the Moon, especially for seeking out its maximum latitude. However, he did not achieve this with enough precision or universality, as is clear from what we have restored concerning the Moon’s orbit in an appendix to Chapter 1 of the aforementioned volume of our *Progymnasmata* <note>Tycho’s "Progymnasmata" or "Introductory Exercises" was his massive work on the new star of 1572 and lunar theory</note>. In that work, it is shown from accurate observations that the maximum latitude of the Moon, which occurs during full moons, is not the same as that which occurs in the Quadratures <note>The "Quadratures" are the first and last quarter phases of the Moon</note>; a notable difference exists between them, which may reach a third part of a degree, as can be seen more extensively in that work through its various circumstances and occasions.

Furthermore, the angle of distance from the vertex **FDE** can be obtained from the "Fertile Table" <term>Table of Tangents</term> <note>original: "Canone fæcundo." Early astronomers called the tangent table "fertile" or "fecund" because of its many uses in calculation</note> or even from Sines, using the intermediate parts of **FE**. For in this Triangle, two equal sides **FD** and **ED** are given. The third side, **FE**, is measured on the longer rule after the observation is performed. Therefore, the angle **FDE**, which measures the distance of the star from the Zenith, cannot remain hidden. Its complement to the Quadrant <note>The remaining amount needed to reach 90 degrees</note> is the sought altitude. These Rules also have this convenience: they can be easily disassembled, folded up, and transported to any location. But, to speak the truth as it is, they do not consistently provide the same precision and certainty—no matter how diligently they are crafted—as the aforementioned Quadrants. For it is extremely difficult for the Rules to retain a perfectly straight line for long. If they are longer, they sag from their own weight; if they are shorter, they 
->cannot<-
perform what they promise because of their small size.

# ANOTHER PARALLACTIC INSTRUMENT, OR
## RULERS FACILITATING BOTH ALTITUDES AND
## AZIMUTHS.

<meta>This page continues the technical description of Tycho Brahe’s large Parallactic Instrument, specifically a version he constructed within a tower at his observatory, Uraniborg. It details the mechanical components, materials, and the geometry used to calculate celestial positions.</meta>

# EXPLANATION OF THE CONSTRUCTION AND USE.

**I**ndeed, some years before, I had constructed other rulers in a certain tower, which filled the entire space with their rotation when necessary. This was done by a specific method, partly imitating the ancient <term>Hipparchian</term> <note>Referring to Hipparchus of Nicaea (c. 190–120 BCE), the Greek astronomer who is credited with the original design of the triquetrum</note> manner (as it is said), and partly intended for more stable and convenient use through new additions devised by myself. The following figure represents their structure: in which **A D** is understood to be the first and fundamental ruler, arranged on the plane of the horizon and rotated as needed. Its length is equal to 8 ½ <term>cubits</term> <note>A cubit is an ancient unit of length, roughly 18 to 22 inches; Tycho’s cubit was likely about 15 inches (38 cm) based on his other instruments</note>. It contains on its upper surface the numbers of a <term>table of sines</term> <note>original: "canonis sinuum"</note> skillfully divided by <term>transversal points</term> <note>A system of diagonal dots that allowed for much finer measurement than simple line markings</note> according to my custom, to six <term>digits</term> <note>original: "Zyphrarum"; referring to the precision of the numerical places</note>, numbered twice. To this, two other rulers are made equal when taken together: namely, when they are applied to it; these are **A B** and **B C**, each of which is 4 1/4 cubits in length. The first one, **A B**, is attached to the beginning of the longer one near **A**, so that it can be raised and lowered there, while simultaneously pulling the second one, **B C**, which is attached to it near **B** so that it can turn. However, there are no divisions on these two smaller rulers, but at least the first one, **A B**, has <term>sights and sighting-holes</term> <note>original: "dioptras & pinnacidia"</note> through which the alignment is made, with slits in the lower sight near the eye at **E**, and in the upper at **F**, parallel in every respect: so that alignment may be made through these in both altitudes and <term>azimuths</term> <note>The horizontal direction of a celestial object, measured in degrees from North</note> in every direction. All three of these rulers are made of <term>brass</term> <note>original: "Orichalcicæ"</note> throughout, very finely smoothed and polished, possessing a four-sided shape of sufficient size so they are not subject to bending. So that the two upper rulers may be more conveniently raised and lowered, a certain protruding support has been added to them, indicated by **L N O P**, which also has its own support below, represented by **P V Z**. Near **V**, however, is a <term>screw</term> <note>original: "Cochlea"</note> which directs the support itself in every direction upon a circular plate at **W** in the underlying base of the whole instrument (of which more later), so that it does not weigh itself down with its own weight. This structure extending toward the <term>Zenith</term> <note>The point directly overhead</note> has the advantage of raising the uppermost rulers (as I said) and lowering them at will, while confining them in their own plane so they are not pulled apart this way or that. They are raised and lowered by means of a cord **K L N M**, which has a small pulley near **L N** above, over which it is easily pulled. This is attached to the first ruler near **K**, where an underlying metallic fitting, which turns on a round pin, is so open on the inside that the sight at **F** can be discerned from the eye near the sight at **E** in every direction without obstacle. It also has the advantage that the position of the instrument can be examined by a <term>plumb line</term> <note>original: "perpendiculum"</note> which ends at **Q**: namely, whether **A D** exactly faces the Horizon, and **L N P O** is perpendicular to it. Although this same thing can also be examined by the plumb line **B R**. For it ought to be exactly in the middle between **A** and **C**. Furthermore, to make the rulers easier to raise and to keep them stationary at a chosen spot, and also to prevent them from wobbling but to keep them in the same plane as the lower and longer body, however they are moved up or down, two <term>quadrants</term> **H G** have been added to them on both sides. The first ruler is contained and moves within these, with a steady <term>lead weight</term> <note>original: "plumula"</note> placed near **H**; these quadrants are covered everywhere with brass plates. From the center **A** to the circumference **I**, it is almost three cubits. Finally, when the divisions have been completed and must be counted during an observation near **C** as an example, they are indicated very sharply by a steel index there, just as there are steel weights on both sides near **C**, which also help in holding the ruler **B C** so that it does not fall back by its own weight.

Furthermore, the prop or base (of which I spoke before) on which the whole instrument rests on one part—especially the rear—and rotates, is to be understood by **X W**. In this, near the top at **T**, is a <term>perpetual screw</term> <note>A worm gear or similar mechanism that allows for continuous fine adjustment</note>, like those I spoke of before regarding the semicircular instrument, which, when raised and lowered by a handle at **T**, also correctly directs this entire instrument; so that the lower and longest ruler corresponds exactly to the plane of the Horizon and fits it plainly. Finally, the outermost part of the ruler **D** rests on a circular wall, expressed by the letters **Y**. Below it, on that same firm wall, it has a brass azimuthal circle, suitably subdivided, and analogous to the surface of the Horizon, corresponding to the altitude of the first base. It is twelve cubits in diameter.

The use of the entire instrument is such that, by its benefit, it reveals both altitudes (namely by the rulers themselves, where it reaches the lowest point) and the azimuths themselves, where the end of the same ruler near the index at **D** touches and marks the azimuthal horizon. The altitudes are obtained in this way: Since both sides of the two rulers **A B** and **B C** are equal, and the third side **A C** is established according to the observation and numbering, it cannot be unknown that the Angle **B A C** shows the sought-after altitude. The same is accomplished by another method if one counts where the plumb line **B R** touches the lower ruler in its divisions, starting the count from **A**. For then is given...

<meta>catchword: ARMILLARY (from Latin ARMIL-)</meta>

# EXPLANATION OF THE CONSTRUCTION AND USE.

...a <term>right-angled triangle</term> <note>original: "Triangulum rectangulum"</note> is produced; one side of which is known from the construction, and the other numbered according to the observation. Therefore, the angle at **A**, which was sought, cannot remain unknown <note>This refers to the trigonometric calculation where, by knowing the lengths of the instrument's arms and the distance measured on the base scale, the star's altitude can be calculated</note>. As to how <term>altitudes</term> <note>The vertical angle of a celestial object above the horizon</note> and <term>azimuths</term> <note>The horizontal angle or compass direction of a celestial object</note> are applied, this must be learned from practical astronomy, as we have indicated before.

This instrument, exceeding the previous rulers in size and length, displays everything more precisely and is more stable and fit for use, especially regarding the taking of altitudes. For in simultaneously tracking azimuths, some difficulty arises because of the very size and mass of the instrument. This, however, can be guarded against by applying a certain degree of care; so that the lower ruler is positioned before the observation and fixed immovably at some specific azimuth toward which the star to be observed is turning. Then, care should be taken with the <term>plumb lines</term> <note>original: "perpendicula"; weighted strings used to ensure the instrument is perfectly level and vertical</note> before the altitude is taken, so that all things are properly arranged, both according to the equilibrium of the <term>horizon</term> and the vertical plane. For then both the altitude and the azimuth can be obtained with this instrument without any perceptible error, no less so than with the largest <term>quadrants</term> <note>A 90-degree measuring instrument, which Tycho also built on a massive scale</note> explained above.

->[Decorative woodcut featuring scrollwork and a central mask.]<-

<summary>This page concludes the geometric explanation of the large parallactic instrument, noting that while its massive size provides superior precision for altitude, it requires careful handling of plumb lines to accurately measure azimuth.</summary>
<keywords>right-angled triangle, altitude, azimuth, practical astronomy, triquetrum, plumb lines, quadrants, Uraniborg</keywords>

<sig>C 4</sig>

## EXPLANATION OF THE CONSTRUCTION AND USE.

**T**he preceding instruments were primarily designed so that **Altitudes** and **Azimuths** <note>The vertical and horizontal coordinates of a star.</note> could be observed in the heavens, either separately or together. However, because the astronomical application of these measurements largely requires the calculation of **Triangles** <note>Specifically spherical trigonometry, which was notoriously difficult and time-consuming before the age of computers or even logarithms.</note>—a method not easily mastered by everyone and avoided by some due to its tedious labor—other machines were devised. These allow the longitudes and latitudes of the stars (the two primary values sought) to be obtained with minimal effort and without troublesome calculation. Among these, I find two were primarily used by the ancients. One they called the <term>Armillary Instrument</term> <note>From the Latin *armilla*, meaning "bracelet" or "ring." It is a model of the celestial sphere consisting of a framework of rings.</note>, which was used by **Hipparchus** and **Ptolemy** and received its name from them. The other is called the <term>Torquetum</term> <note>A complex medieval instrument used to transform coordinates between different astronomical systems.</note>, an instrument I believe was devised and used by the **Arabs** or **Chaldeans**. It performs the same function on flat circular surfaces that the former does with **Rings**. I chose not to have this latter instrument constructed, however; for if the machine were to have the required size, it would be weighed down by its own bulk and rendered unfit for use. Nevertheless, if anyone desires its construction, they may consult the *Caesarean Astronomy* of **Apian** near the end, or **Schöner’s** small book on the same, prefixed to the observations of the most distinguished **Regiomontanus** and his disciple **Walther**, a citizen of Nuremberg. There, his wish will be granted.

I have, however, already seen to the construction of an <term>Armillary</term> instrument, in which I have changed several things used by **Hipparchus** and **Ptolemy** for the sake of more reliable convenience. You see its design here. This instrument consists of at least four circular rings (whereas my predecessors used five or six), the first of which, **E B G N H**, represents the <term>Meridian</term>. It must be arranged so that it is analogous to the one in the heavens. Upon this, at **C D**, are the poles of the equator, which are raised or lowered until they correspond exactly to the latitude of the place where the observation is being made. This is checked by the plumb line **B S** hanging from the top near the **Zenith**, where the division of the Meridian indicates the complement of the altitude of the Pole, and is removed by the same amount below the other Pole.

This Meridian contains all the other rings within it. The first of these is **C F I H**, which carries another transversely, **P O Q N**, representing the <term>Zodiac</term> <note>The ecliptic ring, showing the path of the sun and planets.</note>. Its poles are at **I K**, distant from the previously mentioned equatorial poles by the difference between the poles of the Ecliptic and the Equator—that is, the maximum declination of the Zodiac. We have observed this ourselves several times with the greatest accuracy to be 23 degrees and 31 ½ minutes <note>The "obliquity of the ecliptic." Tycho’s measurement was remarkably accurate for his time.</note>, slightly different than our predecessors. Through the poles of this Zodiac passes a polished brass rod **I A K**, having a cylinder at the center **A**; through these, the convenience provided by the full rings used by the ancients is supplied to us. Furthermore, another ring serving for latitudes, indicated by **K L R M**, is turned within this one around the same poles of the Zodiac.

The Zodiacal ring itself has four slotted <term>pinnacidia</term> <note>Viewing sights or vanes used to align the eye with a celestial object.</note> in our usual manner, three of which are visible near the letters **Q**. The fourth is hidden. The latitude ring also has four of this kind near **R**, where the fourth is also hidden. All these sights are made of brass, just as all the rings are covered in brass and have the divisions of degrees made on the outer part through our customary transversal points <note>A method of dividing degrees into smaller minutes using diagonal lines to increase precision.</note>. The Meridian itself is made of solid steel, so that it may more firmly support the inner rings. It measures three cubits <note>Roughly 4.5 to 5 feet (1.5 meters) in diameter.</note> in diameter; from this, the size of the inner and adjacent parts can be inferred.

Next, these rings have a pedestal made of solid, dense iron upon which they entirely rest, indicated by the letters **V T D W Y Z**. There is a hole near **S** for the lead plumb line. In the lower part of this pedestal, four screws at **b** pass through an iron circle in four equidistant places. These, resting upon four stone columns reinforced with iron plates at **a**, can raise and lower the whole instrument so that all its rings correspond exactly in their planes and paths to the celestial circles they represent. Furthermore, four iron supports **X** have been added, so that the pedestal—and with it the whole instrument—does not wobble from side to side due to its great size. Additionally, steps **d** are added, which are climbed when necessary during observation or counting. This instrument also requires a special small building, the roof of which can have half removed during observation, and the remaining part rotated as desired.

The **use** of these Armillaries, like those of Ptolemy, is for taking the longitudes and latitudes of the stars. This requires two observers. One of them looks through both slots of any <term>sight</term> on the Zodiacal ring (which is fixed at the known longitude of a fixed star) at that same star near the Axis **I K**, and thus holds all the rings firmly so that the plane of the Armillary Ecliptic matches the celestial one in every way. Meanwhile, the other observer moves any of the other sights on the Zodiac, so that...

<summary>Tycho Brahe describes his improved version of the Zodiacal Armillary Sphere, a large-scale instrument for measuring celestial coordinates. He emphasizes his mechanical refinements, such as the use of steel for the meridian and specialized sights, to achieve higher precision than ancient astronomers.</summary>
<keywords>Armillary sphere, Tycho Brahe, astronomical instruments, longitude, latitude, meridian, zodiac, pinnacidia, obliquity of the ecliptic</keywords>

## EXPLANATION OF THE CONSTRUCTION AND USE.

<note>original: "illi Sideri..." - This sentence continues from the previous page's discussion of aligning the instrument.</note> ...to that Star whose longitude one is busy investigating, it will be most convenient: until the star is seen exactly through both small slits <note>The narrow openings in the sighting vanes used for precise alignment.</note> along the said axis. For then, the position of that same <term>sighting vane</term> <note>pinnacidium: A sliding piece on the astronomical instrument used for sighting a star.</note> numbered on the **Zodiacal Ring** reveals the sought-after longitude. Furthermore, to derive the latitude, the **Ring of Latitudes** must be moved to the same <term>Ecliptic</term> <note>The sun's apparent path across the sky; the baseline for this coordinate system.</note> longitude that was found to be its position. Then, one of its sighting vanes must be moved up or down until the star is seen through both slits, both above and below, next to the cylinder at point **A**. For then the sighting vane itself will show the latitude that one wished to find, to be counted from the Ecliptic on the said Ring of Latitude, either above or below, according to whether it is Southern or Northern. Thus, both the longitude and latitude of the proposed star are easily revealed.

Although this Armillary Instrument is convenient for measuring the positions of stars quickly and promptly, and was constructed by us in this improved form—which allows its component rings to wobble out of their proper plane less than other versions—nevertheless, even this does not so exactly prevent an error of one or two <term>minutes</term> <note>Minutes of arc; 1/60th of a degree.</note> from creeping in. This occurs primarily because the Zodiacal Ring does not revolve in perfect balance everywhere; by its own weight, it pulls itself and the other rings somewhat out of their proper planes in various ways. It is for this reason that we have rarely used such rings, especially when the highest precision is required. Instead, we have devised **Equatorial Rings**, which are not subject to this flaw. This issue cannot be corrected in larger Zodiacal instruments because of the even heavier weight of the rings. In smaller ones, the weight is not as noticeable, **But**
->their smallness does not allow for<-
->exact observation of minutes.<-

<summary>Tycho Brahe explains the method for determining a star's longitude and latitude using his improved armillary sphere, while admitting that the weight of the rings can still cause minor inaccuracies.</summary>

<meta>The previous page concluded a technical description of using a zodiacal armillary to find celestial longitude and latitude. This page transitions to a new section describing the "Equatorial Armillary," a more stable and balanced version of the instrument designed by Tycho Brahe.</meta>

### EXPLANATION OF THE CONSTRUCTION AND USE.

---

**W**e have therefore devised certain **Equatorial Armillaries** <note>A series of metal or wooden rings representing the great circles of the heavens, used to determine the coordinates of stars.</note> which, being arranged in the equilibrium of the Earth’s Polar Axis, are not pulled in either direction by their own weight or rotation, nor can they be forced out of the exact alignment of the plane of the circles they represent. This stability was lacking in the previous version for the reasons stated. Their form is as shown in the accompanying illustration. There are only three rings with a smooth axis; these measure both the equatorial distances of the stars <note>Right Ascension</note> and their declinations <note>The distance of a star north or south of the celestial equator.</note>. The first and largest of these, labeled **A E S F**, contains and supports the others and serves as the **Meridian**; it has a diameter of nearly 4 cubits <note>Approximately 6 feet or 1.8 meters.</note>. The size of the other inner rings must be estimated from this one. They must also be arranged and regulated according to its plane. Near **A**, and at the opposite location at **D**, it has a smooth axis (as I mentioned), which rotates with the inner ring and serves to measure declinations. At the midpoint of this axis (which is also the center of all the rings), it supports a **Cylinder** fixed perpendicularly at **G**, which is the same size in diameter as the axis itself.

The axis, along with the meridian it passes through diametrically, must be raised until the plumb line **B V** (above and below) touches the complement of the polar altitude of that location on its divisions—which show every single minute. It should be parallel to the plane of the meridian, or gently touching it on both sides. The inner ring, which I said serves for measuring declinations, is indicated by **N M O P**. This ring can sometimes be aligned with the plane of the meridian, and then rotated inside it in any direction, as the position of the star to be observed requires.

In two places, where it encircles another transverse ring crossing it at right angles (which is assigned to the **Equator**), it has a plate on the outside indicating the degrees and minutes of the equator on both sides—that is, from one side and its opposite. It also contains its <term>sighting vanes</term> <note>pinnacidia: Metal plates with small slits or holes used to align the instrument with a star.</note> in four places, or quadrants; at least two of these are shown here at **M** and **O**. In these, the slits are parallel and equidistant to the cylinder at **G**, and also to the axis **A D** from the other side. Each quadrant on the outer part of the ring nearest the eye is divided into 90 degrees, and each of these into 60 minutes in our usual manner, often indicated by transversal points.

The other transverse ring, which I said fulfills the duties of the **Equator**, is shown by **H L K I**. It is firmly attached to the aforementioned meridian in two opposite places at **C** and its diameter, standing exactly at right angles to it, where it remains motionless. Its outer part is also finely divided into all the degrees of the circle and their minutes. It has four sighting vanes made in the same way as the previous ones, two of which, **I** and **K**, are shown here on one half of the circle. The remaining two are to be understood as being in the other hemisphere.

All the sighting vanes are skillfully crafted from <term>brass</term> <note>orichalcum: A gold-colored alloy, likely brass or a similar copper alloy, highly valued for scientific instruments.</note>. The entire meridian is made of solid steel, so that it is strong enough to support the remaining inner parts. The two rings that cross each other within it are fitted with strong brass plates only on their outer parts (which I mentioned), where the divisions are located. They are not covered in brass all the way around, so that they might weigh less. They are nevertheless constructed from wooden boards curved into circles and treated with multiple layers of bitumen <note>A tar-like substance used for waterproofing and stabilization.</note>. They are so artificially composed that they do not consist of a single solid piece of wood, but of several thin strips smoothed lengthwise and glued together with such care that, as one strip grew upwards, the next was applied in the opposite way—that is, the part facing the top and the root are swapped.

For experience has taught me that wooden material for instruments must be handled this way so they are less prone to warping and changing. For this purpose, walnut and a species of fir which the Germans call <term>spruce-wood</term> <note>original: "Feuchtenholz" — Referring to the Picea abies or Norway Spruce.</note> are especially suitable, provided they are without defects and have been dried for a long time. Afterward, the rings, glued together from various wooden layers and brought to the correct shape and size, are wrapped in thin linen or parchment glued to them. They are then encrusted and colored with red lead mixed with white lead and linseed oil, so that they feel the changes in the air less.

Furthermore, these rings rest on a pedestal also made of solid and strong wood of a particular shape, which you see marked by the letter **Y**. The square base at the bottom has screws <note>cochleas: Specifically leveling screws.</note> at the corners at **X**, by which the whole instrument is directed into the required arrangement analogous to the celestial circles it mimics. Four iron supports are also added toward its upper part near the meridian, serving as a brace and stronger support so the rings do not wobble; one is marked **F b X**, the other **E A X**. The remaining two are on the back side. In the place where this pedestal touches the meridian, it is rounded like a semicircle, so that the meridian resting on it touches it on all sides and leans firmly upon it. For this reason...

# EXPLANATION OF THE CONSTRUCTION AND USE.

For this reason, four metal brackets are placed near **F**, **S**, **R**, and **E**, within which the **Meridian** <note>The large outer ring representing the celestial meridian.</note> is held and can be turned gradually and firmly until its Axis corresponds to the Axis of the world, and thus be fixed immovably. Below, on the sides of this pedestal, on either side of the hole which holds the lead weight near **V**, there are four portraits skillfully painted for the sake of decoration; these represent the likenesses of *Ptolemy*, *Albategnius* <note>The Latinized name of Al-Battani, a 9th-century Arab astronomer whose work was foundational for European science.</note>, and *Copernicus*; they also include our own. Two of these—namely, *Copernicus’s* and mine—are seen near each other. The remaining two are hidden on the other side. Other matters concerning this pedestal, such as the crossbars near **g** and **f**, or whatever other parts are applied for the sake of stability or decoration, are sufficiently clear from the illustration itself to anyone looking carefully.

The **use** of this instrument is for observing (as I said) both the *Declinations* and the *Right Ascensions* of the *Stars*. For the rotated **Armilla** <note>ring</note> **D N M P**, with its Axis, is drawn toward the star to be noted, and its <term>pinnacidia</term> <note>original: "pinnacidia"; these are sliding sight-vanes used to align the eye with the star.</note> are moved back and forth upon it until any star is sighted through them parallel to the **Cylinder G**, both above and below. Then the sight-vane itself reveals the desired **Declination** <note>The coordinate of a star measured north or south from the celestial equator.</note> upon the divisions of the outer **brass** <note>original: "Orichalcica"</note> plate. In the other or transverse ring, a suitable sight-vane is moved toward some star whose **Right Ascension** <note>The coordinate of a star measured eastward along the celestial equator.</note> is already known from elsewhere. Meanwhile, the remaining sight-vane, which is most convenient, is moved by another observer (for there must be two in this process) toward any other star, until that star is also seen on both sides of the Axis of the instrument. Afterward, the arc intercepted between both sight-vanes is counted; this shows the **Ascensional Difference of the Stars**, which, being added or subtracted as the calculation requires, reveals the previously unknown Right Ascension from the known one. The same can be achieved if the Declination Armilla is fixed at the position of the *known Right Ascension* and the known star is sighted near it, while in the meantime another sight-vane on the **Equatorial Armilla** is moved by another observer until the star whose Right Ascension is sought is sighted around the Axis. In that case, the same sight-vane shows the desired angle on the outer limb of the Equator without any addition or subtraction. To what end the accurately observed *Declinations and Right Ascensions of the Stars* lead, and how easy is the derivation of their 

->*Longitude as well as Latitude* from these,<-
->is known by those who are well-versed in the *Astronomical Arena*;<-
->and we shall explain this elsewhere in a brief<-
->summary.<-

# EXPLANATION OF THE CONSTRUCTION AND USE.

For this reason, four metal brackets are placed near **F**, **S**, **R**, and **E**, within which the **Meridian** <note>The large outer ring representing the celestial meridian, the circle passing through the celestial poles and the observer's zenith.</note> is held and can be turned gradually and firmly until its Axis corresponds to the Axis of the world, and thus be fixed immovably. The **use** of this instrument is for observing (as I said) both the **Declinations** <note>The coordinate of a star measured in degrees north or south of the celestial equator.</note> and the **Right Ascensions** <note>The celestial equivalent of longitude, measured eastward along the celestial equator from the vernal equinox.</note> of the **Stars**.
