# EXPLANATION OF THE CONSTRUCTION AND USE.

<margin>A</margin> I have also seen to it that other Equatorial Armillaries of the same size were constructed, the design of which you see here. The outer ring represents the **Meridian** (as before). It is made entirely of steel, polished perfectly on all sides, and marked with divisions covering the entire circle, so that it even contains individual minutes. In this ring, all other parts—the plumb line, the Poles, and the Axis—are to be understood in the same way as in the previous instruments. As for what is contained within this ring, there are three other rings, so that the whole instrument consists of four, which is one more than the preceding one; this is in addition to the smooth Axis and the cylinder fixed to it, which also conveniently serves the function of a ring. This instrument has three inner rings (excluding the Meridian) for this reason: the **Equator** is rotatable, rather than stationary as in the previous design. Therefore, the ring **I F G** is required to support and hold the Equator at right angles; this ring is also made of pure steel to make it stronger for that task, though it is marked with no divisions. The Equator itself, indicated by **P S R**, has its own divisions down to individual minutes, and indeed on both surfaces, as the need to distinguish either equatorial distances or moments of time requires. <term>Pinnacidia<note>Sighting vanes: small plates with holes or slits used to align the observer's eye with a star.</note></term> are fitted to it at **R** and **P**, as well as several others not depicted here that serve the observation; their form is the same as in the preceding instrument, as is the method for subdividing the degrees. Another inner ring, designated by the letters **N K L M**, is suitable for investigating **Declinations** <note>The coordinate of a star measured in degrees north or south of the celestial equator.</note>, as before. It can be rotated within the Equatorial ring, which it crosses at right angles, so that at its points of contact it also indicates the equatorial points by means of suitable small plates placed near **S** and **M**. It also has its own sighting vanes near **Q** and **O** and two others not depicted here, which are to be understood as being on the opposite side. Divisions are also present, corresponding to the minutes in each quadrant. All those rings contained within the Meridian are covered on all sides with brass plates; this is done with such precision and polishing that you would say they were cast entirely from solid brass. The smooth Axis is also made of brass, as are the sighting vanes. The plumb line **R T** consists of a thin brass wire, as is our custom. If anything else has been omitted here, you will grasp it by looking at the illustration, just as with the previous ones.

These rings rest upon a support or pedestal made of solid steel, which is designated by **X T V Y Z** and the other letters placed between them. The upper semicircular part, **G W V**, continuously surrounds and supports the Meridian (which contains the other rings) from below; four brackets are added, just as in the previous instrument. The rest of the form of this pedestal is clear enough from the figure itself; some parts have been added for the sake of beauty beyond utility and strength, though these also provide support. Everything consists of solid steel, as was said before. The lowest circle **Z**, placed beneath these, is thicker and made of iron; it has screws at four equidistant locations. Small iron tools are placed under these screws on a square stone, so that by turning these screws, the whole instrument can be directed as required so the rings are analogous to the celestial ones. This is done with the help of the plumb line **B T**, which aligns the plane of the mechanical meridian with the celestial one and further arranges its position according to the **Elevation of the Pole** <note>The latitude of the observer, which determines the angle of the celestial pole above the horizon.</note>. Thus, it makes all the inner rings analogous to the celestial circles they emulate. The whole machine is quite robust in itself and not easily subject to warping, as it is made entirely of metal.

Its use is similar to the previous one, except that here the Equator can be rotated at will within the Meridian. Declinations are taken in the same way as before on the Declination ring, using the cylinder near the center **A** and the appropriate sighting vanes. Equatorial distances, or **Right Ascensions** <note>The celestial equivalent of longitude, measured eastward along the celestial equator from the vernal equinox.</note>, are taken on the transverse equatorial ring as before, except that here the steel ring that carries these may also have some use in this practice. There is also this added convenience: when a star is aligned at its proper right ascension, the point of the equator passing the Meridian indicates the **Right Ascension of the Midheaven** <note>The point of the celestial equator that is currently on the observer's meridian; original Latin: *ascensionem rectam medii Coeli*.</note>. Thus, the exact moment of time is also known, provided the exact position of the Sun is known, such as our Tables supply. The Equator can also be fixed to the Meridian, and thus the distance of any star from it can be taken by a sighting vane moved along it, and from this the moment of time can be known, as in the previous instrument (which I neglected to mention before). Thus, by these two armillary instruments, both Right Ascensions and Declinations can be conveniently measured from the heavens, along with the most precise moments of time, all of which are of the greatest use in astronomy. If anyone requires more on the construction and use of this instrument, let them consult the first volume of our *Astronomical Exercises* <note>Original: *Progymnasmatum Astronomicorum*; Tycho's major work on the reconstruction of astronomy.</note>, chapter two, from page 210 to page 252 complete. However, the greater and principal part of that information is sufficiently indicated here, along with certain other matters.

<summary>Tycho Brahe describes a more advanced, four-ringed Equatorial Armillary Sphere made of steel and brass, explaining its mechanical improvements for higher precision. The instrument allows astronomers to measure the coordinates of stars (declination and right ascension) and determine exact time more efficiently than previous models.</summary>

The author, Tycho Brahe, discusses the necessity of an astronomer's independence from political whim. # EXPLANATION OF THE CONSTRUCTION AND USE.

I will also add this: that these **Armillaries** <note>The armillary sphere is a model of objects in the sky, consisting of a spherical framework of rings.</note> can be taken apart by means of certain screws and then put back together again. The same can be done with the **steel pedestal** or support itself, so that they can be conveniently transported to other locations. This is a feature that should be implemented in all instruments, wherever it can be achieved without damaging them. 

For it is necessary for an astronomer to be a <term>citizen of the world<note>original Greek: κοσμοπολίτην (kosmopolitēn). This reflects Tycho's own life, as he eventually moved his entire practice from Denmark to Prague.</note></term>, so that, if necessity and use demand it, he is not so bound to any one region that he is not willing and able to migrate elsewhere and carry his instruments with him freely and gladly. Since there are very few who occupy themselves with these arduous disciplines and exercises, and one very rarely finds among politicians—those who wish to lead the administration of the State—any who are intensely delighted by these things and think it wise to foster and sustain them; instead, there are far more who, out of ignorance, are repelled by them and judge them useless. 

The student of **Divine Astronomy** ought not to be subject to such unskilled judgments, but should look down upon them from on high. He should consider nothing better than to keep watch over these studies, and not be moved at all by the insolence of others. When they become too troublesome, whether they be politicians or others, he should move himself and his possessions elsewhere. Indeed, he should learn to place these **celestial and sublime concerns**, which bear the **traces of immortality**, above even his native soil (which attracts all men with its sweetness and does not allow them to forget it). Rather, with a constant and persevering spirit, he should always remind himself in every turn of events that:

->*Everywhere the earth lies below, everywhere the sky lies open above,*<-
->*And every land is a homeland for the brave man.*<-

<summary>Tycho Brahe explains that his instruments are designed to be modular and portable, allowing the astronomer to remain mobile. He argues that a scientist must be a "citizen of the world," ready to move if political leaders fail to support the "divine" pursuit of astronomy.</summary>
<keywords>portability, astronomical instruments, steel pedestal, citizen of the world, patronage, scientific independence, Tycho Brahe, exile</keywords>

# GREAT EQUATORIAL ARMILLARIES,
# CONSISTING OF A CIRCLE
## AND A HALF.

<note>original Latin: *Armillae Aequatoriae Maximae*. An armillary sphere is a model of the objects in the sky, consisting of a spherical framework of rings, centered on Earth or the Sun, that represent lines of celestial longitude and latitude and other astronomically important features such as the equator.</note>

<note>The phrase "consisting of a circle and a half" (Latin: *sesquialtero circulo*) describes the instrument's specific geometry. Unlike a full sphere of many rings, this version uses one full equatorial ring and a semi-circular meridian ring to achieve high precision with less weight and mechanical complexity.</note>

<meta>catchword: EXPLANATION-</meta>

<summary>This page acts as the formal introduction and title for the Great Equatorial Armillary, a massive and precise instrument used by Tycho Brahe for mapping the stars. It marks the shift from the theoretical requirements of an astronomer to the practical details of his specialized equipment.</summary>

<keywords>equatorial armillary, astronomical instruments, Tycho Brahe, celestial equator, Renaissance science, observational astronomy</keywords>

<meta>Continuity: This page, marked with signature D 2, follows the title page for the Great Equatorial Armillary. This page provides the technical "Explanation of the Construction and Use," detailing the physical dimensions, materials, and mechanical adjustments of the instrument.</meta>

# EXPLANATION OF THE CONSTRUCTION AND USE.

**A**lthough the two machines of the equatorial <term>Armillaries</term> <note>original: *Armillarum*. An armillary is an astronomical instrument made of rings representing the great circles of the heavens.</note> previously depicted and explained (which also cost us a great deal) serve the use I mentioned with sufficient accuracy, I nevertheless later devised and saw to the construction of others requiring fewer components. Specifically, these use only a single, complete revolving <term>Armilla</term> and another fixed semi-circular one. By these two, everything I said regarding the previous ones is managed not only conveniently, but far more promptly and without the obstacle that the former ones present when a sighting must be made near the <term>Equatorial Armilla</term> itself. For then, its forward part blocks the view; just as in the <term>Armilla</term> used for <term>Declinations</term> <note>original: *Declinationum*. Declination is the astronomical equivalent of latitude, measuring a star's distance north or south of the celestial equator.</note>, sighting becomes difficult near the <term>Equator</term> itself, not to mention other impediments. These second versions have this further advantage: they can be made much larger. Therefore, the degrees with their divisions can be displayed even more distinctly, while still remaining suited for use in their stability and rotation—and this more easily than the previous ones. The form of these huge and extremely efficient <term>Armillaries</term> is what we show here, as far as it can be represented on a flat surface.

The letters **A** indicate the largest <term>Armilla</term>, which is reinforced everywhere with plates of <term>Orichalcum</term> <note>original: *Orichalcicis*. A golden-colored brass or bronze alloy highly valued for scientific instruments.</note> and has a diameter of 7 <term>cubits</term> <note>approximately 4.4 meters or 14.5 feet, based on the Danish cubit of the time.</note>. Its degrees are so large that individual minutes are subdivided into four parts. Its axis **B**, consisting of steel and perfectly smooth all around, is hollow inside so that it does not hinder use with its weight; it is three inches in diameter. Fixed around its middle is a smooth cylinder of <term>Orichalcum</term>, around which two brass rulers, **E** and **F**, rotate closely against the axis on both sides. Near **F**, at the surface of the <term>Armilla</term>, they have <term>pinnacidia</term> <note>original: *pinnacidia*. Sighting vanes used to align the instrument with a star.</note> with <term>Dioptra</term> <note>original: *Dioptra*. Slits or apertures used for precise sighting.</note> of gaps parallel to the cylinder itself. The remaining spaces you see at the letters **G** are crossbeams or floorings holding and binding the whole <term>Armilla</term> together, so that it is not moved from its roundness and flatness because of its great size.

Further down at **D**, the axis ends in a smooth point made of square steel-iron, having a similar pointed hole which, by means of screws placed on either side, can be pushed a little in whichever direction is desired. Upon this rests that continuous smooth axis, which rotates the whole <term>Armilla</term> as one. The iron base beneath this is fixed into a large and long stone column of 4 <term>cubits</term>, two of which stand on a firm foundation below the ground; the other two above have on their front part a likeness of Atlas, King of Mauritania **I**, artificially carved into the same stone. Atlas touches with his crown a stone globe **H**, placed on top of the column for the sake of ornament. The uppermost part of the axis rests on another iron support at **C**, which is forked at the bottom near **K** and passes into two branches, each of which is strongly fixed to a large, smooth column of solid stone. These columns are spaced far enough apart to provide an entrance gate to the instrument; they are indicated by **M** and **L**.

At the top near **C** is a Screw <note>original: *Cochlea*.</note>, which drives a transverse iron upon which the axis rests, so that it corresponds to the plane of the Meridian. It is also raised by another method both there and below at **D**, and is moved firmly until it corresponds exactly to the <term>Axis Mundi</term> <note>original: *axi Mundi*. The Earth's axis projected into the heavens, around which the stars appear to rotate.</note>; thus it is secured. The other semi-circular armilla is indicated by the letters **C** <note>The text likely refers to the secondary structure near the primary ring.</note>; it is held immovably in the plane of the <term>Equator</term> by eight small stone columns indicated by **P**, with as many iron clamps existing at **Q**, which hold and direct it until they mark out an exact semicircle around the middle of the axis and, moreover, incline themselves constantly according to the plane of the <term>Equator</term>. It is also clad everywhere in <term>Orichalcum</term> and has a diameter of 9 <term>cubits</term>; therefore, it is capable of even more minute subdivisions than the <term>Armilla</term> itself. Because of this, this semi-circle is two <term>cubits</term> wider than the full <term>Armilla</term> so that one can pass conveniently between the two as needed. And by stone stairs outside the armilla (which are not shown here) at **N**, one ascends and descends along the path of the <term>Armilla</term> so that the observer's eye may be conveniently applied to it. Inside, there are other round stairs indicated by the letters **T**, by which one ascends or descends around the full <term>Armilla</term> during observation, according to whether the position of the star to be noted is higher or lower.

Near both letters **N** appears a wall which, surrounding the entire instrument, creates a kind of small tower and half-crypt encircling this whole machine. It has a roof above that can be opened in whichever direction is desired during observation and closed afterward, so that this precious machine is safe from the harm of air and rain.

Its use is as follows: on the revolving circular Armilla, <term>Declinations</term> are taken by moving the <term>Dioptra</term> at **F** and sighting the star on both sides near the cylinder **E**. For in this way, the sought-after declination is numbered at **E**. This can be done in two ways, namely with both diopters, by rotating the <term>Armilla</term> so that the other part is also brought to the eye; thus the declination of the same star is checked twice, and it ought to be the same on both sides. On the other semi-circular one, let its sighting vanes be moved

It explains how the instrument's massive size allows for extreme precision in measuring stellar declination while protecting the mechanism from the elements with a retractable roof.</summary>

## EXPLANATION OF THE CONSTRUCTION AND USE.

...[the] <term>sights</term> <note>original: *pinnacidia*. These are adjustable sighting vanes or plates with slits used to align the instrument with a celestial body.</note> next to **R**, until the Star to be observed is seen through those slits around **Axis B** on both sides. For then the sight itself shows its equatorial distance from the <term>Meridian</term> <note>The imaginary north-south line in the sky that passes directly overhead.</note> on that same semicircular <term>armilla</term> <note>original: *armilla*. A ring or hoop forming part of an astronomical instrument.</note>, to be counted either toward the east or the west. From this, the exact moment of time can be known with great precision from the known location of the Star and the Sun.

But if the <term>Right Ascension</term> <note>original: *ascensio Recta*. The celestial equivalent of longitude, measuring a star's position eastward along the celestial equator.</note> of any Star is to be sought, one observer should direct one of the sights toward a star that has a known position; another observer, however, directs the remaining sight in the same way toward the other star whose right ascension is sought. This is done so that both stars are seen at the exact same instant around the same axis through the slits of the sights. For the arc thus intercepted between each sight then reveals their <term>ascensional difference</term> <note>original: *differentiam ascensionalem*. The angular distance between the two stars' positions along the celestial equator.</note>, from which the sought right ascension is obtained, just as was indicated in the preceding sections.

If anyone requires more, let them consult our first book of *Astronomical Letters* <note>original: *Epistolarum Astronomicarum*. This refers to Tycho Brahe's 1596 work, *Astronomiae Instauratae Mechanica*, where he published his correspondence and detailed instrument descriptions.</note>, page 246 and the two following pages, concerning the construction and use of this most ample instrument. There, several things are explained more extensively which I have omitted here for the sake of brevity. Furthermore, the structure as well as the use can be more readily grasped from the accompanying illustration itself—if it is considered more carefully—than through long-winded verbal descriptions.

->[Decorative woodcut featuring a central cherub amidst acanthus leaf scrolls and hanging plumb-bobs]<-

# THE BIPARTITE ARC
## FOR SMALLER DISTANCES OF THE STARS
### for which it serves.

...[the] <term>sights</term> <note>original: *pinnacidia*. These are adjustable sighting vanes or plates with slits used to align the instrument with a celestial body.</note> next to **R**, until the Star to be observed is seen through those slits around **Axis B** on both sides. For then the sight itself shows its equatorial distance from the <term>Meridian</term> <note>The imaginary north-south line in the sky that passes directly overhead.</note> on that same semicircular <term>armilla</term> <note>original: *armilla*. A ring or hoop forming part of an astronomical instrument.</note>, to be counted either toward the east or the west. From this, the exact moment of time can be known with great precision from the known location of the Star and the Sun.

But if the <term>Right Ascension</term> <note>original: *ascensio Recta*. The celestial equivalent of longitude, measuring a star's position eastward along the celestial equator.</note> of any Star is to be sought, one observer should direct one of the sights toward a star that has a known position; another observer, however, directs the remaining sight in the same way toward the other star whose right ascension is sought. This is done so that both stars are seen at the exact same instant around the same axis through the slits of the sights. For the arc thus intercepted between each sight then reveals their <term>ascensional difference</term> <note>original: *differentiam ascensionalem*. The angular distance between the two stars measured along the celestial equator.</note>—which is the difference in their positions along the path of the sky's rotation.

# EXPLANATION OF THE CONSTRUCTION AND USE.

We have already dealt with those astronomical instruments that investigate the altitudes and <term>azimuths</term> <note>The horizontal angle of a celestial body measured clockwise from the North or South point of the horizon.</note> of the stars in various ways, as well as those that provide longitudes, latitudes, and furthermore, <term>Declinations</term> <note>The celestial equivalent of latitude, measuring a star's distance north or south of the celestial equator.</note> and <term>Right Ascensions</term> <note>The celestial equivalent of longitude, measuring a star's position eastward along the celestial equator.</note>. It remains for us to take account of those which investigate the <term>intervals</term> <note>original: *intercapedines*. The angular distance or "gap" between two celestial objects.</note> between stars, and afterward, we shall deal with several others of a mixed nature. 

As far as the distances of the stars are concerned: in years past, an <term>Astronomical Radius</term> <note>original: *Radius Astronomicus*. Also known as a cross-staff, this was a common 16th-century instrument consisting of a graduated rod with a sliding cross-piece.</note> was prepared by certain people; it is handy enough in itself and easy to transport, and at the same time, it is quite well-suited for geometric demonstrations in theory. But in actual practice and in its ultimate application, it does not provide what it promises at the outset. This is for various reasons which I will refrain from listing here; perhaps elsewhere I shall deal sufficiently with those reasons and the correction of the instrument, as far as is possible. 

In the meantime, I shall speak here of certain other instruments which were devised by me and have been used by me until now, which are suitable for use and provide the required certainty. And so, the first one, which provides an index for smaller distances within a twelfth part of a circle—or one <term>sign</term> <note>In astronomy, a "sign" of the zodiac corresponds to 30 degrees, which is one-twelfth of the 360-degree circle.</note>, as they call it—is as the depicted figure shows.

The first part is an oblong rod starting from **F** and ending at the cross-bar **B A**: its length is 4 <term>cubits</term> <note>A traditional unit of length; in this context, it likely refers to a measurement of about 18 to 22 inches per cubit.</note>. It has on the said cross-bar two cylinders near **A** and **B**, equidistant from the common joint. From their centers, two arcs, **F C** and **F D**, are drawn below from point **F**. Each of these, beyond the parallel of half of **A B** counted from **F** on both sides (which ought to have been represented by **K I**, though the figure does not indicate it sufficiently), shows the start of the numbering on the arc **K C** or **K D**, and makes that numbering fifteen degrees on both sides. The remaining parts through **C G** and **D H**, and those interspersed around **L** and shown thereafter, are added for the sake of the framework and support, and also for decoration. 

This instrument rests on a smooth and thick support, which is shown by **T R S Q**. In the middle, where the balance point is, there is a square hole near **E**, in which it is placed on its own support by a square bolt. The support is arranged such that near **S** and **T**, and also **Q** and **R**, there are round iron plates. These revolve on a smooth underlying cylinder, which is at least a <term>span</term> <note>original: *spithama*. An ancient unit of length, usually the distance from the tip of the thumb to the tip of the little finger when the hand is fully extended.</note> in diameter; four small lead weights hold it tenaciously from four sides so that it may easily stay still in any position. 

By this method, the instrument itself rotates only upward and downward. If, however, it must be tilted or reclined to the side, that is done by moving the semicircle **O N M** and tightening the screw near **N**. In this way, the whole instrument turns as one. It can also be revolved through the channel **P V** (which acts like a round axle) in every direction toward all parts of the sky. That channel stands upon another pedestal signified by **V** and the letter **X**, in which it is also turned around in every direction with the whole instrument, as need requires, so that it can be directed toward any plane of the stars, just as the logic of the observation demands. 

The inner axis of the same channel, along with the channel itself and thus the instrument resting on it, can be raised higher in the underlying pedestal and thus fixed immovably as use requires. For a solid quadrilateral wooden beam descends inside the quadrilateral shape of the pedestal; this beam can be carried upward and let down, and fixed wherever one pleases. Thus the instrument can be raised or lowered at once, depending on whether the stars whose interval is to be investigated are higher or lower in the heavens. On the instrument itself, as far as the <term>limbs</term> <note>The graduated edges or scales of the instrument.</note> where the divisions are, as well as on the cross-bar and the cylinders, everything is made of <term>brass</term> <note>original: *Orichalcica*. A golden-colored alloy, likely brass or a similar copper-based metal favored for precision instruments.</note>. The frameworks and supports are of skillfully crafted ironwork. The rest is of wood, cleverly assembled and reinforced with small metal plates where necessary, so that the whole instrument is firm and suitable for observations.

Its use (as I said) is in conveniently measuring smaller distances; since in the <term>sextant</term> <note>An instrument with an arc of 60 degrees (one-sixth of a circle). Tycho notes here that it is physically difficult for two people to use a sextant for very small angles.</note> (of which more later), which also requires two observers, this cannot be performed aptly because of the proximity of the heads, which requires a suitable space—although we have also remedied this inconvenience by another means, of which we shall speak hereafter. 

But so that the intervals of the stars which do not exceed a twelfth part of the circle may be marked by this <term>instrument</term> <note>original: *Organo*.</note>, let one observer fix a <term>sighting vane</term> <note>original: *pinnacidium*. A small plate with a slit or hole used for sighting a star.</note> at **K** or **I**, as may be most convenient. Let the other observer pull the one that is not fixed along the outer limb until they both see the two stars they are investigating around cylinders **B** and **A** on both sides, each person looking toward his own cylinder in the same part. The distance that was sought must be counted on the lower limb in degrees and <term>transverse minutes</term> <note>A system of diagonal lines or dots used by Tycho to allow for much more precise readings than standard degree marks.</note> next to the appropriate sighting vane. If, however, both observers move their sights, an even larger interval can be established in the same way, provided it does not exceed the prescribed limit. It follows, therefore, that where both observers have placed each sighting vane next to the very beginning of the divisions (which is at **K** and **I**), and thus [look] through the cy-

<meta>The text cuts off mid-word "cylindros" (cylinders). The catchword "lindros" on the following page will complete it.</meta>

<summary>This page describes the construction and mechanical adjustments of the Bipartite Arc, a specialized instrument for measuring small angular distances between stars. It explains the mounting system that allows for rotation, tilting, and height adjustment, and notes that its design avoids the physical crowding of observers that occurs with a standard sextant.</summary>

<sig>D 4v</sig>

<meta>Catchword: SEX-</meta>

# EXPLANATION OF THE CONSTRUCTION AND USE.

...the front cylinders A and B establish the <term>collimation</term> <note>The process of aligning the optical axis of an instrument or the act of sighting a target.</note>, so that each one points toward its own target, arranged from the same side of the long ruler, so that they then sight the same star; provided that they direct the view in a parallel manner. And from this, the accuracy of the instrument can be tested, whether it is functioning correctly or not. Even though the support or <term>pedestal</term> <note>original: *fulcrum sive pedestale*</note> of this instrument is convenient enough and suited for the required uses; nevertheless, another method, which involves a globular body (of which we will speak later in the explanation of the Sextant), is even more adaptable and allows the instrument to be directed more quickly to the <term>plane</term> <note>The flat geometric surface in which two stars and the observer's eye all lie.</note> of any stars. But variety in these matters not only brings delight but also has its own utility.

With this instrument, I investigated primarily the distances of the stars in Cassiopeia from one another, which are set forth in the fourth chapter of our *Progymnasmata* <note>original: *Astronomiae Instaurandae Progymnasmata*. This was Tycho's major work, the "Introduction to the Restoration of Astronomy," which detailed his observations of the 1572 supernova.</note> on the restoration of astronomy, page 145 and following. I compared these with the distances discovered earlier, while the <term>new star</term> <note>original: *nova stella*. This refers to the famous supernova that appeared in Cassiopeia in 1572, which proved that the heavens could change.</note> still lasted—for the sake of which these distances were primarily recorded—and I found them to be accurate. If anyone has any instrument with which they think they can investigate the distances of the constellations, let them make a trial on these same stars of Cassiopeia, to see if they find the same gap between them that we have noted in the cited place, down to the <term>minute</term> <note>A unit of angular measurement equal to 1/60th of a degree.</note> itself, and even half a minute. Otherwise, let them know that the instrument they use, and their method of observing, is by no means correctly established.

# EXPLANATION OF THE CONSTRUCTION AND USE.

...the front cylinders A and B establish the <term>collimation</term> <note>The process of aligning the optical axis of an instrument or the act of sighting a target.</note>, so that each one points toward its own target, arranged from the same side of the long ruler, so that they then sight the same star; provided that they direct the view in a parallel manner. But variety in these matters not only brings delight but also has its own utility.

With this instrument, I investigated primarily the distances of the stars in Cassiopeia from one another, which are set forth in the fourth chapter of our *Progymnasmata* <note>original: *Astronomiae Instaurandae Progymnasmata*. This was Tycho's major work, the "Introduction to the Restoration of Astronomy," which detailed his observations of the 1572 supernova.</note> on the restoration of astronomy, page 145 and following. I compared these with the distances discovered earlier, while the <term>new star</term> <note>original: *nova stella*. This refers to the famous supernova that appeared in Cassiopeia in 1572. Tycho’s measurements of its distance proved it was in the "heavens" beyond the planets, shattering the Aristotelian idea that the heavens were perfect and unchanging.</note> was still visible...

## EXPLANATION OF THE CONSTRUCTION AND USE.

**I** NOW come to the explanation of the astronomical sextant, insofar as it is particularly suitable for examining the distances between stars in the heavens. Since I had discovered through many years of experience that the <term>Astronomical Radius</term> <note>Also known as a cross-staff, a simpler instrument for measuring angles which Tycho found insufficiently precise.</note> was by no means sufficient for this task with the necessary precision and certainty, driven by necessity, I devised certain sextants of various forms which would perform this exactly without any error. Among these, those reflecting the composition you see here seemed most convenient to me. Therefore, I had three of this kind constructed, differing only slightly in their framing. Its explanation is as follows: The frame contained within **A B C** represents the sextant itself with its support structure; its center at **A** has a round <term>Cylinder</term>. The circumference **C B**, containing a sixth part of a circle <note>60 degrees, which is why the instrument is called a "sextant."</note>, is removed from the center by the sides **A C** and **A B** at a distance of four cubits. To this, the Ruler **A D** is joined from the center itself, with its <term>sighting vane</term> <note>original: *pinnacidium*; a small plate with slits used for sighting.</note> near **D**, which has slits parallel to the Cylinder resting at **A**. It can be moved back and forth along the circumference and fixed wherever one pleases. It also has another Cylinder resting near **F**, equal to the first; for which another sighting vane near **G** is intended, to be added or removed as use requires. The remaining intermediate parts are joined together for the sake of stability, as appears here. Near **E**, a quadrangular hole passes through, which is made at the center of gravity of the entire instrument, so that by its benefit the sextant can be placed upon the lower support and removed once the observation is completed. Its <term>Limb</term> <note>The graduated outer edge of the instrument.</note> near the circumference, which has <term>transversal divisions</term> <note>Tycho's innovative method of using diagonal lines to allow for the reading of very small fractions of a degree, such as single minutes of arc.</note> and can distinguish single minutes in four parts, is made entirely of brass, as are the cylinders and sighting vanes. The remaining parts are indeed wooden, but so skillfully composed and glued together from various pieces—as we have already indicated in the explanation of the armillaries—that they cannot warp or be altered by changes in the air; they are further covered with a linen cloth of an oil-vined color <note>Likely a dark, protective varnish or oil-based paint intended to stabilize the wood against humidity.</note>. For if all its parts were covered with metal plates, the instrument would be rendered unmanageable by excessive weight. Indeed, it would harm itself by its own heaviness in maintaining the proper plane.

The pedestal or support on which it rests is as follows: Near **I** is a certain Globe, encased everywhere with copper plates and perfectly round, having a diameter a little more than two feet. Around it are four semicircular iron tools, grasping a certain round iron <term>Armilla</term> <note>A ring or hoop.</note> crosswise in eight places around the middle of the Globe. All these are distant from the surface of the Globe on the inside by as much as the thickness of one finger, and a little more. For in those places where these iron tools contain that ring, there are eight iron screws which drive a round plate (cut back into an oblong shape so that it functions like a spring) against the Globe, so that it is gripped tenaciously by them and remains firm however it is rotated. These semicircular iron tools also have such a spring on the inside, in which the Globe usefully rests, which is the ninth one. On the outside, however, it is attached by a certain square iron to a certain hollow square wooden column; this column passes through the lower pedestal, which is seen around the letters **K**, so that it can be raised and lowered within it and fixed in a certain place as the convenience of the observation requires. For in taking distances at lower inclinations, the column must be lowered. In higher ones, it must be raised, so that the <term>sightings</term> <note>original: *collimationes*</note> can be completed without trouble. Furthermore, the sextant itself is placed above near the hole **E**, as we have said, upon this Globe which lies beneath it, and with the Globe it is most conveniently directed, tilted into the plane of any two stars. And so that they remain there more firmly without wobbling until the observation is finished, two long and smooth rods **M** and **N O**, having a pointed iron tip below near **M** and **O**, are added so that they rest more firmly on the floor; these cross each other above near **P**. One of the observers holds them there by hand and guides them as he wishes.

The use of the instrument requires two observers; one of whom, with his eye applied to the fixed sighting vane near **C**, sees any star through its slits around Cylinder **A**, and thus holds the sextant unmoved by the aid of the rods near **P**. While the plane of the sextant itself lies in the plane of the two stars whose distance one wishes to measure; a task which the Globe performs for it, on which it is rotated for a short time until both those stars are seen along its flat surface. Then the other observer moves the Ruler with its sighting vane at **D**, until he also sights the other star through the slits there on both sides of Cylinder **A**. This must be attempted until both observers have sighted their respective stars at one and same time; then, a signal being given by voice, they must stop, and the circumference of the sextant on its globular support is to be drawn back to the eye of each observer, so that he can conveniently count the distance **C D** intercepted by the sighting vanes, which they were seeking. Furthermore, so that even smaller distances with this same...

# EXPLANATION OF THE CONSTRUCTION AND USE.

...so that they might be taken conveniently by the sextant; we have devised this innovation: that with another <term>Cylinder</term> <note>A vertical post used as a sighting reference.</note> placed at **F**, and another <term>sighting vane</term> <note>original: *pinnacidium*; a small plate with slits used for aligning the eye with a target.</note> near **G**, these two being equidistant from the line **A E** passing from the Center to the middle of the circumference; and with one observer looking through this sighting vane and this Cylinder; while the other attends to the sighting vane at **D** and the Cylinder at **A**, the Distance of the stars from the middle of the circumference near the thirtieth Degree up to the sighting vane **D** is to be counted; and thus, however small the distance may be, nothing prevents both observers from being able to obtain it. If you desire more regarding this Instrument, consult the first Volume of my *Astronomical Exercises* <note>original: *Progymnasmatum Astronomicorum*; Tycho’s massive work on the restoration of astronomy, published posthumously in 1602 but printed earlier.</note>, on page 247 and the three following. There you will find these things explained more broadly: indeed, in recording the distances of the fixed stars, which we set forth systematically <note>original: *Canonicè*</note> in the second chapter there, and which we mapped out precisely according to the celestial standard, its use was very frequent. For concerning this Instrument, I can testify that it provides the intervals between the stars with such certainty and precision, and so quickly and easily, that nothing in it can be found wanting. This is evident even from this: if anyone considers more deeply the Distances of the stars indicated and applied to use in the aforementioned book from page 195 up to 203: how, namely, having also applied the <term>Declination</term> <note>The angular distance of a star north or south of the celestial equator.</note> of the stars, it most exactly encompasses and closes the whole circuit of the circle in the <term>Ascensional differences</term> <note>A term in spherical trigonometry used to convert between coordinates; it relates to the time it takes for a star to rise compared to a point on the equator.</note> constructed from here by the help of Triangles,

...so that they might be taken conveniently by the sextant; we have devised this innovation: that with another <term>Cylinder</term> <note>A vertical post used as a sighting reference.</note> placed at **F**, and another <term>sighting vane</term> <note>original: *pinnacidium*; a small plate with slits used for aligning the eye with a target.</note> near **G**, these two being equidistant from the line **A E** passing from the Center to the middle of the circumference; and with one observer looking through this sighting vane and this Cylinder; while the other attends to the sighting vane at **D** and the Cylinder at **A**, the Distance of the stars from the middle of the circumference near the thirtieth Degree up to the sighting vane **D** is to be counted; and thus, however small the distance may be, nothing prevents both observers from being able to obtain it. If you desire more regarding this Instrument, consult the first Volume of my *Astronomical Exercises* <note>original: *Progymnasmatum Astronomicorum*; Tycho’s massive work on the restoration of astronomy, published posthumously in 1602 but printed earlier.</note>, on page 247 and the three following. There you will find these things explained more broadly: indeed, in recording the distances of the fixed stars, which we set forth systematically <note>original: *Canonicè*; following a strict rule or standard of accuracy.</note> in the second chapter there, and which we mapped out precisely according to the celestial standard, its use was very frequent. For concerning this Instrument, I can testify that it provides the intervals between the stars with such certainty and precision, and so quickly and easily, that nothing in it can be found wanting. This is evident even from this: if anyone considers how accurately the positions of the stars are determined when the sightings are performed with the utmost care...

<meta>This page continues the description of the steel sextant. The previous page discussed the general framing of the instrument; this section focuses on the detailed mechanical explanation of its operation and the adjustable pedestal.</meta>

# EXPLANATION OF THE CONSTRUCTION AND USE.

I had also previously constructed another sextant of solid **Steel** with great durability, and so arranged it that a single Observer, with its mediation, might scrutinize the distances of the Stars quite conveniently. This entire Instrument can be disassembled by means of suitable <term>screws</term> <note>original: *cochleis*; literally "snail-shells," the standard Latin term for screws in this period.</note>, both the sextant itself and its support, and put back together again, so that it might be transported in a crate to other regions where there is a need; just as was done by us about 24 years ago in our youth, when we were traveling through Germany. Its construction is evident from the figure itself; we shall add a fuller explanation here, repeated in summary from the second book of our *Progymnasmata* <note>Tycho’s *Astronomiae Instauratae Progymnasmata*, his foundational work on the "new astronomy."</note>, where near the end this same Instrument is both illustrated and explained.

The sextant itself is represented by **A B C D**, and its center is at **A**, whose Arc **B D** is divided into sixty Degrees and the individual minutes of these, in the manner familiar to us by <term>transversal lines</term> <note>A method of dividing a scale using diagonal lines to achieve higher precision than standard tick marks.</note>. The Arms of the Instrument **A B** and **B C** are joined at **A** around a round pin, so that it can be widened or narrowed like a compass; and this is done by the screw indicated by the letters **G H**. Although this screw is straight, it is nevertheless attached to the Arms where it joins them by <term>movable nuts</term> <note>original: *matricibus*; literally "matrices," referring here to the threaded female parts of the screw mechanism.</note> on all sides, so that it serves the circular motion of the Instrument nonetheless. It also has two handles marked with the letters **I K**, by which the Instrument is held during the sighting. **B** and **C** are two <term>sighting vanes</term> <note>original: *pinnacidia*; small plates with slits or holes used for aligning the eye with a celestial target.</note>, through which the eye, directed toward **O** near the Center, observes two stars at once. By the benefit of the screw **G H**, widening or narrowing the angle **B A C**, the Arc **B C** shows the sought distance—except that the <term>Parallax of the Instrument</term> <note>An error caused by the observer's eye not being exactly at the geometric center of the instrument's arc.</note> arising from this must be subtracted. However, I later found another means so that the sighting occurs in parallel, no differently than in other <term>sights and vanes</term> <note>original: *dioptris & pinnacidiis*.</note>; and thus there will be no need for the subtraction of Parallax. But because this arrangement of sights is not represented here, we have decided to explain it elsewhere, where this same sextant is marked with them.

Furthermore, it has a pedestal, on which the sextant is directed even more conveniently toward any plane. Each Arm of this sextant, **A B** and **A C**, is about three <term>cubits</term> <note>A unit of measure roughly 18 inches or 45 centimeters; the arms were thus about 4.5 feet long.</note> long, to which the <term>chord</term> <note>original: *subtensa*; the straight line connecting the ends of the arc.</note> of its Arc **B D** is equal. This entire sextant is skillfully fashioned from solid steel, except that the sighting vanes are made of **Brass** <note>original: *Orichalcica*; an alloy of copper and zinc prized for its durability and resistance to rust.</note>; and it rests upon a certain support, so that it might be more fit for use and easier to handle during observation.

The support and pedestal are arranged in this manner: First, it stands firmly on the Ground by a cross-shaped base **V X Y Z**. Next to this is a sort of hollow leg at **T**, so that a certain long screw at **Q** may descend into it. This screw is raised or lowered as needed by another screw called a "perpetual screw" <note>An early term for a worm gear or a screw that stays in a fixed position while moving another part.</note> enclosed within **P**. The sextant itself moves with it. Both screws consist of solid iron metal, as does the entire upper part of the support. A polished iron rod **A O**, hollow on the inside, is marked around another oblong axis by a <term>capital</term> <note>original: *capitello*; the top or head of the column.</note> appearing there on the screw **Q**. From this, a certain point emerges, to which another long iron piece shown by the letters **L M** is attached; this carries the sextant itself, fixed to it around **L**, which can be extended through its entire length.

That capital is four-sided and is so constructed on the inside with the mechanism of rollers that when its screw is turned at **N**, then that point, through the internal roller, inclines the oblong iron **L M** together with the whole sextant attached to it laterally from side to side to the position of the two stars. By the Arc **S R** as well, the center placed at **A** is raised and lowered together with the sextant itself, inserted into the polished iron **O H** (which was mentioned) by a special joint around **S**, according as the altitude or declination of the star requires. It is fixed as one pleases in that same arc by a screw added on the other side near **9**. But all these things are better perceived by ocular inspection and handling.

With this Instrument, which I saw to the making of many years ago, I scrutinized the intervals of the Stars. Its greatest use was in the Observations of the **Comet of the year 1577**. For this reason, in the book which we wrote clearly concerning that same comet, near the end (as I said), this illustrated sextant is described more fully. If anyone wishes, let them read more about it there from page 459 to page 461.

Its use is for taking the distances of stars within the sixth part of a Circle <note>A sextant covers 60 degrees, which is one-sixth of a 360-degree circle.</note>, and that by only one observer, who, applying the eye to the Center **A**, and with the whole sextant arranged in their plane by the screws of the support, turns the screw **G H** in either direction. Thus he widens or narrows the angle **B A C** until both stars are aligned most exactly within the sighting vanes **B C**. Then the division of the arc **B D** will show the distance that was sought. From the beginning, its circumference had not only the common division, but also the <term>Nonnian</term> <note>A complex scale division system designed by Pedro Nunes; Tycho eventually found it less practical than his own transversal method.</note> one. But when I was taught by experience itself that the other method by transversal points is much more convenient and accurate, I used those later, setting the others aside. I also made the circumference of the sextant out of **Brass**, so that it would not be liable to rust as Steel is.

<summary>Tycho Brahe describes a portable, solid steel sextant designed for a single observer to measure stellar distances. He details its complex screw-driven pedestal and notes its significant role in observing the Great Comet of 1577.</summary>

# EXPLANATION OF THE CONSTRUCTION AND USE.

...and I completed the distribution of the scale upon it in my usual manner, by means of ten points placed transversally across each sixth part of a degree <note>This refers to Tycho’s method of transversal mapping, allowing him to read measurements down to the minute of an arc—a level of precision previously unattainable.</note>. And thus, its use was more convenient. As for the purpose to which the distances of the stars in astronomical matters pertain, let that be sought elsewhere, both in our own writings and those of other authors. For their usefulness is very widely apparent; and the observations of the said Comet, reviewed in the second chapter of the book named above—which were made with this very sextant—provide examples of such things, which in the following chapter are brought to their intended conclusions through the calculations of <term>Spherical Triangles</term> <note>The mathematical method used to calculate positions on the surface of a sphere, essential for mapping the heavens.</note>.

When Paul Wittich, the distinguished mathematician from Wrocław <note>original: *Vratislaviensis*</note>, had seen this sextant at my home in the year 1580, he later revealed it at Kassel to the most Illustrious Prince WILLIAM, Landgrave of Hesse, etc., of most praised memory. The Landgrave soon took care to have such an instrument made by his own skillful <term>mechanician</term> <note>original: *avtomatopæum*; literally a maker of automata or complex clockwork.</note>, with certain things altered, either as Wittich remembered them well enough, or as seemed best to them otherwise. For in place of the screw **G**, they attached a chain, through which the angle of the Instrument is widened or narrowed. They retained the same form of division for the scale which Wittich had learned from me. Afterward, others also imitated this construction; and some even claimed the invention for themselves, as has happened with most of my other inventions. Indeed, the proof of this Instrument’s excellence is that the distances measured with it at Kassel agree, within a single minute and its half, with those that we performed in Denmark with our own 
->sextants: as is clear from the first volume<-
->of our Astronomical<-
->Letters.<-

It is "similar to the previous one" (the sextant) because it operates on shared geometric principles of arcs and angles.</note>

<meta>This page continues the description of astronomical instruments, specifically focusing on a portable wooden sextant used for measuring the distances between stars. It follows the introductory heading from the previous page's catchword.</meta>

# EXPLANATION OF THE CONSTRUCTION AND USE.

I had already <gloss>prepared</gloss> <note>original: "PARATAM"</note> a similar instrument some time ago while I was staying in my youth in Augsburg <note>original: "Augustæ Vindelicorum"</note> in Upper Germany. That one, however, was made only of wood and lacked a supporting base, though it was otherwise manageable because of its lightness. When I departed from there, I left it as a gift for Paul Hainzel, the Consul of that most noble Republic, a man of the fondest memory and a great lover of these studies. Later, upon my return to my homeland, when that new and wonderfully admiring star <note>This refers to the Supernova of 1572, which Tycho observed in the constellation Cassiopeia</note> suddenly shone forth from the heavens, I saw to it that another instrument of this kind was made for me, in order to precisely denote its distances from the neighboring stars of Cassiopeia. You have an image of it here. Its arc, upon which the divisions are marked, is understood by **B C**. This arc is at least a twelfth part of a circle, or 30 degrees; this was sufficient for the smaller distances, such as those we have discussed, and its length would not be heavy or troublesome. Nevertheless, other arcs, either larger or smaller, can be applied to it as one pleases. The rulers **A B** and **A C** are joined at the center **A**, where they can rotate against each other, and they hold the circumference at **B** and **C**. They have a length of 4 cubits <note>approx. 6 feet or 1.8 meters</note>, a width of three digits, and a thickness of two. They are four-sided and made of very dry walnut wood, which is less liable to change due to air quality than other woods. Where the rulers are coupled at center **A**, they are reinforced with a metal bolt, a bore-hole, and the entire joint is strengthened with brass plates. The arc itself is solid metal: not too thick or wide, however, lest it be harmful due to its weight; this also prevents the obstruction of warping to which wooden material is subject. The sights <term>pinnacidia<gloss>small brass plates with viewing slits used for alignment</gloss></term> at **B** and **C** are also made of brass <note>original: "Orichalcica"</note>, and so adapted that their inner side corresponds exactly with the inner surface of the rulers. On the other, lower side, there is a screw by which the movable sight is fixed once the observation is completed, until the numbering on the scale <note>original: "limbo"</note> is recorded. Furthermore, another long screw exists near **D E**, by which the angle of the instrument **B A C** is contracted or widened as the distance to be measured requires. And although this screw moves in a straight line, it can nevertheless suffice for the circular path around **D E** above the center **A**, since on the other side, where it has the nuts, it is mounted on small round rotating pins, as we have also suggested previously. Moreover, within this screw toward the center, and even closer to the eye, is a certain circumference **G L H**, fixed to the first ruler at **G** and passing through the other at **L**. Its function is to keep both rulers in their proper plane, lest they be pulled apart somewhat due to their length. The angle of the instrument can also be fixed on this same arc from the back. It is placed at a distance from the center roughly one-third the length of the rulers, so that by applying the other hand there, the motion of the rulers can be conveniently controlled and stabilized. It also has two handles on the other side, which could not be represented here. I had not yet constructed a pedestal for it then; but since the instrument was not too heavy, it could be turned and held in the planes of any stars without much difficulty upon some transverse support firmly placed below, until the observation was completed.

The use of it is just as in the previous one: with the eye applied at **I**, the handle **F** is turned, and together with the rotation of the screw **D E**, the angle of the instrument **B A C** is contracted or widened until both stars whose distance we seek are seen precisely and centrally within the sights **B** and **C**. For then the arc intercepted by the sights and the rulers grants the observer his wish. But since the center of sight cannot be applied to the center of the instrument from which the divisions proceed—because the solid part from **A** to **I** prevents it—it is therefore necessary that the distances measured with the eye placed eccentrically <note>The eye is not at the exact geometric pivot point of the device</note> and numbered on the arc are slightly larger than those which exist in the sky itself.

To remedy this inconvenience, I constructed a certain table and marked it on the other side of the instrument so it would always be ready. This table provided the <term>parallax<gloss>the difference in apparent position of an object viewed along two different lines of sight</gloss></term> of the instrument—namely, how much must be subtracted for each degree of observation made, so that the true distance, consistent with the heavens, would result. This is exactly the same result that would come from the center of the instrument if the center of sight were united with it. The method by which this table was prepared, using the principles of plane triangles, is demonstrated by us in the first volume of our *Introduction to the Renewal of Astronomy* <note>original: "Progymnasmatis Instaurationis Astronomicæ"</note>, page 342, in the fourth chapter. That book, from page 335 to 343, provides a clear description of this instrument. I refer the reader who is eager to know more about this to that work.

## EXPLANATION OF THE CONSTRUCTION AND USE.

...I refer the reader. He will also find there, a little further on, the distances of the **New Star** <note>This refers to the supernova of 1572 in Cassiopeia, which Tycho famously proved was a distant celestial body rather than an atmospheric phenomenon.</note> from the closer stars of **Cassiopeia** and certain others, as well as the distances of the fixed stars from one another, measured most precisely with the same instrument and put into practice. For I had no other instrument suited to this task at hand at that time: as I had recently returned home from my travels in Germany and was then occupied with chemical <note>original: "Pyronomicis", literally "fire-management," referring to Tycho's early work in alchemy and chemistry</note> exercises rather than astronomical ones.

Although this instrument is indeed not as excellent as those which I devised later over several successive years and had constructed with great effort and expense (and which were therefore later set aside by me as obsolete), nevertheless, such as it is, since it is not inconvenient to use, I thought it should be illustrated and explained here along with the others. I do this so that I might also take into account those who do not have the means to build those instruments which are so laborious and costly. Being content with this instrument, they will investigate the gaps between stars more correctly and precisely than has been done before by means of the **Astronomical Staff** <note>original: "Radium Astronomicum", also known as the Jacob’s Staff or Cross-staff, a common but less accurate tool used by earlier astronomers</note>, provided that they properly and quickly guard against the **parallax** <note>Here Tycho refers to "instrumental parallax" or observational error caused by the alignment of the eye with the sights, rather than the modern astronomical concept of stellar parallax.</note> of which I spoke.

# THE ARRANGEMENT OF THE SAME
## INSTRUMENT SO THAT IT MAY SERVE
### FOR TAKING ALTITUDES <term>altitudes</term> <note>original: "altitudibus capiendis." In astronomy, altitude refers to the vertical angle of a celestial body measured from the observer's local horizon toward the zenith (the point directly overhead).</note>.

<meta>This page continues the description of the portable wooden sextant, shifting from its use in measuring angular distances between stars to its application as a vertical instrument for measuring a star's height above the horizon (altitude). ->## EXPLANATION OF THE CONSTRUCTION AND USE.<-

**I**nstrument, if it specifically comprises a sextant <note>A sextant is an astronomical instrument used to measure angles up to 60 degrees, or one-sixth of a circle.</note> of a circle, its circumference is not unsuitable for examining the **altitudes** <term>altitudes</term> <note>The vertical angle of a celestial body measured from the horizon toward the zenith.</note> of the stars as well: provided it is arranged as the attached figure shows. By this method, at that time when that **new star** <note>original: "nova illa stella." This refers to the famous supernova of 1572 (SN 1572) in the constellation Cassiopeia, which Tycho famously observed.</note> was seen in the year [15]72 and the following year, I investigated its minimum altitude when it was on the **meridian** <term>meridian</term> <note>The imaginary great circle on the celestial sphere passing through the north and south poles and the observer's zenith.</note> below the Pole. Indeed, it was not possible to measure its maximum altitude when it approached the peak <note>original: "vertici," referring to the zenith.</note> with the same instrument; for in our horizon, that wonderful star, when it was on the meridian above the Pole, was not far distant from the **Zenith** <term>Zenith</term> <note>The point in the sky directly above the observer.</note>. Therefore, its altitude could not be contained within a sixth part of a circle <note>i.e., it was higher than 60 degrees from the horizon.</note>; as can be seen in the aforementioned book and the indicated chapter.

The method by which I established the observation, also revealed in the same place, was of this kind: This sextant, as far as the lower rule **A B** was concerned, was arranged in a certain high window above the wall **R**, so that it corresponded precisely to the celestial meridian adapted for the observation. A certain support indicated by the letters **V X** was placed beneath it: and this, from the rear part toward the center near **T**, was of such a height that the rule **A B** itself would coincide exactly with the level <note>original: "æquilibrio."</note> of the horizon. 

I tested this in the following way: I directed the arc of the instrument **K N** with its rules to the plane of the meridian, and at the end where the 60 degrees finish, near **N**, I hung a **plumb line** <note>A weight on a string used to establish a vertical line.</note> marked by **N S**. I raised or lowered the fundamental rule **A B** by means of certain wedges placed between **T** and **V X**, as was necessary, and with it the whole instrument, until the bronze wire **N O S** (to which the lead weight is attached near **S**) precisely touched the middle point between the center of the sextant at **A** and the periphery of the division at **O**. 

For it is clear from this that the rule or base **A B** of the instrument was exactly parallel to the horizon. Since, from the *Elements of Geometry*, a sextant of a circle forms an isosceles triangle from the center (as is demonstrated in the 15th Proposition of the fourth book of **Euclid**) <note>Euclid's Elements, Book IV, Prop. 15 describes how to inscribe a regular hexagon in a circle; since a sextant is 1/6th of a circle, it forms an equilateral/isosceles triangle with the center.</note> through the points **R N A** and the line of the rule **R A**. And since the straight line descending from **N**, which faces the Zenith, divides the base **R A** into two equal parts, it necessarily follows that this happens at right angles, as appears from Proposition 12 of Book 1 and those immediately preceding. On account of which, the plumb line **N S** is always directed toward the Zenith, and **N O** is joined to it, it is entirely necessary that the rule **R A** be orthogonal <note>At a right angle (90 degrees).</note> to that same plumb line: and therefore also in the level of the horizon, from which the peak [Zenith] is distant on all sides by a quadrant of a circle, which makes a right angle. 

Therefore, with all things worked out and ordered with as much accuracy as possible, until the new star reached the meridian in its lower position by the motion of the universe, I waited, and then I sighted through another **sighting-vane** <term>pinnacidium</term> <note>A small plate with a hole or slit used for aligning the eye with a celestial object.</note> analogous to the previous one near **C** of the rule, placed not far from the center near **R**, which could be removed if necessity required. For in its forward part, a certain small slit had been made, along with the upper side of the first sighting-vane, parallel with respect to the lower part and the center, through which, while observing and moving the long **screw** <term>cochlea</term> **O E**, the angle of the instrument was varied, until I sighted the new star precisely on the upper edge of the sighting-vane at **C**, with half of it seen and the other half hidden, as far as could be discerned with any sharpness. For at that time I had not yet invented the small slits parallel to each other for the first sighting-vane, by whose benefit the center of a star is most exactly noted. 

In this manner, therefore, through the arc intercepted between both sighting-vanes **B C**, I found the minimum altitude of the star above the horizon, just as I recorded in the cited place of the aforementioned book. However, since the rule **B A**, and thus the whole sextant, could easily be moved from the level of the horizon during observation if the supports on which the instrument rested yielded even slightly to one side or the other through the driven wedges; therefore, near the small line located at **O** in the middle of the lower rule, which the plumb line of the instrument ought to strike with its contact when standing correctly, certain divisions were adapted on each side in a certain small arc **P Q** described around the center **N**. Thus, for the quantity of one degree in such a diameter of the circle, minutes were distributed on each side: in the front part **O P** to be subtracted; in the rear part **O Q** to be added, according as the plumb line showed some inclination from the equilibrium one way or the other. And in that way, after the observation was completed, I proved the altitude found through the arc **B C** by inspecting the plumb line again, and (wherever it seemed necessary) I limited it more accurately. 

Although the matter might be conducted more exquisitely if the sighting were done through two parallel slits moved to the eye near the forward sighting-vane; nevertheless, even by acting thus as we have now said, that which was sought resulted precisely enough; especially where the experiment can be made repeatedly on the same star; as in the case of that new star, which, for as long as it lasted, perpetually retained the same altitude on the meridian, changed imperceptibly. Indeed, those things which have been said here regarding this as an example, [apply] equally to measuring the altitudes of other stars, and those not exceeding the sixth part of a circle— <note>The text cuts off here, continuing with the catchword "excedentibus" on the next page.</note>

## EXPLANATION OF THE CONSTRUCTION AND USE.

...these [parts] are to be understood: and this not only when they are on the **meridian** <term>meridian</term> <note>The imaginary great circle passing through the celestial poles and the observer's zenith; the point where stars reach their highest or lowest daily altitude.</note>, but also in any other **vertical circle** <note>A circle on the celestial sphere passing through the zenith and nadir, perpendicular to the horizon.</note>; namely, by directing the plane of the Instrument into the plane of that same circle; and with all other things arranged thus, as was indicated before.

The use of this Instrument is also evident in determining **Altitudes** <term>Altitudes</term> <note>The vertical angle of a celestial body above the horizon.</note> from what was said before: although (to speak the truth of the matter) it is possible to carry this out with less trouble and even more precision using those **Quadrants** <term>Quadrants</term> <note>An astronomical instrument shaped like a quarter-circle (90 degrees), generally more stable and easier to use for altitude measurements than a sextant.</note>, especially the larger ones, which I have already mentioned before. But since one may not have such instruments at hand, which are quite expensive, it will be permissible to use this one, which we have just discussed, or some similar one; just as we previously advised regarding the taking of distances with the benefit of the same [instrument].

However, the skillful researcher must be wary of those instruments and their arrangement which easily wobble or are prone to developing a defect in any way. For the matter concerns the smallest details, from which the greatest conclusions are drawn; lest we **lose our effort and our oil** <note>original: "operam & oleum perdamus." An ancient Latin proverb meaning to waste time and labor, similar to "burning the midnight oil" for no result.</note>, as the saying goes. This has happened to most of the more recent astronomers. If only it were not possible to suspect the same of those ancient ones, regarding precise subtlety. Indeed, a more exquisite method would be given for establishing an Astronomy that corresponds **exactly** <note>original: "ad amussim," literally "according to a carpenter's rule."</note> across all centuries.

->In which, however, as much as the observations and<-
->opinions of our predecessors allow, with the<-
->Supreme Will's favor, we have both worked and<-
->will work, so that all things, purged of errors,<-
->may be restored to their wholeness.<-

# EXPLANATION OF THE CONSTRUCTION
## AND USE.

Regarding that massive **Quadrant** <term>Quadrant</term> <note>An astronomical instrument shaped like a quarter-circle, used to measure the altitudes of celestial bodies.</note>, which we caused to be constructed twenty-six years ago in the garden of Lord Paul Hainzel at his estate half a German mile distant from **Augsburg** <note>original: "Augustæ Vindelicorum," the Roman name for the city in Bavaria.</note>, it pleases me to include here both the illustration and the explanation, even though it no longer exists; for five years after its construction, it fell into ruin. Nevertheless, if anyone should wish to have a similar one built, I believe I shall perform a worthwhile service if I add this instruction so that they may have a model to imitate. This Quadrant is represented by the letters **A C P**, along with its interwoven timber frames. It was entirely fashioned from **oak heartwood** <note>original: "quercino robore"</note> that had been dried for many years. The sides and the frames were like large beams. It had a radius—or a distance from the center to the circumference—of at least 14 **cubits** <note>A cubit is roughly the length of a forearm; a 14-cubit radius would be approximately 6 to 7 meters (20+ feet), making this an exceptionally large instrument for the 16th century.</note>. On the **rim** <note>original: "limbo"</note> or circumference, where the divisions were marked, it was covered with **brass** <term>brass</term> <note>original: "Orichalcicis"</note> plates. The interwoven frames, compacted from transverse beams and smoothed, were arranged for the purpose of holding the entire Quadrant more firmly together and maintaining it in its proper size and flat plane. The division of the circumference itself was only according to the common form. For at that time, I had not yet discovered the other more convenient method <note>Tycho refers here to his later use of transversals or "nonius" scales for higher precision.</note> which I used later in other instruments. Nevertheless, each single **Degree** <term>Degree</term> could be distributed not only into single minutes, but even any minute into six small parts: so that it could distinctly provide even **ten-second intervals**. The **Sights** <term>Sights</term> <note>original: "Pinnacidia"; these are the alignment plates used to target a star.</note> were on the other side of the Quadrant at **D E**, where the observer would stand near **D**. The sight closest to the eye had a small hole in the middle, through which stars were sighted at the upper end near the diametrical line at **E**. If, however, the **Sun** was to be observed, another perforated sight was used at the front position near **E**, which admitted the light of the Sun through a round hole into a circle of the same size on the inner part of the lower sight. For this, a certain oblong canal <note>Essentially a light-shielding tube.</note> was especially used, so that the light of the Sun would be less scattered in the air and could be more exactly discerned within the limits of that same circle. Furthermore, near its center **A**, a thin brass wire was hung, which could support a **lead weight** <note>original: "plumbum"</note> of several pounds at the hole **H**, so that the wire would not be easily moved by the wind. For the wire itself showed the sought-after altitude on the scale of divisions as exactly as possible.

This Quadrant machine was fixed to a thick and sturdy oak column around its center at **A** by a strong, rounded iron pin, secured at the back at **G**. Two brackets <note>original: "capreolis"</note> also supported the rim itself, adapted slightly above the hole where the lead plumb-line is. These were for the purpose of binding the whole Quadrant tightly to the column when it was raised or lowered, as the observation required, and to hold it so that it would not fall back by its own weight before the measurement of the observation was completed. The column itself was secured and turned in its lower part within a certain underground structure **X Y S P K**, so that its lowest part—tapered and made of steel at **K**—could be rotated very easily in a base-hole of the same shape. Above, there was a round, cone-shaped hole surrounding it on all sides, so that it would not wobble back and forth, but rather the column itself and the Quadrant attached to it could be turned in all **vertical planes** <term>vertical planes</term> <note>By rotating the column, the quadrant could be pointed toward any direction (azimuth) on the horizon.</note>. The rotation of which I speak was done by the handles **Q O I N**. The Quadrant stood unmoved by its own weight in whatever plane was needed. This entire lower section seen below the handles was hidden <note>original: "Crypticum"</note>, firmly joined together and supported by stones both below and on all sides, so that it might more firmly support the entire machine standing above it and prevent it from being pulled apart. For the machine itself stood under the open sky; therefore, it was exposed to the winds and the harms of the weather. It was nevertheless covered, especially around its circumference, so that the rim of the divisions would not suffer any damage.

Its use was solely for measuring most accurately the **Altitudes** <term>Altitudes</term> of both the Sun and the rest of the **Planets**, such that it could be recorded within a sixth part of a minute <note>10 seconds of arc.</note>, provided the observer applied the required diligence. Our predecessors hardly ever reached such precision, although we later achieved the same results even with smaller instruments crafted with a different efficient design. When an altitude was to be taken, the observer placed his eye near the sight at **D**, and searched for the star in the other forward sight, raising or lowering the entire Quadrant until this could be achieved. If, however, the altitudes were lower <note>Meaning closer to the horizon, requiring the observer to climb higher to look through the bottom sight.</note>, there was need for ladders by which the observer might ascend; and then, once the machine was left unmoved, the Altitude...
