QV A.

# EXPLANATION OF THE CONSTRUCTION AND USE.

The sought-after **altitude** <term>altitude</term> <note>The angular distance of a celestial object above the horizon.</note> was shown on the circumference by means of the **plumb line** <term>plumb line</term> <note>original: "filum perpendiculi"; a weighted cord used to establish a vertical line, allowing the observer to read the precise angle on the quadrant's graduated scale.</note>. And let these remarks suffice regarding the construction and use of the instrument.

If anyone requires more complete details regarding the circumstances, he will find them in the first volume of our ***Progymnasmata*** <term>Progymnasmata</term> <note>Refers to Tycho Brahe's work *Astronomiae Instauratae Progymnasmata* (Preliminary Exercises for the Restoration of Astronomy), which dealt extensively with the supernova of 1572.</note> from page 353 to 359. For the altitudes of the **new star** <note>The "Nova Stella" or supernova of 1572 in the constellation Cassiopeia. Tycho’s observations of this event proved that the heavens were not changeless, as previously believed.</note>, and also those of certain other stars, recorded via this same machine by the aforementioned Lord **Paul Hainzel** <note>Paulus Hainzelius (1527–1581) was the Burgomaster (mayor) of Augsburg and a patron of astronomy who helped Tycho build this massive quadrant in his garden.</note>, the Burgomaster, for my sake, and communicated to me afterwards, are recorded in the following pages of that work. From these records, their precision can be evident to a knowledgeable person; they also agreed perfectly with those measurements which I later took in Denmark with other **instruments** <term>instruments</term> <note>original: "Organis".</note>. And it is to be wished that this remarkable machine had been preserved there for a longer time and put to use, or at least that another had been built in its place.

->But since men mostly attend to earthly things rather than<-
->celestial ones, they are accustomed to neglect such matters,<-
->perhaps with greater loss than they themselves<-
->easily perceive.<-

# THE GREAT **STEEL QUADRANT** <term>quadrant</term> <note>A precision astronomical instrument used for measuring the altitude (height) of celestial bodies above the horizon, featuring a 90-degree graduated arc.</note>
## ENCLOSED IN A SQUARE, AND
### resting on a steel **AZIMUTHAL** <term>azimuthal</term> <note>An azimuthal mount allows the entire instrument to rotate horizontally. This enables the astronomer to measure not just the height of a star, but also its exact compass direction (azimuth).</note> **HORIZON** <term>horizon</term> <note>original: "horizonti"; in this context, it refers to the large, level circular base plate that allowed for horizontal rotation.</note>.

<meta>This page continues the technical description of the Great Steel Quadrant, moving from its general introduction to a detailed explanation of its components, its mechanical assembly, and its use in astronomical observation. It also includes a laudatory poem by Tycho Brahe's associate and son-in-law, Tengnagel.</meta>

# EXPLANATION OF THE CONSTRUCTION AND USE.

We previously explained this Quadrant among the instruments depicted as number 7, though arranged in a different manner; it was set within a wall vault on a strong steel axis looking toward the **Zenith** and **Nadir** <term>Zenith and Nadir</term> <note>The points directly above and below an observer, respectively.</note>, and at the same time, it had a fixed **Azimuthal** <term>azimuthal</term> <note>Referring to the horizontal angle or direction of a celestial body.</note> ring within the circumference of the wall-vault, just as it was sufficiently depicted and explained there with the other requirements belonging to it. However, since I promised in that same place that I would show its other arrangement, which I had used from the beginning, here you have it: it shall be briefly explained as follows: **A B C D** is the Quadrant itself, described from the Center **A**, and enclosed by the Square **A E F G**. Its sighting-rule <term>regula</term> <note>The movable arm or alidade used for sighting stars.</note> **A C I** extends downward to the handle **H**, near which is the **sighting-vane** <term>pinnacidium</term> <note>A small plate with a slit or hole used to align the instrument with a star.</note>, having slits parallel to the upper one at **C**. It is elongated from **C** to **I** at the upper part, so that it can touch the Square everywhere; to this, certain brass wires <note>original: "fila quædam Orichalcica"</note> have been added near **I** in place of a support, so that the index remains on a straight line everywhere. What is seen from the back part, **A L M N O**, indicates the iron support, sustaining the whole Quadrant perpendicularly on the underlying horizontal plane. For near **A**, it is joined to the Azimuthal Horizon like a bracket having a fine-threaded screw beneath it, so that by the handle which is at the back, it can be moved around within the Azimuthal circle wherever one pleases. The Azimuthal Circle itself, of which I speak, is represented by the letters **P Q R S**. Through **X**, five strong columns are indicated which carry the whole machine, at the tops of which, near **Y** and **Z**, are certain screws, called **endless screws** <term>cochleae perennes</term> <note>Also known as worm gears, these allowed for very fine adjustments to level the instrument.</note>, also with their own rotating handles, disposing the Azimuthal Circle into the equilibrium of the Horizon, and directing the whole Quadrant into a vertical plane, just as the **plummets** <term>perpendicula</term> <note>Lead weights on strings used to ensure the instrument was perfectly vertical.</note> marked at **V** and **W** require. The rest is clear from the indication made in the place cited above, where this same Quadrant was arranged differently and somewhat more conveniently. Therefore, I did not care to repeat more regarding this explanation, lest I should wearidly "re-cook" <note>An idiom meaning to repeat the same thing over and over.</note> the same things that were said before. However, so that the space below might nevertheless be filled, as before, a poem in hexameter verse has been added, which the noble and learned youth **FRANCISCUS GANSNEB TENGNAGEL** <note>Tycho Brahe’s assistant and future son-in-law, who helped publish Tycho’s works.</note>, who was a member of my household for some time and a companion of my migration from Denmark, composed extemporaneously to be subjoined here.

> *Yield, you humble Arts, and yield, listless care;*
> *For Divine **Urania** <note>The Muse of Astronomy.</note> has raised her head so far above all the Muses,*
> *As much as the Heavens surpass the Earth, as much as the nourishing light*
> *Conquers the darkness: And **G O D** Himself gave to man a face*
> *So sublime, that he might look upon the Stars, that he might lift his eyes upward,*
> *Gaze upon the Ether, and bring the wandering Stars under his rule.*
> *Happy are they, therefore, to whom these Divine things are a care,*
> *While others seek wealth, and pursue honors,*
> *Crave delights and luxury, and dealing in any*
> *Trifles, lead their lives stupidly like sluggish cattle,*
> *Worthless men. Ah, where does blind ignorance*
> *Drive these headlong, whose hearts do not care for lofty things?*
> *You, whose generous mind burns with the fire of the celestial seed,*
> *Ascend briskly into the heavenly recesses.*
> *Come here, **Timochares**, **Ptolemy**, **Copernicus**, although* <note>Timochares (3rd century BCE), Ptolemy (2nd century CE), and Nicolaus Copernicus (1473–1543) were the foundational astronomers whose data Tycho sought to improve.</note>
> *You relied too much on deceptive instruments.*
> *Is it permitted to climb a high tower with small*
> *Steps, or to grasp the Heavens with a mere pole?*

*How may the inner sanctuaries of the star-bearing world lie open?*
*Unless that care had once frustrated you, it would now be granted*
*to reckon the stars better by your numbers.*
*But you, whose mind is neighbor to the Gods, come now,*
**TYCHO**, *most celebrated glory of the Danish people:*
*It pleased the Powers Above that you should see such obstacles*
*yielding to human strength; and you bring aid to the labor*
*of Hercules <note>A reference to the mythological hero Hercules, who briefly held up the sky for Atlas; here, Tycho is compared to Hercules for his immense task of mapping the heavens.</note>, which Atlas did not well endure.*
*Kindly provide the instruments, which you know to be the greatest and best*
*to build, and which this book of yours shares with the world:*
*Through thrice seven years <note>original: "Annis ter septem"; referring to the 21 years Tycho spent observing at Uraniborg.</note>, during which the twinkling stars*

*endure it with a lofty mind, just as you do: the earth is a birthplace*
*everywhere to the magnanimous man. Hail, inhabitant of the World.*
*Enough has been given to the North and the Danes. Well done, renowned* **TYCHO**,
*proceed the more to spread your praises through the World.*

*to bring forth the tokens of your genius with excellent fruit.*
*And thus future ages will speak your name more abundantly,*
*as long as golden-haired Cynthius <term>Cynthius</term> <note>An epithet for Apollo, used here to represent the Sun.</note> counts the years,*

Tycho Brahe here begins a "Brief Indication" or supplement, describing instruments he has built but which were not yet ready to be illustrated with woodcut engravings at the time of publication.</meta>

## About
# A BRIEF ACCOUNT OF CERTAIN OTHER INSTRUMENTS OF OURS WHICH HAVE NOT YET BEEN ENGRAVED.

Thus far, we have presented and explained our astronomical instruments, at least as many as had been drawn and engraved <note>The Latin "exsculpta" refers to the woodcut illustrations used in the printing process.</note>, as far as was possible for the time being. However, I still have certain others ready at hand which, because they have not yet been depicted or cut into woodblocks, it was necessary to omit here. I shall nevertheless take care that, as soon as it may be done, these too shall be joined to the previous ones, so that this entire mechanical treatise may be more fully enlarged, with certain aids and tables conducive to this purpose being added. In the meantime, I shall nonetheless briefly touch upon some of those that remain.

## THE TWO-PRONGED SEXTANT.

We have also constructed a certain <term>Sextant</term><note>An instrument used for measuring the angular distance between two objects, such as stars; it covers a sixth of a circle (60 degrees).</note> which, because it has sighting arms extending from the center that can be narrowed or widened, crossing one another in the shape of an X, we have for that reason called it "two-pronged" <note>original: "bifurcatum"</note>. It possesses a steel arc comprising a sixth part of a circle. The length of the arms is 4 cubits <note>Roughly 6 feet or 1.8 meters.</note>. It also has brass sights <note>original: "Pinnacidia Orichalcica"</note> arranged in various ways away from the center near the ends of the arms on both sides. The arms themselves are made of Brazilwood <note>A dense, heavy wood from South America, highly prized in the 16th century for its resistance to warping.</note>, because that wood is compact and durable. It has divisions made upon the arc in our usual manner. By means of this sextant, one may investigate the distances between stars with sufficient precision through two observers, each of whom looks through the sights of his own arm—except that this is achieved with somewhat more trouble than with the other sextants, and not quite as quickly. It is, however, easily portable, and thus occasionally convenient for various uses.

## A LARGE SEMICIRCLE FOR MARKING GREATER DISTANCES IN THE HEAVENS.

Since by means of the sextants devised and prepared by us, only those distances of the stars can be observed which are contained within a sixth part of the sky, and it occasionally happens in practice that distances larger than these must be measured—sometimes even larger than a fourth part of the sky <note>A "fourth part" refers to a Quadrant, or 90 degrees.</note>, as the convenience and practice of observation demands (which most often occurs in the greater distances of the Sun and Moon, when they are seen together during the day around either <term>quadrature</term><note>The point where the Moon is 90 degrees away from the Sun, appearing as a half-moon.</note> or beyond; and it also finds a place on other occasions)—we have therefore caused a certain semicircular instrument to be made. It is strengthened everywhere by cross-beams and firmly covered with brass plates both on the circumference and on the diametrical arm. By its benefit, the intervals between stars, however great they may finally be—even up to the completion of a full hemisphere <note>180 degrees.</note>—may be exactly measured. This semicircle has a diameter of 6 cubits; and near the middle of the whole structure, where it balances itself almost everywhere, there is a square hole, in which 

<summary>Tycho begins a technical supplement describing instruments not yet illustrated in the book, specifically a portable "two-pronged" sextant made of Brazilwood and a large six-cubit semicircle for measuring vast angular distances up to 180 degrees.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the technical descriptions of instruments that Tycho Brahe had built but not yet illustrated in his main work. It follows from the description of the "two-pronged sextant" started on the previous page.</meta>

...placed upon a strong support, particularly a globular one <note>A ball-and-socket joint allowing for movement in multiple directions.</note> during observation, and is directed toward the plane of the stars by two observers; its use is established no differently than in the case of the Sextants. For this reason, it will not be necessary to add more about it here; instead, let those who desire further details consult the explanations for using the Sextant to capture distances.

## THE ASTRONOMICAL STAFF.

We do not entirely reject the use of the **A**stronomical Staff <note>Also known as a Jacob’s Staff or Cross-staff, a popular portable navigation and astronomical tool.</note>, especially for use while traveling, since it is easily portable and can be enclosed in a very small case—even though it does not provide the most exact and completely certain distances of the stars. I have such a tool at hand, not indeed constructed by myself, but fashioned by Walter Arsenius, the nephew of that most excellent mathematician Gemma Frisius <note>Gemma Frisius (1508–1555) was a Dutch physician and mathematician who famously described the use of the cross-staff for surveying.</note>, who formerly lived at Louvain in Belgium (and who published a small book on this subject). It consists entirely of <term>brass</term> <note>original: "Orichalcicis"</note> plates skillfully put together, although the interior is wood; it possesses <term>sights</term> <note>original: "pinnacidia" – small plates with holes or slits for viewing stars.</note> and divisions exactly as handed down by the same Gemma in his aforementioned book. The longer rod of this tool, or the <term>Staff</term> <note>original: "Radius"</note> itself, is a little more than three cubits <note>About 4.5 to 5 feet long.</note> in length. In its four-sided thickness, it is everywhere about the width of a rather large thumb. The cross-piece is about half the size of the Staff itself, both in length and width. Furthermore, I have also seen to it that another Staff was made by my own craftsmen, also entirely of <term>brass</term>, but hollow inside and stuffed with no wood at all. For wood has this nature: unless it is helped in a peculiar way, it forces the <term>brass</term> plates with which it is covered to bend wherever it is pulled by its own instability and changes in the air <note>Wood warps with humidity, which would ruin the precision of the metal scales attached to it.</note>. That Staff, which we constructed later, is not four-sided but three-sided, because it would thus be lighter, and these three surfaces would suffice for denoting all the divisions upon it. Moreover, I produced equal divisions on it by means of the transversal points <note>A method of using diagonal dots to read scales with much higher precision than simple lines.</note> commonly used by us, so that it might equal a five-digit <term>Table of Sines</term> <note>original: "Canonem Sinuum"</note>; thus it would execute everything more subtly than the aforementioned Staff of Gemma. I also used an unequal division, ordered differently, since that used by him was flawed. The length and width, and nearly the entire proportion, are equal to the previous one, and it has similar <term>sights</term>. But to tell the truth, a Staff, however it is prepared, does not supply the truest distances of the stars (as we also suggested before), not even the narrower ones within 15 degrees, let alone the wider ones, where it fails even more. It will not be difficult to explain the reasons for this elsewhere; here we strive for brevity. Nevertheless, I have myself devised a certain means by which the difficulties and hallucinations <note>Errors or "ghost" readings in measurement.</note> of the Staff might be totally corrected, by also employing in it sights having slits, and a <term>Cylinder</term> around which two observers may search out distances, no differently than in sextants. Regarding this, we shall say more to your Devotion <note>A formal address to the reader or patron.</note> at another time, when it has been prepared (for it is now under construction).

## THE ASTRONOMICAL RING.

**F**urthermore, we have at hand an Astronomical Ring, an instrument used by many others, both ancient and modern, on account of its convenience. It was made several years ago by the same nephew of Gemma, following the description which Gemma himself set forth in a concise booklet. This <term>Ring</term> is entirely of <term>brass</term> and skillfully assembled. It has a diameter of about one cubit <note>Approximately 18–24 inches.</note>. To this we have added another invention on the inside: namely, long and smooth <term>Cylinders</term> crossing each other crosswise and perpendicularly, grazing the surface of the inner ring and fitted there with a square support. Then also, by means of <term>sights</term> and certain other methods provided by us, it is arranged so that it not only performs much more conveniently what Gemma and others handed down concerning the use of this Ring, but it also supplies certain other things. For in this way, not only can the Declination of the stars be captured more aptly than before, but also their differences in ascension, and thus their very Right Ascensions <note>The celestial equivalent of longitude.</note> can be denoted from the heavens—to say nothing of the longitudes and latitudes of the stars, which can also be obtained by a certain peculiar method. We shall deal with these things more fully, God willing, when the instrument has been fashioned and engraved.

<summary>Tycho Brahe describes his improved versions of the portable cross-staff and astronomical ring, emphasizing his transition from wood-core to hollow brass construction to prevent warping and his use of transversal dots for higher precision.</summary>
<keywords>Cross-staff, Astronomical Ring, Gemma Frisius, Walter Arsenius, brass instruments, celestial navigation, precision measurement, warping, transversal points</keywords>

--- Page 77 ---
<meta>This page continues Tycho Brahe’s discussion of astronomical instruments, moving from the limitations of small "ring" instruments to more complex portable armillary spheres and the design of the astrolabe. It follows the description of the astronomical staff and other portable tools.</meta>

But however that may be, it is not possible to scrutinize celestial appearances with sufficient precision using such small and playful instruments; this is because their small size does not allow for the degrees to be subdivided into minutes with the necessary scrupulousness. Furthermore, if they were made large enough to provide such detail, they would become unmanageable due to their size and mass; moreover, they would weigh themselves down so much that they would not be suitable for use without some error. We also possess another smaller <term>brass</term> <note>original: "Orichalcicus"</note> ring, which is barely the length of a short <term>span</term> <note>original: "spitami" – the distance from the tip of the thumb to the tip of the little finger, roughly 7-9 inches.</note> in diameter. But this one pleases me even less.

The primary use of such rings is for distinguishing the hours of the night and day, although they do not perform this with the ease and precision they promise. Nevertheless, they are suitable for this purpose in some way when other more certain means are lacking, especially since they are conveniently portable due to their manageable size. As for the other uses which <note>Gemma Frisius (1508–1555), a famous Dutch cartographer and instrument maker.</note> Gemma and others have compiled, which are less essential and not entirely certain, let those who wish to know seek them in their own booklets.

## THE PORTABLE ARMILLARY SPHERE.

Indeed, we have also constructed a single **P**ortable Armillary Sphere <note>An instrument consisting of a framework of rings representing the principal circles of the celestial sphere.</note>, stabilized internally by suitable supports, which can be easily carried out and set up under the open sky on stone pedestals using its own adapted screws. In this way, it can be rotated with very little effort toward any part of the sky. By its benefit, the <term>declinations</term> <note>The angular distance of a star north or south of the celestial equator.</note> of the stars alone are captured, though not quite as subtly as with those larger instruments mentioned previously. However, since this Armillary is nearly 3 <term>cubits</term> <note>A unit of length based on the forearm; Tycho's cubit was likely around 18 inches, making the instrument about 4.5 feet wide.</note> in diameter, and its circumference is covered everywhere in brass so that it expresses every single minute of all the degrees by means of <term>transversal points</term> <note>A system of diagonal dots that allowed Tycho to read measurements far more precisely than simple scale marks.</note>, it has served not uselessly for capturing declinations. I have used it most especially when the stars approach the horizon; for with those larger instruments, it may perhaps not be possible to execute this so conveniently due to the obstructions of the <term>crypts</term> <note>original: "cryptarum" – Tycho built underground observatories at Stjerneborg to shield his large instruments from the wind.</note>, if any should interfere. It is also used for other reasons, as sometimes a greater convenience is found in these than in those larger ones for carrying out this work in certain peculiar locations, and also so that the same result may be confirmed by a variety of instruments.

It is also possible to adapt to this Armillary, whenever one pleases, its own semicircular Equator and a smooth cylindrical axis; thus, it can be accommodated for several uses, namely for capturing <term>right ascensions</term> <note>The celestial equivalent of longitude.</note> and the exact moments of time. We have also been accustomed to doing this occasionally.

## THE ASTROLABE.

The **A**strolabe is an instrument used very much by the ancients, and especially by astrologers, and first invented (as I believe) with skillful imagination by the Arabs. It compresses and arranges the circles and use of the celestial sphere onto a flat plane. We have not yet wished to construct one of a proper magnitude because its use in the observations of the stars is less convenient, nor is it sufficient and certain for them. Nevertheless, I possess a small one purchased at my own expense, elaborately and solidly fashioned from brass, which equals only the length of a large span in diameter. It is composed according to that ancient method with its own <term>plates</term> <note>original: "matricibus" – interchangeable discs for different latitudes.</note>, which Johannes Stoffler <note>A German mathematician (1452–1531) whose treatise on the astrolabe was a standard textbook.</note> and others followed and clearly handed down. Let those who wish to do so seek its construction and use from them.

But some years ago at Nuremberg, I took care to have round brass plates diligently fashioned, which are more than two cubits in diameter and of a suitable thickness. On these, I decided to elaborately construct a **Universal Astrolabe** which, without the need for separate plates, would provide on at least two faces everything that can be taught regarding the entire doctrine of the <term>Primum Mobile</term> <note>The "First Mover," the outermost sphere in the geocentric model which carries all others.</note>, and which can otherwise be conveniently performed on a globular body that this instrument represents on a plane. Therefore, I also intend to add a peculiar <term>rete</term> <note>The "net," the cutout top plate of an astrolabe showing the positions of the stars.</note> to it, and to mark it with the principal fixed stars—far more of them than were in use among the ancients. Perhaps this Astrolabe, by its own particular convenience, will surpass that invention of De Roys <note>Likely referring to the "Saphea" or universal astrolabe design.</note> and that other even more plausible one of Gemma Frisius later put into practice...

<summary>Tycho Brahe describes the limitations of small astronomical rings and introduces his portable armillary sphere and a planned "Universal Astrolabe" of significant size and precision.</summary>
<keywords>portable armillary sphere, astrolabe, brass instruments, transversal points, declination, Gemma Frisius, Johannes Stoffler, astronomical measurement, Nuremberg</keywords>

--- Page 78 ---
<meta>This page continues Tycho Brahe’s discussion of astronomical instruments. He concludes his thoughts on a potential "universal" astrolabe and begins describing a new category of portable, multi-purpose instruments he designed as he prepared to leave Denmark. Continuity is maintained with the previous discussion of small rings and the need for precision.</meta>

...it will excel in convenience and subtle reasoning, and exist even more universally. But so far, because of the construction of other instruments and a manifold weight of business, the opportunity has not been granted to us to prepare such an **Astrolabe**. We also left it unfinished all the more willingly because its use does not properly and precisely explore the motions and locations of the stars, but rather presupposes them to be already known from elsewhere; furthermore, we did not yet have the fixed stars, with which it ought to be marked, fully explored according to their own proper restoration. Since these stars are now exactly known to us by Divine favor, it will not be difficult—once some leisure is obtained—to construct an **Astrolabe** of this kind, especially since the brass <note>original: "Orichalcicæ"</note> plates once acquired for those uses, as I said, are still partially preserved in my possession.

<insert>Concerning</insert>
# CERTAIN OTHER ASTRONOMICAL IN-
# STRUMENTS; EXPEDITING MANY THINGS WITH A
# WONDROUS BREVITY, WHICH I RECENTLY DE-
# VISED AND, BY DIVINE FAVOR AT THE PROPER TIME,
## I have decided to elaborate.

The instruments we have already reviewed, both those depicted and explained in such manifold ways as well as those indicated briefly afterward, we already have ready and at hand. And although they suffice for all kinds of observations of the celestial bodies by their variety and abundance, I nonetheless began to reflect within myself—since I silently foresaw the necessity of migrating from Denmark <note>original: "Dania"</note>, my sweetest homeland—lest I should unpraiseworthily abandon these studies pursued for so many years, with such great effort, and at the greatest expense even into the third <term>septenary</term> <note>A period of seven years; Tycho is referring to the twenty-first year of his astronomical labor.</note>, which is usually the <term>climacteric</term> <note>A critical period or turning point in life, historically thought to occur in years divisible by 7 or 9.</note> and subject to changes. I wondered whether it might not be possible to devise certain other <term>Astronomical Instruments</term> <note>original: "Organa Astronomica"</note> which, with fewer requirements, might conveniently and surely exhibit all the things that the others built here provide; instruments which would also not lack the ease of being transported to any other locations, if by chance it were not possible to have those I possess here moved to me quickly enough and without inconvenience. Therefore, by Divine favor, I am certain that I have recently invented several of this kind. Among these are three rules <note>straight-edged measuring sticks or scales</note> which will suffice not only for taking the altitudes of the stars, like those Ptolemaic ones, but also for searching out their distances; and these can be aptly assembled and transported to any place. Indeed, we have also invented an **Armillary Instrument** <note>original: "Armillare Instrumentum" - a model of objects in the sky consisting of a spherical framework of rings.</note>, consisting of only one and a half rings, which will not only scrutinize the <term>declinations</term> <note>The angular distance of a star north or south of the celestial equator.</note> and <term>right ascensions</term> <note>The distance of a point east of the First Point of Aries, measured along the celestial equator.</note> of the stars, but will also most easily reveal their <term>longitudes</term> and <term>latitudes</term>, and will simultaneously suffice for observing <term>altitudes</term> and <term>azimuths</term> <note>The horizontal direction or bearing of a celestial object.</note>, if it is properly arranged for those uses. Nay, it will even not decline to provide the distances of the stars, if one is so inclined to use it; thus this one instrument will be equal to all kinds of uses, and can be disassembled and reassembled without difficulty whenever it pleases; and, enclosed and protected in its own case, it can be transferred to other shores. Furthermore, another method occurred to me by which the same most efficient and useful instrument could be formed with few means using long rules joined in square and triangular forms, so that it provides all the things we said of the previous method; and also certain other things, if it should seem fit.

This version will have this advantage over the preceding one: that when disassembled and packed into a narrower space and a straighter—namely, oblong—small box, it can be even more conveniently transported to various places; it can also be safely constructed in a larger size than the preceding one. Finally, I have also imagined another certain instrument which (though one might think it impossible) is suitable for skillfully searching out the stars' declinations and right ascensions, as well as their longitudes, latitudes, distances, altitudes, and azimuths, using only a single circle. This can also be constructed in a square form, though not as conveniently. Indeed, these very things can be done using only half a circle, or even its third or fourth...

<summary>Tycho Brahe explains his decision to postpone a universal astrolabe in favor of inventing new, highly portable astronomical instruments. These multi-purpose tools were designed to be easily disassembled and transported as he anticipated leaving his homeland of Denmark.</summary>
<keywords>Tycho Brahe, Astrolabe, Armillary Sphere, astronomical instruments, portability, Denmark, celestial coordinates, climacteric period</keywords>

--- Page 79 ---
<meta>This page continues Tycho Brahe’s discussion of his astronomical inventions. He concludes a technical point—likely regarding the reduction of instrument size—and transitions into an explanation of why he intends to keep his most innovative designs secret, sharing them only with high-ranking patrons.</meta>

...can be done in a certain way in a fourth part <note>This likely refers to a quadrant, which is a quarter-circle instrument</note>, although not so succinctly and universally, but in a certain partial and sometimes composite manner. These, however, when properly prepared, will provide that very thing which is sought with sufficient precision and certainty.

Concerning these and similar matters—which I have devised thus far, partly driven by the necessity that teaches the <term>Arts</term> <note>original: "Artes"; here referring to the technical skills and mechanical sciences required for astronomy</note>, and partly those I am yet to discover in the future by the favor of Divine kindness—I shall hardly publish anything openly, lest such rare inventions, if made known to everyone, become common and lose their value (as usually happens). But I shall reserve them only for Illustrious and Princely men who are deeply moved by such things; for it is their place, more than other men, to be imbued with these sublime <term>Studies</term> <note>original: "Studiis"; referring to the high pursuit of celestial science</note> and to cultivate them liberally and laudably.

->Once I am certain of their <term>Clement</term> <note>original: "Clementi"; a formal term for the gracious or favorable disposition of a sovereign</note><-
->will toward me, I shall not<-
->refuse to reveal and explain these matters, provided,<-
->however, that they keep them<-
->among their secrets.<-

# THE GREAT BRASS
# GLOBE.

<note>A highly detailed engraving is featured here, depicting the "Globus Magnus Orichalcicus." This massive celestial globe, nearly six feet in diameter, is mounted on a sturdy, four-legged stand. The globe's surface is engraved with the stars and constellations known to the 16th century, including Gemini, Scorpio, and Leo. It is surrounded by a horizontal ring (the horizon) and a vertical ring (the meridian), both used for taking precise measurements. Above the globe, a hemispherical wooden cover is suspended by a rope and pulley, used to protect the delicate instrument from dust and the elements when not in use. Various parts are marked with letters (such as Y, Z, I, and L) which correspond to the technical explanation that begins on the following page.</note>

<term>Brass</term> <note>original: "Orichalcicus"; referring to orichalcum, a golden-toned alloy of copper and zinc. In the Renaissance, this was the preferred material for high-precision scientific instruments due to its stability and the ease with which fine scales could be engraved upon it.</note>

<meta>catchword: EXPLAN-</meta>

<summary>This page serves as the title and primary illustration for Tycho Brahe's Great Brass Globe, one of the most significant astronomical instruments of the pre-telescopic era. It provides a visual overview of the instrument's complex mounting and protective housing.</summary>

<keywords>Tycho Brahe, celestial globe, brass instruments, astronomical measurements, constellations, Renaissance science, scientific illustration</keywords>

--- Page 81 ---
<meta>This page provides the technical explanation of the Great Brass Globe depicted on the previous page, detailing its construction in Augsburg, its transportation to Denmark, and its finishing over a period of twenty-five years.</meta>

EXPLANATION OF THE CONSTRUCTION AND USE.
<margin>G</margin>

# EXPLANATION OF THE CONSTRUCTION AND USE.

<margin>Decorative initial G depicting an armillary sphere</margin>
**G**lobes of the largest size, we have taken care to construct with the highest diligence and no less expense (as with all our other instruments). The interior of this globe consists of wooden material, skillfully composed of many rings and joined pieces supported from the center, and afterwards turned into a spherical shape. I arranged for this work, as far as the wooden frame is concerned, to be prepared at Augsburg <note>original: "Augustæ Vindelicorũ"</note> in the year 1570, before I departed from there for my homeland, having found a suitable craftsman there whom I had otherwise sought in vain for a long time. Because of its vast size, which made transport difficult, it remained at Augsburg into the fifth year, at which time I returned there. Specifically, in the year 1575, when I went to Regensburg for the time of the coronation of the most August Emperor Rudolf II, returning from Italy and passing through Augsburg, I found that globe which had already been prepared there for a long time. However, it was not sufficiently finished in its roundness in every part, and moreover, it was affected by certain cracks here and there. I nevertheless took care to have it brought to me in Denmark the following year, not without difficulty. There, after its cracks were repaired and its sphericity restored by the application of membranes, and after a two-year trial period to see if it would warp in any direction—so that it might withstand the changes of two summers and winters—I finally found that it constantly retained its perfectly round shape in every part. I then took care to have it totally covered with thin plates of <term>brass</term> <note>orichalcum; a golden-colored copper alloy</note> of the proper thickness. This was done with such zeal and subtlety that you would say the globe consisted of solid brass, with the joints of the plates scarcely appearing. I soon had it polished into an exact spherical form. Finally, after the <term>Zodiac</term> <note>the path of the Sun through the stars</note> and the <term>Equator</term> with their poles were marked upon it, we subtly distributed each of their degrees into 60 minutes by means of points and <term>transversals</term> <note>diagonal lines used to divide small scales into precise fractions</note> (as we are accustomed to do). However, a year's space was still left before the final distribution so that a trial could be made to see if, after the application of the brass, it would preserve its roundness in every way through both winter and summer. When this had been sufficiently tested, I adapted not only the circles of which I have spoken, but also the stars of the <term>eighth sphere</term> <note>the sphere of the fixed stars</note>, as many as it was possible to obtain from the heavens in the meantime. These I noted in their proper places with the greatest diligence; having increased in number more and more in successive years, they finally reached a thousand. Thus, I have fitted to it all the stars which are even slightly visible to the eye, deliberately relating their positions to the completed year 1600, which is soon approaching. Thus, approximately 25 years have passed from the first construction of this globe until it was finished with its divisions and stars. Although this delay might seem tedious, it was of no small utility, so that everything might be prepared more steadily, fully, and perfectly. And, "soon enough, if well enough" <note>original: "Et, sat citò, si sat benè"; a Latin proverb suggesting quality is more important than speed</note>. In the meantime, however, its outer rings were fitted, namely the *Meridian*, which the letters **E F G H** represent, and then the *Horizon* (of which more later), between which the globe itself is contained and revolves on its axis around the poles **I K**. This Meridian consists of solid steel and has divisions for every minute of every degree. The Horizon **L M N** has the width of a palm, is also covered with brass, and is divided into its degrees and minutes for <term>azimuthal</term> <note>horizontal direction or bearing</note> measurements. A brass <term>vertical quadrant</term> <note>an instrument for measuring the height of stars above the horizon</note> descends from the Zenith to the Horizon, beginning at **B** and ending at the Horizon, subdivided into 90 degrees with their individual minutes; this serves for counting altitudes on itself and azimuths on the Horizon. The Horizon itself, with its Meridian and the entire enclosed globe, rests on a firm pedestal represented by **O P Q R S T V X**, in which there are two iron pieces **Q R** crossing each other diagonally, which you see on one side, and two similar ones on the other for the sake of support and stability, lest the Horizon or the entire machine wobble at all because of its size and weight. This entire support is about 5 feet high; in its lower part, various mathematical artists are notably depicted for the sake of ornament, along with certain other things adding beauty. The globe itself has a diameter of nearly 6 feet. From this, the Meridian, Horizon, and other parts can be estimated. I believe that such a great globe-machine, so solidly and subtly elaborated and standing correctly in every part, has been constructed and finished by no one anywhere in the world (may the statement be free from envy). It is a huge and magnificent work, which many from various regions have traveled to Denmark to see, along with my other instruments, while the <term>Kingdom of Urania</term> <note>Uraniborg, Tycho's observatory</note> and its famous citadel stood unharmed. Around the Horizon, these words are read in golden letters:

> IN THE YEAR 1584 FROM THE BIRTH OF CHRIST, DURING THE REIGN OF FREDERICK II IN DENMARK, TYCHO BRAHE, SON OF OTTO, DECREED THAT THIS GLOBE, CONFORMING TO THE CELESTIAL MACHINERY, IN WHICH THE STARS OF THE EIGHTH SPHERE DISCOVERED FROM THE HEAVENS WITH INSTRUMENTS ARE REPRESENTED EXACTLY IN THEIR PROPER PLACES, SHOULD BE BUILT FOR HIMSELF AND POSTERITY, TO INVESTIGATE THE APPEARANCES OF THE WANDERING STARS THROUGH THESE MEANS, THE HEAVENS HAVING BEEN OPENED BY MECHANICAL WORK TO EARTH-BORN MEN WHO GRASP THAT REASON.

The reason this year 1584 is inscribed is that it fell around the middle of the time while this structure was being elaborated; specifically, four years before the death of King Frederick of most praised memory, who liberally and kindly fostered me and my studies and favored me with Royal love as long as he lived. I will also add this: that this magnificent machine has a cover above, indicated by **Y Z**, which is concave and round on the inside so that it may enclose the upper hemisphere of the globe. When lowered by a cord on a pulley attached to a beam wherever desired, it protects the globe itself from dust and other impurities.

The *Use* of this globe is the same as that of other celestial globes, which I intend to describe in a specific book at my leisure, for it cannot be finished in a few words. However, it has this prerogative over others because of its size: that all things may be carried out upon it most exactly, even to the very minute. Thus, individual matters concerning the doctrine of the <term>prime mobile</term> <note>the daily rotation of the heavens</note> and observations of celestial bodies with respect to the Ecliptic and Equator, or any other circles in the sphere, are performed here mechanically with the least trouble,

-> and without laborious calculation. <-

<summary>Tycho Brahe describes the 25-year process of creating his Great Brass Globe, highlighting its wooden core, brass plating, and the extreme precision of its 1,000 engraved stars. He emphasizes the instrument's massive size—six feet in diameter—and its ability to solve complex astronomical problems mechanically rather than through tedious math.</summary>
<keywords>Tycho Brahe, Great Brass Globe, Augsburg, Frederick II, astronomical instruments, Uraniborg, celestial coordinates, stars, 16th-century science</keywords>

This page marks a transition in the manuscript from the description of physical instruments to a personal narrative of Tycho Brahe's astronomical career. It follows the description of the Great Brass Globe.
</meta>

# ON THOSE THINGS WHICH WE HAVE HITHERTO ACCOMPLISHED IN ASTRONOMICAL MATTERS BY THE GIFT OF GOD, AND WHICH REMAIN TO BE PERFORMED HEREAFTER BY HIS FAVOR.

## THE THIRTY-FIVE YEARS OF OBSERVATIONS AND THE EARLY YEARS OF STUDY.

### AN AUTOBIOGRAPHICAL PREFACE.

**I**n the year of our Lord 1563—exactly thirty-five years ago—at the time when that great <term>conjunction</term> <note>The alignment of two or more planets as seen from Earth.</note> of the superior <term>planets</term> <note>In the 16th century, the "superior planets" were those further from the Sun than Earth: Mars, Jupiter, and Saturn.</note> occurred around the end of Cancer and the beginning of Leo, just as I had completed my sixteenth year of age, I was applying myself to the study of the humanities at Leipzig. I was supported there, together with my tutor, by my dearest paternal uncle **GEORGE BRAHE**, who passed away about thirty years ago. For my father himself, **OTTO BRAHE**, of honored memory, did not greatly desire that his sons (of whom he had five, and I am the firstborn) be steeped in Latin letters—a stance he later regretted. But my aforementioned uncle had raised me from infancy and thereafter liberally supported me as long as he lived, until my eighteenth year, and always treated me as a son, intending to adopt me as his heir. For his marriage was childless; he had married the most noble and prudent Lady **INGERA OXEN** <note>original: "Ingera Oxonia"</note>, sister of the great **PEDER OXEN** <note>original: "Petri Oxonii"</note>, later the Master of the Realm of Denmark, who died five years ago and who also, while she lived, embraced me with singular love like a son. She afterwards served for twelve years as Mistress of the Court in the Queen's household during the time of King **FREDERICK II** of most praiseworthy memory. She was succeeded in that office for eight years by my dearest and most honored mother **BEATA BILLE**, who by God's grace still survives, being 71 years of age.

It happened, therefore, by a certain peculiar fate, that having been taken away in infancy from my parents without their knowledge by my aforementioned uncle, I was sent by him to a Latin school around my seventh year, and after my fourteenth year, was sent to Leipzig to continue my studies, where I remained for three years. I recount these things from a little further back so that it may be clear by what occasion I was first applied to liberal studies and later turned my mind to astronomical ones, and so that I may gratefully honor the memory of the parents who deserved well of me. 

But since I had already previously made some beginnings in the rudiments of astronomy from books in my homeland of Denmark—especially from the <term>ephemerides</term> <note>Tables predicting the positions of celestial bodies at regular intervals.</note> (to come to the point I intended), to which I was drawn by a certain natural inclination—I then began at Leipzig to cultivate astronomy more and more. This was despite my tutor's unwillingness and reluctance; he preferred that I apply myself to jurisprudence <note>The study of law, which was considered the proper career for a nobleman.</note>, claiming it was my parents' will (which I nevertheless did as much as my age allowed). I bought books secretly so that my tutor would not catch scent of it. Soon, I gradually grew accustomed to identifying the <term>asterisms</term> <note>Patterns of stars; groups that form recognizable shapes in the night sky.</note> of the sky. All of those that could be conveniently seen from that horizon, I learned within the space of a month by my own effort and with no one showing me, using a certain small globe, barely the size of a fist, which I used to carry with me secretly in the evenings. Indeed, I never happened to have a teacher in mathematics; otherwise, I might have progressed sooner and perhaps further in it. 

I also soon began to attend to the motions of the planets. But because I noticed from their alignments with fixed stars and from lines drawn here and there, even from that tiny globe, that their celestial positions did not agree with either the <term>Alphonsine</term> <note>Tables based on the older Ptolemaic (Earth-centered) system, commissioned in the 13th century.</note> or the <term>Copernican</term> <note>Refers to calculations based on the newer sun-centered theory of Nicolaus Copernicus.</note> calculations—though they approached the latter more than the former—I thereafter watched more diligently every day to note their appearances. I then compared them with the calculation of the <term>Prutenic Tables</term> <note>original: "Tabularum Prutenicarum"; the first major astronomical tables based on Copernican theory.</note> (for I had also made myself familiar with these by my own study), not trusting the ephemerides; for I had found those of Stadius <note>Johannes Stadius, a Flemish astronomer.</note>, which were then the only ones available derived from those numbers, to be indulgent and full of errors in many places. 

But since I had no instruments at hand, as my tutor begrudged me them, I performed the task from the beginning as much as possible with a certain large compass <note>Actually a pair of dividers used to measure the angular distance between two stars.</note>. By applying its joint to my eye and directing both feet toward the planet to be observed and some nearby fixed star, or by taking the distances between two planets in this way, I occasionally investigated the intercepted degrees mechanically according to the size of the circle. Although this method of observing was not sufficiently exact, I was nevertheless led by it to clearly detect the intolerable errors of both calculations. This was amply demonstrated by that great conjunction of the year 1563, which I mentioned at the beginning and for which reason I primarily started from it, in the motion of Saturn and Jupiter. Indeed, it missed the Alphonsine numbers by an entire month, and Copernicus's by several days, though only a few. For his restoration in these two planets does not wander so far from the celestial standard, especially regarding Saturn, where I noticed Copernicus's calculation never eluded the sky by more than half a degree, or at most two-thirds <note>original: "besse unius"</note>; Jupiter sometimes suggests a somewhat larger difference. 

Afterward, in the year 1564, I secretly had a certain astronomical <term>radius</term> <note>A cross-staff, an instrument used for measuring the angle between two celestial bodies.</note> made for me from wood, according to the prescription of **GEMMA FRISIUS**. It was diligently graduated with transversal points <note>A technique for reading scales with higher precision by using diagonal lines.</note> derived from his teacher Homelius by **BARTHOLOMEW SCULTETUS**, who was also living in Leipzig at the time and was familiar to me because of our shared studies. Having obtained this radius, I thereafter worked strenuously on observations of the stars whenever a pleasing clarity of the sky was granted, and often spent entire nights from the window of my room while my tutor slept and was unaware...

<summary>Tycho Brahe recounts his early life and his secret transition from legal studies to astronomy at Leipzig, highlighting how he discovered the massive inaccuracies in existing astronomical tables during the 1563 Jupiter-Saturn conjunction.</summary>
<keywords>Tycho Brahe, Leipzig, Jupiter-Saturn conjunction 1563, Alphonsine Tables, Prutenic Tables, Cross-staff, Peder Oxen, Beata Bille, George Brahe, astronomical instruments, Copernicus</keywords>

--- Page 83 ---
<meta>This page continues Tycho Brahe’s autobiographical narrative, focusing on his early travels across Europe, the appearance of the "New Star" of 1572, and the founding of his observatory, Uraniborg, on the island of Hven under the patronage of King Frederick II.</meta>

...of a certain wide [instrument], I was vigilant in using them, and I recorded them separately in a certain little book which I still preserve. Shortly after, I noticed that the angles of distances, taken in various ways according to the equal divisions of the <term>Radius</term> <note>Also known as a cross-staff, used for measuring the angle between two celestial bodies.</note> and related into numbers geometrically through the rule of proportions, were consistent with each other in all respects. Therefore, having tracked down the cause of the error, I discovered a Table by which the defects of that Radius could be corrected. At that time, I did not have the convenience to see that a new one was made. For my <term>Pedagogue</term> <note>A private tutor or guardian. Tycho often complained that his early handlers tried to steer him toward law and away from astronomy.</note>, who managed my money, did not allow such things to be manufactured for me.

Thus, I carried out many observations with that Radius as long as I stayed in Leipzig, and later when I was recalled to my homeland. Then, going back to Germany, first at *Wittenberg* and soon at *Rostock*, they attended to the stars as much as possible. Around the year 1569 and the following year, while staying at *Augsburg* <note>original: "Augusta Vindelicorum"</note>, besides that very large <term>Quadrant</term> <note>A large instrument used to measure the altitude of stars.</note> which we constructed in the Consul's garden outside the city (of which I spoke above), I frequently observed the stars with another instrument, namely a wooden <term>Sextant</term> devised by us there, and recorded them in a special book. This I also did later, having returned home again, with another similar but somewhat larger one—especially when that wonderful <term>New Star</term> <note>The supernova of 1572 in the constellation Cassiopeia, which proved the heavens were not "unchanging."</note> which appeared in the year 1572 recalled me to the consideration of heavenly things. At that time, I was sweating a great deal over <term>Pyronomic</term> <note>From the Greek 'pyr' (fire); Tycho used this term for his chemical and alchemical experiments involving furnaces.</note> labors, which I had begun in Augsburg and was then continuing. I observed this star diligently and described it faithfully and accurately, first in a small book at that time, and finally in a complete volume.

Later, I successively had other astronomical instruments made, some of which I carried with me when traveling again through all of Germany and passing through part of Italy, where I did not cease observing the stars during the journeys whenever the opportunity arose. Finally, having returned to my homeland around my 28th year, I was preparing soon after for another longer departure in secret. For I had decided to choose *Basel* <note>original: "Basileae Rauracorum"</note> or its vicinity as my home to lay the foundations for the restoration of astronomy. That place pleased me above others in Germany, both because of its famous University and the distinguished learned men there, as well as the healthiness of the air and convenience of living; and because Basel was at the junction of three great regions of Europe: Italy, France, and Germany. Thus, I could correspond with many famous and learned men and spread my inventions more widely for public use.

For I foresaw that I could not cultivate these studies comfortably or safely in my homeland, especially if I stayed in *Scania* <note>A province in what is now southern Sweden, then part of Denmark.</note> at my seat of Knudstrup, or elsewhere in Denmark, where there would be a frequent crowd of nobles and friends who would be a hindrance, interrupting my philosophical leisure. But it happened that while I was turning these things over in my mind in secret, and was already girding myself for the journey as if I were not doing so, the most Serene and Powerful King of *Denmark* and *Norway*, **Frederick II**, of most praised memory, sent one of his noble pages to Knudstrup with royal letters, which commanded me to come to him immediately wherever he might be found in *Zealand*.

After I had appeared quickly, that excellent and never-sufficiently praised King, of his own accord and gracious will, offered me that island of the famous Danish Sound, called **Hven** <note>original: "Huenna"</note> by our people (which a Latin speaker might call *Venusia*, and foreigners call *Scarlatina*). He asked me to build buildings there and instruments for astronomical exercises and also for pyronomic labors, and he graciously promised to provide liberally for the expenses.

So, after deliberating for a while and taking counsel with wise men, I changed my previous intention and not unwillingly acquiesced to the King's will, especially since I saw that on that island, which is situated separately between *Scania* and *Zealand*, I could be free from the noise of interrupters and that the leisure and opportunity I was seeking elsewhere could be granted to me in my homeland, to which we owe the most. Soon, therefore, I began to build the castle of **Uraniborg**, suitable for astronomical matters, in the year 1576; and successively, I completed both the buildings and various astronomical instruments suitable for accurately conducting observations, the main part of which is described and explained in this book.

Meanwhile, I also watched over the observations vigorously, having called many students to help, gifted with sharp minds and sight, whom I supported there continuously and instructed in these and other philosophical disciplines. Thus, by the goodness of God, it happened that almost no clear night was missed, and we obtained many very exact celestial observations: of the fixed stars, all the planets, and also the comets that shone in the meantime, seven of which we accurately recorded there. These diligent observations were carried out for 21 years; I first wrote them in large volumes and later distributed them separately into individual books for each year. I arranged them so that the fixed stars noted in that year had their place, and all the planets had their own distinct place, starting from the Sun and Moon and passing through the other five planets down to Mercury. For we did not leave even Mercury unobserved, although it is very rarely visible. Indeed, in almost every single year it was diligently noted by us, both in the morning and evening; although the great **Copernicus** excused himself for not observing it because of the excessive inclination of the sphere and the vapors of the Vistula River: yet...

<summary>Tycho Brahe describes his early astronomical training, his move to the island of Hven under King Frederick II's patronage, and the 21 years of meticulous observations conducted at Uraniborg, including the rare tracking of Mercury.</summary>
<keywords>Tycho Brahe, Uraniborg, Hven, Frederick II, Copernicus, astronomical instruments, supernova 1572, Mercury, Augsburg, Basel</keywords>

--- Page 84 ---
<meta>Catchword: Ptolemy. This page continues Tycho Brahe’s critique of his predecessors' observations and his systematic classification of his own lifelong astronomical work, leading into his specific findings on the Sun and Moon.</meta>

...even though we live in a slightly more inclined <term>Sphere</term> <note>Referring to the celestial sphere and the observer's latitude.</note>, and on an island surrounded on all sides by the sea (which is often more vaporous), we have nonetheless observed and measured <insert>Mercury</insert> many times (as has been said). But perhaps the house where **Copernicus** lived was so situated that it did not provide a clear view of the **Horizon** on all sides; therefore, it was less suitable for observations, especially those involving lower altitudes. This was also reported to me by that student of mine <note>original: "Studiosus ille meus" — likely Elias Olsen Morsing.</note>, whom I sent there 14 years ago for the purpose of examining the altitude of the **Pole**. For this reason, Copernicus, lacking his own specific data on Mercury, was forced to borrow some from the book of observations by **Walther**, a disciple of **Regiomontanus** <note>Johannes Müller von Königsberg, a major 15th-century astronomer.</note>, and a citizen of Nuremberg. Although Copernicus did not apply these observations quite faithfully or precisely to his own theories and demonstrations, it would still have been desirable if, for the other planets—which he labored with great daring to restore from his own observations—he had obtained results that were not much more uncertain.

If he had, we would certainly now possess far more correct versions of their <term>Apogees</term> <note>The point in an orbit farthest from the Earth.</note> and <term>Eccentricities</term> <note>The measure of how much an orbit deviates from a perfect circle.</note>, and everything else pertaining to this; and I would have been able to spare myself many years of the greatest and most tireless labors and enormous expenses. Therefore, since I have ready at hand the most select and accurate observations of 21 years, sought from the heavens with various and skillfully crafted instruments (which we have shown in the preceding pages)—to say nothing now of the observations of the 14 preceding years—I guard them as a most rare and precious treasure. I may perhaps eventually make them all public, if Divine mercy grants me time to add even more to them.

From all of this, it is clear that I have been accustomed to observing the stars since my sixteenth year, and I have continued them for almost 35 continuous years up to this point. Among these, however, some are more certain and excellent than others. For those which I performed at **Leipzig** in my boyhood, and up to my twenty-first year, I am accustomed to calling *boyish* and *doubtful*. Those which I obtained afterwards up to my twenty-eighth year, I call *youthful* and *mediocre*. But the third group, which I measured afterwards at **Uraniborg** with those most exact instruments during my more mature age for nearly 21 years (up to the completion of my fiftieth year), I call and judge to be *manly, authoritative, and most certain*. Upon these, I strive with arduous effort to found and build the restoration of astronomy, although some of the observations from previous years also contribute not a little. What we have achieved and worked out so far in these matters by the help of God, and what remains to be executed and perfected in the future by the favor of the same Deity, is as follows:

Regarding the **SUN**, we have before all else restored its motion from the most accurate observations of many years, not only by most carefully examining the **Equinoctial** entries <note>The moments the Sun crosses the celestial equator; the equinoxes.</note>, but also by applying intermediate points and **Solstitial** points, especially in the northern semicircle of the **Ecliptic** <note>The Sun's apparent path across the sky.</note>; for there, the midday Sun is not subject to <term>refractions</term> <note>The bending of light caused by the atmosphere, which makes celestial bodies appear higher than they are.</note>. Therefore, I have verified this many times on both sides, and from this, I have geometrically deduced both the **Apogee** and the **Eccentricity** of the Sun corresponding to these times. In both of these, an evident error crept into the work of the **Alphonsine** tables as well as **Copernicus**, so much so that the Sun’s Apogee in Copernicus’s numbers is nearly three degrees ahead, and the Eccentricity is almost 2 1/9 parts, where the radius of the eccentric circle is 60. In Copernicus, almost a quarter of one part is missing. Even in establishing the simple motion of the Sun, he commits an error of nearly a quarter of a degree in these years. From this, the Alphonsine tables compared with the Copernican ones can be evaluated. I have indeed deduced from here the tables of both uniform motions and <term>Prosthaphaereses</term> <note>Equations of correction used to find the true position of a planet.</note> in exact numbers, so that there is no longer any doubt about the course of the Sun, verified with precision and laid open with suitable numbers. It was absolutely necessary that this be done first for the Sun, since it stands as the rule of celestial motions and describes the Ecliptic, to which all other motions are referred. I also discovered its maximum <term>Obliquity</term> <note>The tilt of the Earth's axis (or the Ecliptic) relative to the equator.</note> from the Equator to be different than what Copernicus and his contemporaries thought, namely 23 degrees and 31 1/2 minutes. This is 3 1/2 minutes greater than their finding, because I took care to account for the refraction of the Sun in its winter position, which they inconsiderately neglected. We have also provided for the Sun with specific tables of Revolutions, and then we added new tables of Declinations and Right Ascensions based on our findings. Moreover, we have addressed its Parallaxes and refractions in unique tables.

In the **MOON**, we have also applied no less diligence so that its errors might be resolved, which are involved in a manifold complexity; they are not so simple or so easily apparent as the ancients and **Copernicus** thought. For the Moon has another certain introduction of inequality according to **Longitude** than was noticed by them. Nor did they denote the proportions of its circuits precisely enough. Furthermore, the Moon exhibits different limits of maximum **Latitude** than those predefined by **Ptolemy**, whom all other astronomers afterwards followed too confidently in this part. Indeed, the Moon changes this latitude itself unequally to the difference of a third of a degree. Nor does it subject those **Nodes**, where its path crosses the Ecliptic, to a uniform motion, as has been thought until now; but in each revolution, it causes them to waver back and forth, and that by a sensible difference which exceeds a reciprocal degree by a small amount on each side. We have both noticed and restored all these things from 16 years of the most diligent observations; among these are 158 **Lunar Eclipses**, accurately recorded from the heavens. For three eclipses are not sufficient to investigate its first inequality, as Pto-

<summary>Tycho Brahe critiques Copernicus’s reliance on second-hand data for Mercury and categorizes his own lifelong observations into three stages of reliability, culminating in his mature work at Uraniborg. He details his corrections to solar theory—specifically regarding refraction and the obliquity of the ecliptic—and lunar theory, where he identifies new complexities in latitude and nodal motion.</summary>

--- Page 85 ---
<meta>Catchword: inva-. This page continues Tycho Brahe’s description of his astronomical reforms, transitioning from his critique of Copernicus’s lunar and planetary data to his own methodology for mapping the Sun, Moon, and stars.</meta>

...Ptolemy, Albategnius <note>The Latinized name of Al-Battani, a 9th-century Arab astronomer who refined the solar year.</note>, and Copernicus were of the opinion; six solar eclipses were also taken into counsel, insofar as they might contribute something to this matter. Furthermore, the Moon—at its quadratures <note>The positions of the Moon when it is 90 degrees from the Sun, such as the first and last quarters.</note>, at its maximum distances from its mean motion, at both <term>Apogee</term> <note>The point in an orbit farthest from the Earth.</note> and <term>Perigee</term> <note>The point in an orbit closest to the Earth.</note>, and at various intermediate points—was explored most frequently and in many ways, so that its intricate motion could be properly established. This task tortured us with the incredible labor of many years. Nevertheless, we finally discovered the principles by which its unstable and multiform wanderings might obey circles and numbers. Therefore, having established a new hypothesis consistent with the appearances, we adapted the numbers for both its regular and irregular motions, not only in longitude but also in latitude; we also handled its <term>Parallaxes</term> <note>The apparent change in the position of a celestial object when viewed from different points.</note> differently than was practiced by Ptolemy and Copernicus. 

As experience itself, agreeing with a single hypothesis, required, we looked ahead; nor did we omit care for lunar <term>refractions</term> <note>The bending of light through the atmosphere, which Tycho was the first to account for systematically.</note>, since without these, other things cannot be exactly distinguished. All these matters, and several others concerning lunar appearances, we have reduced into succinct canons <note>Tables or sets of rules.</note>, intended for calculating and deriving its motions. Therefore, with the path of both luminaries <note>The Sun and the Moon.</note> thus restored so that it corresponds to the celestial appearances themselves, their eclipses as well as other configurations, their manner and timing, can henceforth be most correctly established—something which has been long desired until now. Truly, what has been said so far regarding the restoration of the Sun and Moon (in terms of their motions being analogous to the heavens), these things and more, the **First** chapter of our *Progymnasmata* <note>Tycho's "Introductory Exercises" for the restoration of astronomy.</note> will clearly explain. There, anyone eager for these things will have their wish granted. It remains only in the further consideration of these luminaries that all things be adapted to several centuries and rendered more universal, which can be performed without much trouble, provided that the observations of the ancients and our predecessors, upon which it is necessary to rely, are themselves correct. However, we reserve this fuller and more universal exposition for our work, the *Astronomical Theater*. Meanwhile, those devoted to astronomy will be able to enjoy very usefully the things we have delivered in the cited place of the *Progymnasmata*, and satisfy their desire.

Furthermore, we have now most accurately verified all the **FIXED STARS** <note>original: "STELLAS INERRANTES"</note>, however many are visible to the eye, including even those called of the sixth magnitude, according to both longitude and latitude, down to the very minute and sometimes even half a minute. In this way, a thousand stars have been rectified by us, whereas the ancients numbered no more than 1,022; those living under a <term>right sphere</term> <note>The celestial sphere as seen from the equator, where all stars are visible.</note> could see over 200 more, which always remain hidden to us here; however, in their place, we have restored other tiny stars overlooked by them because of their smallness. We labored in this arduous work for nearly 20 years, for the reason that we wished to test the whole matter accurately with various instruments. Because tiny stars are only seen in winter when the nights are dark and the Moon is hidden, the expectation of many years was required before these things could be duly and sufficiently completed; even around the new moons, when this work should especially be put into practice, the sky was rarely clear. 

By what method we arrived at an exact knowledge of the longitudes of the fixed stars from the <term>Equinox</term> <note>The point where the celestial equator and ecliptic intersect.</note>—using Venus as an intermediary during both its morning and evening appearances with the Sun—and how we proved this in many ways, referring everything back to that star which is brighter overhead and third in number (which we established by deliberate effort as the foundation for the rest, since the two preceding it are less visible), the second chapter of the said *Progymnasmata* will abundantly explain. It will also show the method by which we deduced others from this: first, how by a triple method throughout the whole circuit of the sky, we reduced certain selected stars along the Zodiac and Equator to the exact completion of the entire great circle. I also noticed that there is not as much complexity in the inequality of their longitudes as Copernicus thought. For those things which he imagined regarding this crept in by the fault of both ancient and recent observations. Therefore, the <term>precession of the equinoxes</term> <note>The slow, westward shift of the equinoxes along the ecliptic.</note> in these years is not as slow as he wished. For even now, the fixed stars do not complete one degree in a hundred years, as his calculation holds, but in only 71 ½ years. They have almost always done this before, if the observations of our predecessors are properly limited, with at least a small irregularity falling in from elsewhere by accident; we shall reveal this more broadly in its own time, God willing.

Indeed, I was the first to discover that the latitudes of the fixed stars are altered somewhat by the variation in the obliquity of the ecliptic, and I have demonstrated this with various examples in the said chapter. Thus, we can more than sufficiently testify—and experience itself will support it—that the locations of the fixed stars have been verified by us with the highest and infallible accuracy, so much so that we have proven many of them many times with different instruments, with the results coinciding into one. Nor did we execute this business mechanically, even though the great brass <note>original: "Orichalcicus"</note> globe was at hand. Rather, we reduced all stars to their proper places through the laborious ratios of triangles: as is evident from what we have shown regarding the constellation of Cassiopeia (in which we number 26 stars, twice as many as the ancients) near the end of the same chapter; although we used many other applications of triangles and different reasonings for other stars as seemed more convenient. If our predecessors and the ancients had applied such diligence in denoting the locations of the fixed stars, their table <note>original: "abacus"</note> reaching us from Hipparchus would by no means have been so faulty; it is not even correct in that sixth part of a degree which it alone displays, but often suggests a far greater and intolerable deviation—which even the distances between the stars themselves, which always...

He emphasizes his 20-year commitment to observation and his use of advanced trigonometry and instruments to replace the flawed data of the ancients.</summary>
