--- Page 86 ---
<meta>This page continues Tycho Brahe’s overview of his astronomical reforms. Having discussed the Sun and Moon on the previous page, he now moves to the fixed stars and the five planets, while also addressing the need for southern hemisphere observations and defending his original discoveries against plagiarism.</meta>

...remain unchanged, they clearly show. For in many stars, these positions are far different than the calculations of the ancients demand. Moreover, that all the fixed stars remain at perpetually the same distances from one another is sufficiently proven by those which **Hipparchus** <note>A Greek astronomer (c. 190–120 BCE) who made the first known star catalog.</note> and **Ptolemy** <note>An influential 2nd-century Greco-Egyptian astronomer whose geocentric model dominated for over a millennium.</note> reported to be in a straight line; indeed, that very alignment remains unchanged to this day. Regarding those fixed stars whose longitude and latitude we have restored to the very minute, and even sometimes to half a minute (as was said), we shall provide the **Canonical Exposition** <note>The official, standardized list of star positions.</note> in its own time and place.

Nor were we occupied only with accurately establishing the longitudes and latitudes of the fixed stars, but for certain principal ones, 1,000 in number, we derived the <term>Right Ascensions</term> <note>The celestial equivalent of longitude, measured along the celestial equator.</note> and <term>Declinations</term> <note>The celestial equivalent of latitude, measuring how far north or south an object is from the celestial equator.</note> through the logic of Triangles <note>Spherical trigonometry.</note>, and adapted them for two centuries in such a way that they could satisfy each intermediate year proportionally. Furthermore, we provided a special table for the <term>Refractions</term> <note>The bending of light as it passes through the atmosphere, which makes stars appear higher than they are.</note> of the stars, constructed from long-standing and varied experience. For unless these are guarded against, the true locations of the stars cannot be obtained, especially where they approach the horizon within the twentieth degree of altitude. Therefore, in the star positions corrected by us, we have always provided for the inclusion of refractions where necessary. These refractions—to mention this in passing—behave somewhat differently in the stars than in the Sun. In the stars, they also differ somewhat from those which the Moon produces, as has already been untangled and revealed by us long ago.

Thus, nothing more is desired concerning the fixed stars than that their universal motion <note>Precession, the slow "wobble" of the Earth's axis that changes star positions over centuries.</note> be adapted to all the centuries of the world's age. This would not be difficult to perform accurately, provided that the observations of the ancients in these matters had not been accepted in too broad a manner. Nevertheless, by applying a suitable limitation, I trust that I shall satisfy the students of **Astronomy** in this part as much as possible.

It would be truly desirable if the remaining stars noted in ancient times, which do not appear in our climate <note>Tycho worked in Denmark, where the stars of the far southern sky never rise above the horizon.</note>, were joined to the first thousand verified by me; and also those others which even the ancients living in the region of Egypt could not see, which exist near the <term>Antarctic Pole</term> <note>The South Pole of the sky.</note>. For we have heard from the reports of those who have sailed beyond the Equator that most beautiful stars shine there as well. Regarding the first group, one would have to set out for Egypt or a similar location in Africa, and there diligently mark down as many stars as arise. To obtain the second group, one would have to sail to **Southern America** or another region beyond the Equator where all the stars around the Antarctic Pole are visible, and there establish observations of them. Therefore, if any **Illustrious and Powerful Lords** should not find it burdensome to assist our desires and those of others in one or both of these tasks, they would truly perform something remarkable, to be celebrated by the name of posterity in perpetual memory; since this has been attempted by no one in a proper manner so far as is known, let alone absolutely achieved. I shall not refuse to supply the instruments and means conducive to this end, provided there are those who wish to manage it and provide suitable men for such a laudable business.

Finally, we have not left untouched the errors of the **five remaining PLANETS** <note>Mercury, Venus, Mars, Jupiter, and Saturn.</note>, nor the task of remedying them. In all of these, we have so thoroughly reorganized the <term>Apogees</term> <note>The points where planets are farthest from the center of their orbits.</note> and <term>Eccentricities</term> <note>The measure of how much an orbit deviates from a perfect circle.</note>—which was the primary requirement—as well as their simple motion and the proportions of their orbits and circuits, that they no longer teem with errors as they have until now. We also discovered a certain other inequality still lurking in those very apogees that was not previously perceived. Furthermore, we perceived that the annual circuit—which **Copernicus** explained by the motion of the Earth in a great orbit, and the ancients explained by <term>Epicycles</term> <note>Small circles whose centers move around the circumference of larger circles.</note>—is subject to a certain variation. We have provided for all these and other pertinent matters by a certain unique **Hypothesis** <note>The Tychonic System, where the Sun orbits the Earth and the planets orbit the Sun.</note> discovered and established by us fourteen years ago from the appearances themselves. Certain others—among whom I know of three notable ones—did not blush to shamelessly claim this as their own invention afterward and sell it to others. At the proper time and place, God willing, I shall point out the occasions of these things and restrain and refute their excessive license, and I shall prove so evidently that it is so, that there will be no room for those endowed with sincere judgment to hesitate or contradict. I will, however, spare them if they frankly confess what they have done and restore my things to me. Therefore, I willingly abstain from naming them now.

We also did not allow the <term>Latitudes</term> <note>The distance of a planet north or south of the Ecliptic (the Sun's path).</note> of the planets to remain uncorrected, as our predecessors from Ptolemy onwards did; but attending diligently to them in all five planets throughout their entire circuit, we defined the <term>Limits</term> and other crossings through the **Ecliptic**, so that all things are congruent with the Heaven itself. In these, we evidently noticed that the nodes <note>The two points where a planet's orbit crosses the plane of the Earth's orbit.</note> of the three superior planets, or the limits of their greatest latitudes, are not subject to the motion of the apogees in a conforming way, but possess a motion of their own—if indeed what Ptolemy handed down about these is correct, from whom both the **Alphonsine** <note>The Alphonsine Tables, 13th-century astronomical tables.</note> authors and Copernicus borrowed their data without applying any correction from their own observations. From this it happens that the planets are sometimes in the Southern part of the sky when their numbers show them to be Northern, and vice versa.

There remains, therefore, nothing else to be exhausted in the five wandering stars than that these things—which behave differently regarding Longitudes and Latitudes than the common Tables report—be deduced from the accurate observations taken from the heavens for more than twenty-five years now (to say nothing of the ten prior ones)...

<meta>This page continues Tycho Brahe’s description of his life's work. Having established a new star catalog on the previous page, he now describes how he organized this data into tables and how he used extraordinary celestial events—like the New Star of 1572 and various comets—to challenge ancient Greek physics and redefine the nature of the heavens.</meta>

<sig>G 4</sig>

...having been explored and established through observations, and reduced into new and competent <term>Tables</term> <note>Latin: *Canones*; these are standardized lists or mathematical rules for calculation.</note>, they might be set forth in numbers, of which some beginnings and foundations have already been laid by us. The rest can be completed without difficulty by several <term>calculators</term> <note>In this era, "calculators" were people employed to perform tedious mathematical computations by hand.</note>, and from these, <term>Ephemerides</term> <note>Tables giving the daily positions of celestial bodies.</note> for as many of the following years as desired can be expanded. The same can be provided for the Sun and Moon, whose tables we already have ready at hand; thus, in future times, it can be proven with minimal effort that the motions of the heavenly bodies restored by us agree with the appearances themselves and remain correct in every respect.

Furthermore, it would contribute greatly to the complete embrace of <term>Astronomy</term> to have the <term>Longitudes</term> of terrestrial places properly known, and not just the latitudes. In this area, we have worked diligently so far as was permitted, and we are convinced that we have arranged several locations more correctly. But this business cannot be sufficiently aided unless several lunar <term>Eclipses</term> are designated with equal diligence—specifically regarding the exact moments of time—by different observers in diverse and far-distant places. If in this part too, Kings, Princes, and other illustrious and powerful men living in separate regions of the earth provide liberal and suitable support, they will indeed have performed a great service. Thus, <term>Astronomy</term>, insofar as it requires the differences of terrestrial horizons, will emerge more perfect.

Moreover, while we have attended with untiring study over many years to those perennial bodies of heaven that are as old as the world, we have also scrutinized with no less diligence those additional bodies of that same ethereal world, as many as have shone forth in the meantime. And first, that **New Star**, wonderful beyond measure, which began to appear toward the end of the year 1572 and lasted for 16 months before it ceased entirely to be seen. Regarding that star (I say), we wrote a certain small book while it was still lasting (as we also briefly hinted above) and showed its appearances. After some years, resuming this care, on account of the greatness of the miracle, we composed an entire volume about it, which we chose to insert into the first volume of our <term>Introductory Exercises</term> <note>Latin: *Progymnasmata*; Tycho's major work titled *Astronomiae Instauratae Progymnasmata*.</note> for certain reasons revealed therein. There, I not only clearly present my observations of this astounding star and demonstrate them geometrically, but I also examine with <term>Philosophical liberty</term> <note>A term meaning he is following reason and evidence rather than being bound by the authority of ancient writers.</note> the opinions of as many others as could be known and obtained, and I inquire and bring into the open whether or not they were consistent with the very truth.

Regarding the **Comet** of huge appearance which followed five years later <note>The Great Comet of 1577.</note>, we have prepared a special book, in which we likewise deal sufficiently with it, both from our own observations and conclusions as well as from the opinions of others. To these we join certain <term>Apologies</term> <note>In this context, an "apology" is a formal written defense of a position or theory.</note> pertaining to this matter and more fully elucidating this cometary business; and we intended for all of this to fill the first part of the second volume of the <term>Introductory Exercises</term>. In the other part, we shall hereafter occupy ourselves with the remaining six minor <term>Comets</term>, which we noted with equal diligence in several successive years. Although these are not yet fully finished, the principal and large part of them, which serves for the Demonstrations, is prepared. For those perpetual stars did not leave us leisure to linger too long over these vanishing and quickly passing things. I hope, however, by the favor of Divine goodness, to complete this second part of that second volume shortly. In it, I will clearly demonstrate through all the <term>Comets</term> I have designated—in some cases openly, in others as opportunity allowed—that they all moved in the **Ethereal region** of the world, and by no means in the **Sublunary Air**, as <term>Aristotle</term> and his followers have persuaded us in vain for so many centuries. My reasons for dealing with <term>Comets</term> in the second volume of the <term>Introductory Exercises</term> before I turn to the remaining five <term>Planets</term> (which I intend to treat in the third) are brought forward in the Preface there. The chief of these reasons is that from the <term>Comets</term>, which I prove to be truly ethereal, it can be sufficiently established that the whole heaven is most clear and fluid, and not filled with any hard and real spheres. Indeed, their paths for the most part cross through the ways that any celestial spheres would provide; and consequently, the <term>Hypothesis</term> <note>This refers to the "Tychonic System," Tycho's model of the universe where the planets circle the sun, and the sun circles a stationary earth.</note> devised by us admits of no absurdity, since no penetration of spheres or dimensions occurs where none really exist.

And regarding those things which we have partly completed in **ASTRONOMICAL MATTERS** so far, and those which still remain to be completed, let it suffice to have indicated them thus briefly.

In **ASTROLOGICAL MATTERS** also, which investigate the effects of the stars, we have placed no negligible labor, so that these too, vindicated from errors and superstitions, might be largely consistent with the experience on which they rely. For I consider it less than possible to find an exact system in them that is equal to Geometric and Astronomical truth. However, when I had been more intensely devoted in my youth to this prognostic part of <term>Astronomy</term>, which is prophetic and <term>stochastic</term> <note>Based on conjecture or probability rather than absolute certainty.</note>, and had afterward set it aside because the motions of the stars on which it is founded were not sufficiently understood, until this inconvenience could be remedied—having finally discovered the paths of the stars more exactly—I found upon taking it up again that there is a greater certainty in this knowledge (however much it may be considered vain and fruitless not only by the common people but also by many learned men, and even some mathematicians among them) than one would easily think. This is true both in meteorological influences and predictions, as well as in <term>Genethliacal</term> <note>Pertaining to the casting of birth charts or horoscopes.</note> ones, provided that the times...

<meta>This page continues Tycho Brahe’s overview of his scientific contributions. Having previously discussed his astronomical tables and observations of the heavens, he now turns to his more private work in astrology and alchemy, and the reception of his work by European elites.</meta>

...are properly established, and the motions of the Stars and their entrances into the Heavens are applied in harmony, and the <term>directions</term> <note>Methods used in astrology to calculate future events based on the movement of planets.</note> and <term>revolutions</term> <note>The return of a planet—usually the Sun—to its original position at birth to predict the coming year.</note> are properly administered: In these two matters, we have also constructed another method based on experience itself, different from what has been commonly used until now. But we do not willingly share such **Astrological** matters with others, insofar as we have found many things in them to be proven; for not everyone knows how to use them discreetly, with the necessary circumspection and without <term>superstition</term> or excessive confidence, which should be attributed to no created things. Therefore, we shall publish either none or very few of our discoveries concerning these things. For this reason, let it be enough to have spoken briefly and generally about them now.

Furthermore, I have spent no small effort on **Spagyric** <note>Alchemy, specifically the preparation of medicines through the separation and recombination of elements.</note> preparations or **Pyronomic** <note>The art of managing fire in chemical and alchemical processes.</note> exercises, to mention this here in passing, since the materials they treat are analogous to the Celestial bodies and their influences. For this reason, I am accustomed to calling it **terrestrial Astronomy**. Having been occupied in learning and practicing this since my twenty-third year no less than in Celestial matters, I have thus far experienced much labor and no small expense regarding many things—as much in **Metals** as in **Minerals**, as well as in **Gems** and **vegetables** <note>Latin: *vegetabilibus*; refers generally to plant life or organic matter.</note> and growing things, and other materials belonging to this field. I shall not hesitate to confer candidly about these with Illustrious and Princely men, and other outstanding and Learned men who are affected by such things and have some knowledge of them, and to communicate some things to them as occasion permits, provided I am certain of their goodwill and that they will keep them secret. For it is neither expedient nor right for such things to become common. Nor is it granted to everyone—though many may profess such things—to execute these **mysteries** in the proper manner according to the demands of Nature, harmlessly and usefully.

Lest the following pages remain empty, I have decided to add three Letters which two most excellent men wrote to me concerning the business of Astronomy: one of which is by the most Noble and Distinguished *Jacobus Curtius* <note>Jacobus Curtius (Jakob Kurtz von Senftenau, d. 1594), the Imperial Vice-Chancellor under Rudolf II.</note> of best memory, formerly Vice-Chancellor of the Empire; the other two are by *Giovanni Magini* of Padua <note>Giovanni Antonio Magini (1555–1617), a famous Italian mathematician and astronomer who competed with Galileo for a chair at Bologna.</note>, a most Excellent Mathematician and Astronomer among the Italians. From these, it may be clear to some extent how much hope these men, prevailing in the judgment of this exceptional Art, have conceived of me; many similar letters are reserved in my possession. I thought these alone should be added, lest I seem too excessive in these matters. Truly, so that I may one day satisfy the

<meta>This page transitions from Tycho Brahe’s self-description of his alchemical and astrological pursuits to a testimonial letter from a high-ranking official. It provides external validation of Tycho’s scientific reputation within the Holy Roman Empire.</meta>

### THE FOLLOWING IS A COPY OF A LETTER
#### From the most distinguished and noble Lord
## JACOBUS CURTIUS, FORMER VICE-CHANCELLOR
## OF THE EMPIRE,
#### To
# TYCHO BRAHE IN DENMARK

-> WRITTEN IN THE YEAR 1590 <-

-> MANY GREETINGS <-

I give great thanks to you, noble as well as most learned man, because you have wished not only to address me with your letters, but also to offer me your goodwill so lovingly. Certainly, although I have not seen your face, I have long loved and respected the better part of you—namely, your mind—from your writings, by which you have already consecrated yourself to eternity. For that reason, I have desired to see, honor, and embrace your whole self no less greedily than **those people of old** <note>A reference to a famous anecdote by Pliny the Younger about a man who traveled from Gades (modern Cádiz, Spain) to Rome specifically to see the historian Livy.</note> who traveled from the furthest borders of Spain to the City to see Livy. This most kind address of yours has increased my desire, and increased it so much that, unless the will of the Emperor and the demands of the public office I hold prevented it: as God may love me, neither the distance and hardship of the journey, nor the love and charms of my most pleasant wife and sweetest children <note>original: "pignorum," literally 'pledges,' a common Latin metaphor for children.</note> would restrain me from seizing our mutual friend Thaddaeus Hageccius <note>Tadeáš Hájek (1525–1600), the personal physician to Emperor Rudolf II and a fellow astronomer who acted as a bridge between Tycho and the Imperial court.</note> as a traveling companion and rushing all the way to you in Denmark. I cannot say how much it delights me to wander through all of **Hven** <note>The island of Hven (modern Ven), where Tycho built his observatories Uraniborg and Stjerneborg.</note> with Hageccius—in mind, since we cannot in body—and especially to embrace, even if only in imagination, your most exact astronomical Instruments and all that mathematical apparatus. But what if I were present as a spectator myself, and could see you not just explaining everything, but putting it into practice? But because that may be wished for, yet can scarcely be hoped for at this time (for I have not entirely cast away all hope:), I will set that aside for now and, turning to your letter, I will respond in few words to its main points.

And this first: regarding the fact that you lovingly seek my judgment on your writings, and that you submit to my assessment that controversy you have with certain most learned men—which is not so much a dispute as a friendly contest—I accept that I am granted such honor by you as I would not value more even if Kings and Princes, debating over entire Kingdoms and Provinces, were to appoint me as their Judge. However, neither my learning, which is either nonexistent or truly meager, nor my modesty allows me to take it upon myself to do what you ask and offer. Yet, so that I may not be entirely deaf to such a friendly request, take this: what I think of your writings in general will be evidenced by the **Imperial Privilege** <note>A legal document issued by the Emperor providing copyright protection and official endorsement for Tycho's publications.</note> of His Imperial Majesty, which I send to you joined with this letter. I was the one who advised the Emperor to write it thus, and furthermore, I was the sponsor for the truth of what the Emperor testifies therein. In particular, those things which have been handed down by you and confirmed by the most solid reasons concerning the recent **Comets** <note>Tycho proved that comets were distant celestial bodies, not atmospheric phenomena, which directly challenged the traditional Aristotelian worldview.</note>—as many as you yourself have observed—I think are such that they can no longer even be doubted by learned men. Therefore, I cannot be brought to believe that those who still show themselves doubtful in this matter, and who wish to seem to patronize the authority of **Aristotle** <note>The Greek philosopher whose physics dominated universities and held that the heavens were unchanging and comets were just "vapors" in the upper atmosphere.</note> against demonstrated truth, truly feel that way. Rather, I think they are acting so that, since they have "turned every stone" and sought out everything from every side which might seem to confirm Aristotle's opinion but weaken yours or render it doubtful, they may propose these things to you as if to be examined against a <term>Lydian stone</term> <note>A "touchstone" used since antiquity to test the purity of gold; here used metaphorically for a definitive test of truth.</note>. Thus they provide you an opportunity to dissipate not only all shadows of error but even the lightest little clouds with the Sun of your learning, and to perfect the most exact doctrine of these matters for posterity. If they contend with you with this mind, then truly I...

<summary>A letter from the Imperial Vice-Chancellor Jacobus Curtius to Tycho Brahe expressing deep admiration for Tycho's work, confirming the delivery of an Imperial Privilege, and discussing the ongoing debate over the nature of comets against Aristotelian tradition.</summary>
<keywords>Jacobus Curtius, Tycho Brahe, Thaddaeus Hageccius, Hven, astronomy, comets, Aristotle, Imperial Privilege, 16th-century science</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the letter from Jacobus Curtius to Tycho Brahe. Curtius transitions from expressions of personal admiration to a technical discussion of Tycho’s astronomical theories and instruments, specifically addressing the rivalry between Tycho and the mathematician Nicolaus Raymarus Ursus.</meta>

<meta>catchword: mate,</meta>

I wish them the very best; but if they act otherwise, I certainly consider their stubbornness worthy of hatred. Nevertheless, that which they have extorted from you for the common benefit through their own stubbornness, I embrace—ordering it to be of value to them (or rather, not of value)—no differently than if they had done it with a good intention: I congratulate the literary republic <note>original: "reipublicæ literariæ," often translated as the "Republic of Letters," the international community of scholars.</note> for their sake.

Regarding the earlier comets, which Regiomontanus <note>Johannes Müller von Königsberg (1436–1476), a foundational German mathematician and astronomer.</note> and others observed, I await those things which you are about to publish shortly; until then, I shall suspend my own opinion as to whether all comets are to be believed as **ethereal** <term>ethereal: located in the heavens beyond the Moon, implying they are permanent celestial bodies</term>, or whether some are truly **elemental** <term>elemental: occurring within the Earth’s atmosphere, which was the traditional Aristotelian view</term>.

To those things which have been objected against your new hypotheses thus far, you seem to me to have responded solidly. Nor do I find anything absurd in them, but rather I find all things fitting together most beautifully. However, whether these hypotheses will provide that which is desired in the **Copernican system**—namely, that they exhibit for us the exact positions of the stars for past, present, and future times—must only be judged when that **Great Work**, which you are laboring upon, has come to light. Nor do I doubt that in that work you will have compared, with the greatest diligence, the more exact observations of all ages that exist with your own (which I am certain are the most exact), and from there you will have constructed this new **Machine of the World** <note>original: "mundi Machinam," a common metaphor for the structure of the universe.</note> with truly divine genius.

Take heart therefore, most noble man, and see to it that we may enjoy your divine discoveries as swiftly as possible. For although this is an arduous task and, as you yourself truly write, a work of many years, I hope—indeed, I trust—that you have already exhausted the greatest part of it, and that at this time you are occupied more in recollecting and arranging those things which you have already established and in constructing tables from them, than in further confirming your discoveries. Since you can be aided in this matter in large part by the labor of your disciples, I wish to ask you again and again not to keep us in suspense for too long. I wish for you, from my heart, a life even unto **Nestorian years** <note>A reference to Nestor, the legendary Greek hero of the Trojan War said to have lived for three generations; a common wish for a very long life.</note>: yet you know how deceptive our hopes are, and how immature a death has often befallen especially divine geniuses. Therefore, cheat the delays, and even if by an untimely birth, bring forth this offspring of your genius to the world. If the fates grant you a longer life, which we greedily desire and hope for, you will be able to refine what has been published, and finally satisfy your own judgment as well (for I do not doubt that you will satisfy ours in the first edition). In the meantime, you will free us from the perpetual fear that, while you hesitate too much, we may be frustrated of the entire work. I certainly have conceived such great hope for you and these hypotheses of yours, that I would dare to affirm for certain that a solid rebuilding of the astronomical art is to be expected from you or from no one. Indeed, besides the divine force of your genius, there is no one in Europe (and when I say Europe, I mean the whole world) who can be compared to you, who is equipped with so many and such great things necessary for this task, and who has contributed so many and such great expenses, labors, and years. All flattery is absent from this, as God may love me; both the dignity which I hold and the gravity which I strive to maintain with all people will sufficiently defend me among those known and unknown, especially in praising a name unknown to me by face.

Those things which you have written in various places and to various people concerning the exact construction and use of the instruments necessary for observations have delighted me exceedingly. Since I have thus far been unable to satisfy myself—as I cannot even bear to see, let alone possess, things that are not exact—I lack all instruments entirely, except those which are acquired only for pleasure. The **Nonnian Quadrant** <note>An instrument using the system of concentric scales proposed by Pedro Nunes (Nonius) to allow for more precise readings of fractions of a degree.</note> once kept me very anxious: but because it is most difficult, indeed almost impossible, to construct it, I thought of other means. I found various ones, a part of which **Christopher Clavius** <note>The influential Jesuit mathematician (1538–1612) and primary architect of the Gregorian calendar.</note>, a distinguished mathematician, published in his booklet on describing solar clocks by means of an instrument. At last, the construction of a certain quadrant came to my mind, which, because it seemed not inelegant to certain learned men, I wished to send to you as well. However, to confess frankly what the matter is and what you most truly affirm: all these inventions have less in their reality <note>original: "in recessu," literally 'in the background' or 'in depth'.</note> than they promise at first glance. Nevertheless, it will be pleasing to me to hear your judgment concerning this last invention of mine. From the booklet of **N. N.** <note>Nicolaus Raymarus Ursus (1551–1600), Tycho’s bitter rival who Tycho accused of plagiarizing his Tychonic system.</note>, your **plagiarist**, which he titled *The Astronomical Foundation*, and from its single diagram...

<meta>This page concludes the letter from Jacobus Curtius to Tycho Brahe and begins a technical description of a geometric instrument. Curtius describes a breakthrough in "prosthaphaeresis," a method to simplify complex astronomical calculations using trigonometric identities. Tycho then provides a commentary on Curtius’s design for a high-precision quadrant, noting its ingenuity despite practical manufacturing difficulties.</meta>

...fully completed <note>original: "mate," the end of "consummate," following from the previous page</note>, which he dedicated to Paul **Wittich** <note>Paul Wittich (c. 1546–1586) was a mathematician who collaborated with both Tycho and Curtius on "prosthaphaeresis," a method for replacing multiplication with simpler addition and subtraction.</note>. I have constructed in recent days—since I was unable to attend to public business due to ill health—a new theory of spherical triangles. In this theory, through a table of sines, tangents, and secants, all cases of both right-angled and oblique-angled triangles are performed most easily through only addition and subtraction, without any multiplication or division. I would send it to you as well, if I did not know that you would easily grasp the whole matter just by inspecting that one diagram. For from that diagram and an axiom already demonstrated by many—that the radius is the mean proportional between the right sine of an arc and the secant of its complement—this whole method has been constructed. As for the rest, I pray that you may be well again and again, most Noble sir, for many years, and I most lovingly offer you all the services of love and goodwill that can proceed from me toward you, earnestly asking that you continue to love me, who loves you from the heart, as you have already begun to do of your own accord. Given at Prague, **June 28, 1590.**

->**Jacobus Curtius von Senftenau.**<- <note>Jacobus Curtius (1553–1594) served as the Imperial Vice-Chancellor to Holy Roman Emperor Rudolf II.</note>

---

*Because the description of the Quadrant <term>Quadrant: an instrument for measuring angles up to 90 degrees, essential for determining the altitude of stars</term>, which he mentions in his letter, was attached, it is pleasing to include it here as well. This is because it is ingenious and excels over other inventions of its kind, both in name and in its other results. Nevertheless—as Lord Curtius himself candidly admits—such subtleties do not always possess in their deep inner workings what they promise at first glance. In this, he agrees with what is asserted on this matter in the first part of the second volume of the* Introduction to the New Astronomy <note>original: "Progymnasmatum." Tycho refers to his own major work, the *Astronomiae Instauratae Progymnasmata*.</note>, *page 461. For besides the fact that intricate and laborious subdivisions sometimes easily introduce some hidden flaw, this inconvenience is also admitted: that the parts of the Quadrant become smaller the closer they are to the center. Therefore, they are less capable of subdivisions, and if the Ruler <note>The "alidade" or sighting arm used to align the instrument with a star.</note> itself does not exactly show a straight line everywhere it passes, or if it cuts a point in the middle (which is difficult to discern), the labor here is in vain—to say nothing of other inconveniences. For this reason, our method, which is located near the rim and circumference of the Quadrant and does not occupy much space, and can be easily prepared, is far more efficient and certain. After all, things that can be accomplished with fewer requirements do not need more. However, it was permissible to subjoin this very ingenious method of Lord Curtius here, even if it is not suitable enough for practice, so that this most excellent man may be granted his own invention, and others (as often happens) do not claim it for themselves; and so that I might, in some way, gratefully honor his memory and the praise of his extraordinary talent even after his death. It is as follows:*

### FOLLOWS THE DESIGN OF THE SUBDIVISIONS OF THE QUADRANT.

->Ingeniously invented by the most Distinguished and Noble Lord:<-
->**CURTIUS, VICE-CHANCELLOR OF THE EMPIRE**<-

**W**ithin any quadrant divided most exactly into ninety degrees, let fifty-nine other quadrants be described <note>These are concentric arcs drawn inside the main frame of the instrument.</note>. And in the one that immediately follows the outermost quadrant, let an arc of sixty-one degrees be taken and divided into sixty equal parts; or let an arc of thirty and a half degrees be taken and divided into thirty equal parts. In either case, each of those parts will contain one degree and one minute. We use only the first of these parts, the rest being omitted as if they were not in the quadrant; for that reason, that division should be made secretly, or—what we would approve more—let that division be made in some other quadrant, and from it let one of those parts be transferred into this one which we have constructed for use, lest the parts to be described afterward be confused with the previous ones.

From the endpoint of this first part, let its radius <note>original: "semidiameter"</note> be transferred into the quadrant, and let the arc which it subtends be divided into sixty equal parts; each of these parts will then be one degree or sixty minutes, for the radius of any circle subtends a sixth part of the circle, that is, sixty degrees. Then, from the endpoint of this arc, twenty-eight of these parts are transferred into the remainder of the quadrant, and they will be equal parts, each of which...

--- Page 92 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical description of the graduation of an astronomical quadrant. The previous page ended with the word "singulas" (singular/each), appearing here as the suffix "-gulas." The text explains a sophisticated method for dividing a large instrument into very fine increments (minutes) using sixty concentric scales.</meta>

...each <note>original: "-gulas," the conclusion of "singulas" from the previous page</note> to contain eighty-eight degrees, to which if you add that first part which we showed contains one degree and one minute, eighty-nine degrees and one minute will result. The remaining part, therefore, which is left until the end of the quadrant, will contain fifty-nine minutes.

In the next quadrant, which immediately follows this one, let an arc of sixty-two degrees be taken and divided into sixty equal parts—or let an arc of thirty-one degrees be divided into thirty equal parts—and each of these parts will contain one degree and two minutes. We use only the first of these parts, omitting the rest. From the end of this first part, the radius <term>semidiameter: the distance from the center of a circle to its edge; in a quadrant, it spans an arc of exactly 60 degrees</term> is again transferred onto the quadrant, and the arc it subtends is divided into sixty equal parts. Twenty-eight of these parts are then carried into the remainder of the quadrant, and we will again have eighty-eight whole degrees. If we add the first part, which contains one degree and two minutes, an arc of eighty-nine degrees and two minutes will result, and that final part which remains until the end of the quadrant will be fifty-eight minutes. For the third quadrant, we shall take from the beginning an arc of sixty-three degrees and divide it into sixty equal parts—or half of it into thirty equal parts—and having taken the first part from there and omitted the rest, it will contain one degree and three minutes. We shall perform the rest in the same way as was done in the first and second quadrants.

For the fourth quadrant, an arc of sixty-four degrees is to be taken; for the fifth, an arc of sixty-five degrees; and so one must proceed, always taking an arc one degree larger for the following quadrant, until the fifty-ninth quadrant. For that one, an arc of one hundred and nineteen degrees must be assumed and divided, as before, into sixty equal parts—or an arc of fifty-nine and a half degrees is to be divided into thirty equal parts—and each of these parts will contain one degree and fifty-nine minutes. From the end of this first part, omitting the rest, the radius must again be transferred into the quadrant, and the arc subtended by it divided into sixty equal parts. Twenty-eight of these are to be carried into the remainder of the quadrant, and we will again have eighty-eight whole degrees. When that first part is added to these, eighty-nine degrees and fifty-nine minutes will result, and accordingly the remaining part, which is left until the end of the quadrant, will contain one minute. By this method of dividing these quadrants, let *0* be inscribed on the first quadrant, which we divided into ninety equal parts. For into whichever part of that quadrant the fiducial line <term>linea fiducię: the straight, calibrated edge of an instrument's alidade or sighting mechanism</term> falls, that arc will contain precisely the whole degrees and nothing further. On the next quadrant following this, let *1* be inscribed. For into whichever part of this quadrant the fiducial line points, it shows one minute. On the following quadrant let *2* be inscribed, and on the one following that *3*, then *4*, and so we shall proceed until the innermost quadrant, to which *59* must be inscribed. Because into whichever of the parts of this innermost quadrant the fiducial line falls, that arc will contain fifty-nine minutes beyond the whole degrees.

This quadrant actually and physically displays five thousand four hundred parts, namely all the primary minutes <note>original: *scrupula*, literally "small sharp stones," a term used for the first sexagesimal divisions of a degree</note> which are contained in ninety degrees. Its use, moreover, is very easy. When the fiducial line, or the thread of the plumb line <term>perpendiculum: a weighted cord used to ensure the instrument is perfectly vertical</term>, falls upon some whole part of any of these quadrants, always add to the whole degrees—which the fiducial line or the plumb-thread shows—as many minutes as are inscribed on the side of that quadrant, and the number of degrees and minutes contained in the cut-off arc will be produced. For example: 

Let the fiducial line fall on the forty-fourth part of that quadrant to which thirty-five minutes are inscribed on both sides; the arc cut off by the fiducial line will therefore contain forty-four whole degrees and, in addition, thirty-five minutes.

<summary>This page describes the geometric construction of a high-precision quadrant using sixty concentric arcs to represent minutes. By offsetting each arc slightly, the user can read both degrees and minutes (up to 5,400 individual minutes) with great accuracy.</summary>
<keywords>astronomical quadrant, graduation, geometric construction, minutes, fiducial line, plumb line, radius, degrees, 5400 parts</keywords>

<meta>The previous page concluded a technical mathematical description of how to divide an astronomical quadrant into sixty concentric scales to achieve high precision. ->TRANSCRIPT OF A LETTER<-

->Of the most Illustrious and Distinguished Mathematician Master<-

# GIOVANNI ANTONIO MAGINI OF PADUA
## Bologna, in the same year 1590.

->TO<-
### TYCHO BRAHE IN DENMARK
->written.<-

->ILLUSTRIOUS SIR.<-

<note>Large decorative initial A begins the text.</note>

I received your most learned book sent to me in your name by a certain Danish student who was formerly a member of your household. Nothing more pleasing could have been presented to me by you, as I am accustomed to searching out such writings with the greatest curiosity and reading them most gladly. For I had scarcely hoped it would reach my hands by any other way, since very few volumes printed there are usually imported into Italy due to the long distance and the difficulty of transport. To this is added that you have clearly signaled how much you value me by this most elegant gift, for which reason I give you the greatest thanks I can. For that which I greatly desire and usually strive for—namely, that a path might open to the favor of men like yourself—you have offered to me of your own accord through your singular kindness.

The reason I was not able to express this my feeling toward you immediately by letter, as was fitting and as I wished, was that I had decided to send you, along with my letter, a work recently composed by me that was soon to be published. However, last summer, when I had fallen ill, I diligently arranged with that same student in a letter written to him so that he would not find it burdensome to ask from you in my name those things which I subjoin here.

I would therefore greatly desire to have a concise description of the fixed stars as they have been corrected by you, so that it might be of use to me in my Ephemerides <term>Ephemerides: tables giving the predicted positions of celestial bodies over time</term>, which are soon to be returned to the press. Furthermore, I seek some knowledge of the eccentricities of the planets, as well as the measurements of each of their orbits, if you have found them to differ from those of the Copernicans and have corrected them. If you will not disdain to gratify me, I shall certainly ensure that you do not regret your kindness toward me. For with that integrity which becomes refined men and professors of the liberal arts, I have decided to praise you in my writings as the author of your own discoveries.

As for my feeling and judgment regarding that remarkable work of yours concerning the Comet seen in the year 1577, do not be without it: consider it thus, most Distinguished Sir. Since I have perceived in it your incredible diligence and accurate method of observation, I am persuaded that you will be able to correct the motions of the heavenly bodies most exactly, and that you will most abundantly satisfy the greatest zeal and expectation of everyone with the fruit of that work, from which everlasting glory shall be joined to your name. I would wish, however, that you apply yourself especially to observing and examining the motion of Mars, since it is commonly believed that it cannot be observed exactly; and I am of the "heresy" <note>original: "hæreſi" — Magini uses this term figuratively to mean a controversial or dissenting scientific opinion.</note> to think that its eccentricity is changed and has its own period, so that a different equation of eccentricity must be introduced for it. Otherwise, if a table is made for the maximum eccentricity, it will not be able to serve for the minimum, and vice-versa. From this we see that Copernicus (even though he detected that this kind of eccentricity varied) did not therefore build tables that would suit individual times, since the equations of Mars calculated for the eccentricity of 5 parts, 51 minutes, and 30 seconds observed by Copernicus can by no means be adapted to the times of Ptolemy himself, in which he defined the eccentricity of Mars as 6 parts.

I cannot but greatly approve of the System of the Universe devised by you, although I would wish that the orbit of the Sun and that of Mars did not intersect at all. But if, as I learned from that student, it has been observed by you that Mars at its acronychal point <term>acronychal: when a planet is opposite the Sun and visible all night; at this time, it is closest to Earth</term> approaches closer to the Earth than the Sun does, then an intersection of this kind must be admitted by all means. I am in great expectation of your observations and speculations of this kind, which I shall not be ashamed to both excel in and follow. And if in constructing Ephemerides and resolved tables from the Copernican method and the Prutenic tables <term>Prutenic Tables: astronomical tables based on the work of Nicolaus Copernicus, widely used in the late 16th century</term>...

H
<meta>catchword: ...broadly [latum]</meta>

<summary>This page contains a 1590 letter from Giovanni Antonio Magini to Tycho Brahe, praising Brahe's work on the 1577 comet and requesting his corrected data on stars and planetary orbits. Magini specifically discusses the difficult problem of Mars's eccentricity and expresses interest in Tycho's new geo-heliocentric model of the universe.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the correspondence from the Italian mathematician Giovanni Antonio Magini to the Danish astronomer Tycho Brahe, concluding a letter from September 1590 and beginning a second letter originally published as a preface to Magini’s mathematical tables.</meta>

...did not deviate so much as a fingernail’s breadth <note>original: "latum quidem unguem deflexi"—a Latin idiom meaning to not stray even the slightest amount.</note>. I also hope, as I vehemently desire, to read this year your first book on the phenomena of the ethereal world <note>Brahe's *De Mundi Aetherei Recentioribus Phaenomenis* (1588), which discussed the comet of 1577 and his new system of the world.</note>, which you promise to publish again. The aforementioned student indicated to me that you were seeking Werner’s book on the motion of the eighth sphere <note>Johannes Werner (1468–1522); his work on the "eighth sphere" (the fixed stars) was a subject of intense debate regarding the movement of the equinoxes.</note>, which, although you searched throughout all of Germany, you were never able to find. Therefore, I have given him one copy so that he might transmit it to you in my name. If there is anything else I can do for your sake, I would consider it a great honor to receive your commands, as I wish for everything to turn out happily for you and for your interests to be best served. This year I am to publicly interpret my hypotheses of the celestial orbs: which will be a good occasion for me to explain at much greater length (as I have already begun to do) those things I have published in this field, and to illustrate them with commentaries, geometric demonstrations, and calculations. However, before these commentaries are made public, I would gladly wish to know and understand your labors, so that I might also include and explain your suppositions within them. And lest I be more troublesome to you with excessive length, I shall make an end of writing here, asking you again and again to look upon my respect for you with a benevolent mind and to take it in good part. Bologna, on the Ides of September, 1590. <note>September 13, 1590.</note>

->To your illustrious lordship,<-

->Your most devoted,<-

->**Giovanni Antonio Magini of Padua.**<-

---

*Another letter from the same most learned Magini, prefixed to a certain book which he titled the "Tetragonic Table," dedicated to me. Although it was published six years ago and sent by Magini himself, I did not receive it; but afterwards, having been found by chance at a bookseller's, one of my students of astronomy brought it to me.*

->To the man of most illustrious nobility and outstanding erudition<-
# TYCHO BRAHE, THE DANE, LORD of KNUDSTRUP,
## and founder of the Citadel of Uraniborg on the Island of the Danish Hellespont, Hven.
->founder.<-
## GIOVANNI ANTONIO MAGINI OF PADUA, MATHEMATICIAN,
->sends his many greetings.<-

For many years now, I have thought long and hard (most noble and learned man) about how I might find an expeditious and easy way by which the square root of any number, however large, could be gathered at a moment's notice. It is truly an arduous and very difficult task, in which no one will plainly believe how much I have sweated, unless they have sometimes made the attempt themselves; indeed, the difficulty of the matter often deterred me from my purpose. But in truth, the matter finally succeeded so happily for me that I found more than I could have hoped for: for what I have achieved in this kind of calculation, you first, most learned Brahe, and then all other cultivators of mathematics into whose hands these things may come, will clearly perceive. However, I do not wish to appear too bold to students here in praising my own inventions, lest some carping critic <note>original: "momus"—referring to Momus, the Greek god of mockery and unfair criticism.</note> mock me for being an immodest herald of my own praise. Yet, whatever kind of work this may be, I wished to dedicate it to you, a man of most glorious name and the most eminent astronomer of our age, for the sake of a grateful mind and singular respect toward you, and for the greatest of reasons. For first, it is you to whom I owe the most, because you opened the doors of your friendship to me so lovingly and kindly, and granted me more honor than my thin and very meager erudition (if there is any) demanded, when you sent me your second book on the more recent phenomena of the world via a certain Danish student, and simultaneously asked through him what I thought of them, and also because you responded most kindly to my letter, discussing many things with me regarding astronomical matters. Secondly, you are the one who I am most confident will welcome this Tetragonic Table <note>A "Tetragonic Table" is a table of squares (*tetragonos* being Greek for square). These were used as a mathematical shortcut for multiplication and finding roots before the common use of logarithms.</note> of mine, because you will be able to use it most successfully to derive the angles of the inequalities of the planets' motions, and indeed by a readier shortcut than through tables of tangents and secants. Finally, because you are that most outstanding restorer of astronomy, to whom our age owes the most, and to whom all posterity will be indebted, since not only I, but almost all men of sincere mind have conceived such hope from your astronomical foundations, that from you alone they expect the restoration of astronomy...

<meta>The text breaks off here with the catchword "tionem" (the end of "restitutionem," or restoration) to be continued on the next page.</meta>

Magini praises Brahe as the primary "restorer" of astronomy and explains how his new mathematical tables will simplify planetary calculations.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the correspondence from the Italian mathematician Giovanni Antonio Magini to the Danish astronomer Tycho Brahe. Magini is encouraging Brahe to publish his comprehensive astronomical work and providing updates on his own instrumental observations in Italy.</meta>

<sig>H 2</sig>

...they eagerly await this illustration. Indeed, you have devised such representations <note>original: "hypotyposes"—these are the conceptual models or sketches of the celestial system</note> of the celestial spheres with wondrous skill and invention, that there is no doubt they can properly suffice to preserve the phenomena and appearances observed in the heavens, and to define the locations of the celestial lights at any given time. I therefore urge you, and beseech you—by this small gift I have offered—that you finally complete what you have so fortunately begun and have been laboring upon for so much time in this branch of learning. For I judge rightly and sincerely, as all hope, that no other person can provide this more correctly or successfully than you, who were born with the sharpest talent for the illustration of **collapsed astronomy** <note>original: "Astronomiæ collapsæ"; a common phrase among Renaissance astronomers who believed the ancient science of Ptolemy had fallen into ruin and needed a complete overhaul based on new, accurate data.</note>. Truly, if you communicate your most laborious and excellent *Theatrum* <note>Referring to Brahe's projected massive work on astronomy, often titled the *Theatrum Astronomicum*.</note> to us as soon as possible, you will surely snatch all glory from those who once attempted the correction of the celestial motions, and those who attempt it now; and you will seize every occasion and material from those who would attempt it in the future.

I know indeed that after the publication of your labors, my own writings will require correction; but so far from these things disturbing me, I rather desire that your speculations be made public as soon as possible. For I shall immediately correct my *Ephemerides* <term>Ephemerides: printed tables giving the daily predicted positions of the sun, moon, and planets</term>, the second edition of which is eagerly expected, or, if there is need, I shall weave together others anew. Let it also be permitted to signify to you through this letter that I have thus far omitted the investigation of the pole at Padua and Bologna, and the meridian altitudes of Mars and those fixed stars which you so greatly wished me to observe. I would not wish to be accused of negligence by you on this account, since the opportunity for performing this was snatched from me by various waves of business which overwhelmed me, added also to the inconvenience of the location, especially at Padua, regarding which that same man can testify to you. I have correctly perceived that the **astronomical sextant**, which I had constructed at Padua according to your invention and for your sake, corresponds most exactly to the heavens down to the individual minutes, since I discovered the same distances of several fixed stars to the exact hair <note>original: "ad unguem"—literally "to the fingernail," meaning with perfect precision.</note> as those you noted in your catalog from your own observations. And I have now carried this instrument with me to Bologna, where I shall attempt to complete many observations for your benefit. 

Furthermore, I do not wish this to be hidden from you: that the most noble and learned man **Paolo Bonfiglio**, a patrician of Bologna and most devoted to you, has had a **ten-foot Quadrant** <term>Quadrant: a large instrument for measuring the altitudes of stars, shaped like a quarter-circle</term> made of wood and metal at his own expense, which will show not only minutes, but parts of minutes in observations. The same noble man is also eager to have a **Sextant** of the same size, with which instruments we may more conveniently perform celestial observations for your sake in the highest tower which he has in his house; concerning all of which things you shall be made more certain in the proper time and place. Here I shall put an end to this letter, earnestly asking that if I can do anything for you, you may use me as is your right. Farewell, most illustrious man and the glory of astronomers; continue to perfect astronomy with your nightly studies and vigils, and

->adorn the Sparta which you have received,<-
-><note>The idiom "Adorn your Sparta" means to do one's duty and fulfill the task one has been given.</note> as they say. And again, long and<-
->happily farewell.<-

->Bologna, on the Calends of February [February 1st], 1592.<-

He also reports on the construction of large-scale astronomical instruments in Bologna intended to assist in Brahe's research.</summary>

<meta>This page follows a letter from the Italian mathematician Giovanni Antonio Magini to Tycho Brahe. It transitions from Magini’s personal correspondence to a collection of celebratory poems and academic news from Italy, specifically mentioning the arrival of Galileo Galilei at the University of Padua.</meta>

Subjoined to this letter were certain poems by eminent men among the Italians; among which space allows for the addition of these by the most excellent Andreas Chioccus.

### To the most Noble and Learned man
## TYCHO BRAHE OF URANIBORG
### founder, and restorer of Astronomy.

<note>original: "Uraniburgi"—Uraniborg, or 'Castle of the Heavens,' was the groundbreaking observatory built by Tycho Brahe on the island of Hven.</note>

While you are carried around, seized by celestial love, following the rapid orbs of the Heavens and the wandering stars, recounting the various courses of the Sun and the labors of the Moon, as well as the Throne of high Jupiter and the realms of the Paphian Goddess <note>original: "Paphiæ Deæ"—a poetic reference to Venus.</note>; then how the Cyllenian fire <note>original: "Cyllenius ignis"—a poetic reference to Mercury.</note> permeates through the opposing orbs and joins the other lights to itself, shining freely with shared splendor; and how the cold stars of Saturn and the stars of bristling Mars wander as enemies through the ethereal recesses—you, great BRAHE, do not seem so much to survey the walls of the flaming world with a wise mind, nor to weave an extraordinary work of celestial reason upon your papers, as you seem to have opened the heavenly houses through the immense void with your very body. These houses, having been scrutinized with much labor, you then reveal as magnificent doctrines from your breast, by which future generations may rise in this fierce age. From here, an admirer of Olympus from your sublime watchtower, you kindle your immortal soul with love for the celestial Fatherland, and you teach us to despise the vile earth. Then you are above even fate, and above its gifts, leading the life of the Gods; nor do mortal deaths touch you, nor the tricks or monsters of a fleeting life.

Success to your courage, great BRAHE, you who are able to know a work greater in such a pivotal moment than it is to cut through dense ranks of troops with a victorious right hand, or to lead captive Kings under your banners, and to have joined at once to a great triumph the ample spoils of barbarian garments shimmering with gold and purple, Mygdonian <note>A poetic term for Phrygian or Eastern.</note> bows, quivers, and Lycian arrows—having conquered the enemy at the Caucasian gates, or surely trapping them trembling in distant Bactria. All-consuming time scatters all things into thin breezes, sipping away famous deeds in a death-like sleep; only the Virtue of the soul climbs high Olympus, and does not fear the contagion of cruel Lachesis <note>One of the three Fates in Greek mythology, specifically the one who measures the thread of life.</note>.

->Andreas Chioccus of Verona.<-

->TO HIM<-
<note>original: "ἘΙΣ ἈΥΤῸΝ"—this poem was originally written in Greek.</note>

Brahe shows the rules of elegance and the skillfully crafted movements of the circles with harmony. Though the work was once labored over in every part of the Ethiopian lands <note>A reference to ancient astronomical traditions often attributed to the Egyptians or "Aethiopians."</note>, no one has yet taught so many things so clearly. If, therefore, it is right to say anything of heavenly words, having learned the best laws of the sacred dwelling: surely it would have been better for you to have come here from the Immortals, you who alone were taught the genuine works of the gods. But it is a beautiful thing for a learned man to envy those bound to the earth and to draw the chorus of the stars down toward them with a far-reaching reach.

->By the Same.<-

---

Because some space still remains, the printer requested that the following small items be subjoined, excerpted from the letters of a certain Doctor of Medicine staying in Padua, sent six years ago to a certain studious Dane.

MAGINI stayed **here in Padua and Venice** for almost the entire summer. For what reason is not entirely clear. Meanwhile, **Galileo Galilei of Florence** obtained the Chair of Mathematics here, who began his lectures on the **seventh of December**. The beginning was splendid, with a great frequency of listeners. He is liberally recommended by **Lord Pinello** <note>Gian Vincenzo Pinello (1535–1601), a famous Italian scholar and mentor whose house was a center of intellectual life in Padua.</note>, who, if he could, would very gladly introduce him into the friendship of **Lord Tycho**. You, who know the mind of **Tycho**, will be able to arrange in these matters what will be beneficial. **Magini** recently published a book, to which he gave the title (**The Tetragonic Table**) <note>original: "Tabula Tetragōnica"—Magini's 1592 work on calculating the areas of squares and other geometric figures.</note> under the patronage of **Tycho**. He gave me a copy to send to you, which I will transmit at the first opportunity. He also reported that the **Most Illustrious Venetians**, in the council of the *pregadi* <note>The Senate of the Republic of Venice.</note>, deliberated that someone skilled in Mathematics should be sent to **Egypt** with a salary of 300 Crowns to make observations there on behalf of **Tycho**. For indeed, **Tycho** is of such great celebrity here as no one else among those who are now living. **Dated at Padua, 28 December, the Year 1592.**

<meta>This page continues Tycho Brahe’s response to Giovanni Antonio Magini. Having acknowledged Magini’s previous letters and the poems included with them, Brahe now discusses the state of astronomy in Italy and proposes a scientific expedition to Egypt to resolve long-standing questions about the Earth’s axial tilt and the accuracy of ancient observations.</meta>

<sig>H 3</sig>
<meta>Catchword: I remind [original: monem,]</meta>

**From** which, therefore, I understand from the letters of that most celebrated **MAGINI** <note>Giovanni Antonio Magini (1555–1617) was an Italian astronomer and cartographer who maintained a significant correspondence with Tycho Brahe.</note> mentioned before, that there are in Italy certain magnificent and excellent men, devoted to astronomical matters, who do not think they should neglect to prepare instruments even in imitation of ours, and to subtly perfect the observations of the celestial bodies with them; this is indeed most pleasant for me to hear. Nor do I doubt that distinguished utility for the expansion of the Art of Astronomy can flow from this: since they live in a more upright sphere <note>Meaning a lower latitude, which offers a better view of certain celestial bodies compared to Tycho’s northern location.</note> than we do, are endowed with remarkable skill, and moreover possess the wealth with which they can easily sustain these expenses. I would truly wish that those things which have so far been detected by them in the heavens be shared with me in a timely manner. I think this can now be done more conveniently, since I am staying in Germany and at this time near **HAMBURG**, a famous marketplace, which also has much commerce with the Italians; I am staying in a certain **RANZOVIAN** <note>original: "RANZOVIANA"—referring to Wandsbek Castle, owned by Tycho’s patron, the wealthy Danish nobleman Heinrich Rantzau.</note> fortress. Or even if I should perhaps be elsewhere in **Germany** <note>original: "Teuthonia"</note> hereafter, whatever it may be will easily find me.

But most of all, I would wish with whatever prayers and devoted mind I can, that what the most illustrious **VENETian** Lords had decided several years ago with a heroic and liberal purpose (as I perceived from the letters I just mentioned), even now be put into execution—if it has been interrupted until now due to some intervening delays (as sometimes happens). This purpose was that some young man, intelligent and skilled in these matters, should be sent to the city of **EGYPT**, formerly called **ALEXANDRIA**, now **CAIRO**, <note>Tycho conflates or associates the two cities as the administrative and intellectual heart of Egypt.</note> who would there, above all, most accurately observe the elevation of the Pole <note>term: "elevation of the Pole" — effectively the latitude of the location.</note> both from the Sun when it is at the very Meridian, as well as from the stars crossing the Meridian toward the South and the North. This should be done using the motion of the Sun corrected by us, and our verification of the fixed stars.

This is desired especially for this reason: so that it can be established for certain whether the height of the Pole <note>latitude</note> nods <note>tilts or shifts</note> slightly over a long tract of time or not; so that the individual horizons of the Earth do not always perceive the same thing after many intervals of centuries. Some excellent mathematicians, even among the Italians themselves, suspect this for no slight reasons. Then also, those things which the great **COPERNICUS** ingeniously imagined regarding the intricate motion of the Earth's axis, in order to save <note>term: "save" — a technical term in early science meaning to provide a mathematical explanation that accounts for observed phenomena.</note> the change of the obliquity of the Ecliptic <note>term: "obliquity of the Ecliptic" — the angle of the Earth's tilt relative to its orbit, which affects the seasons.</note>, might also be examined in a certain way. Although (to say what the matter seems to demand) even if the altitude of the pole above any horizon remains exactly the same forever, his hypothesis in this part could still stand, provided other things do not stand in the way. However, to dispute subtly about these things now and to interpose my judgment is not our purpose, nor does the place allow for it.

But I would judge that these things should be done and tested especially in **ALEXANDRIA**, because it is most credible that **PTOLEMY**, the principal master in the Astronomical arts, once measured the latitude of the place in which he lived (or, what is the same, the height of the Pole) most exactly, specifically about fifteen centuries ago. Thus, an error of even one or two minutes <note>original: "scrupuli" — literally 'scruples', used here as astronomical minutes.</note> would not be hidden, since he recorded it as 31 degrees and 58 minutes <note>original: "32. Gradus deficientibus solummodo duobus Minutis"</note>; which some of his predecessors also discovered. For what is reported about other inclinations of the Pole or the Equator by him in his *Geography*, or elsewhere in writings, lacks exact reliability; and for the most part, they are taken according to a broad estimation. Just as with the fixed stars, which are seen and can be observed for the most part every year in any one horizon, the required diligence was not applied according to the measurement of longitude and latitude. Therefore, a much easier slip could occur in places of the Earthly globe that are not so open and favorable to observation in any one position. Nor is it credible that what the ancients produced in searching out the latitudes of certain principal cities from the proportion of the **Gnomon** <note>term: "Gnomon" — a vertical rod whose shadow is used to measure the sun’s altitude.</note> and the shadow is perfectly correct, since the extremity of the shadow is not clearly precise enough, and the Gnomon must be very long...

<summary>Tycho Brahe discusses his correspondence with Magini and proposes a scientific mission to Egypt to measure the Earth's latitude. He aims to determine if the Earth's axial tilt changes over centuries, a question vital for verifying Copernicus's theories and Ptolemy's ancient observations.</summary>

<keywords>Tycho Brahe, Giovanni Antonio Magini, Alexandria, Cairo, latitude, axial tilt, Nicolaus Copernicus, Claudius Ptolemy, gnomon, obliquity of the ecliptic, Wandsbek Castle</keywords>

--- Page 98 ---
<meta>This page continues Tycho Brahe’s appeal for a scientific mission to Egypt. He has been discussing the necessity of precise observations to confirm if the Earth's axial tilt has changed since antiquity, comparing his own era's data with that of Pliny and Regiomontanus.</meta>

I remind you, and it is most exactly arranged to its own plane, such things as it reveals in the very minute of a degree. I say nothing of the fact that the equinoctial moments, around which they mostly practiced this study <term>pragmateia</term> <note>A Greek term for a systematic scientific treatise or investigation.</note>, were not as precisely known to them as this operation requires. And since the Sun varies its declination <term>declination</term> <note>The angular distance of a celestial body north or south of the celestial equator.</note> most rapidly around the equinoxes, it was easy to commit some deviation in this part, although it is not very obvious to the eyes. Therefore, those things reported by **PLINY** regarding the relationship of the shadow to the **gnomon** <note>The part of a sundial that casts a shadow; Pliny the Elder recorded these ratios for different latitudes.</note> in the city of **ROME**, stating it is as 8 to 9 at the equinoxes, even if properly weighed, and with the Sun's semi-diameter considered together, prove in a certain way that the inclination of the world's axis <note>The "obliquity of the ecliptic," or the tilt of the Earth's axis relative to its orbit.</note> at **ROME** is the same now as it once was in the times of **PLINY**, specifically through a comparison made with those things read to have been explored there about a hundred years ago by that most excellent mathematician **JOHANNES REGIOMONTANUS** <note>Johannes Müller von Königsberg (1436–1476), the most influential astronomer in Europe before Copernicus.</note>. However, since a doubt <note>original: "scrupulus," literally a small pebble that causes irritation or hesitation.</note> can still be raised as to whether those ancient gnomonic measurements were taken precisely enough, we are almost no more certain in these matters than before. I do not wish to suggest now that **Regiomontanus's** own record is unstable, so much so that it is not consistent with itself within four minutes. Nay, he even brought forth these very things a bit too boldly from a position of the Sun not sufficiently established, and therefore with its declination being doubtful; to say nothing of atmospheric refraction <note>The bending of light by the atmosphere, which makes celestial objects appear higher than they are.</note> and the parallax of the Sun, both of which he seems to have neglected. By no other method, therefore, as far as I can see, will it be possible to test without doubt whether the axis of the universe's revolution and the daily motion has altered somewhat in respect to a certain horizon on Earth over the passing centuries, than if the elevation of the pole at **ALEXANDRIA** were now recorded as accurately as possible (as has been said) and compared with that ancient **Ptolemaic** one.

Furthermore, not only would this newly instituted observation in **EGYPT** grant this benefit, but many fixed stars could also be recorded from the heavens by that same work, which never rise in our northern horizons, all the way down to **Canopus** <note>The brightest star in the constellation Carina, visible from Egypt but not from Tycho’s observatory in Denmark.</note> itself. In the Sun also, and the Moon, and the other planets, many things could be explored there, both regarding refractions and other matters serving to scrutinize their standard most subtly; through these, the observations of the ancients living there could be evaluated, and at the same time, it could be investigated how they agree or disagree with those things discovered in these northern locations. Many aids for the complete restoration of Astronomy might come from this, which would be too long to enumerate one by one.

You, therefore, most Illustrious and Powerful **VENETIANS**, if you do not find it burdensome to apply your exceptional and most praiseworthy care to this—as I was previously made aware of your excellent intention—you will have done a thing not only useful and long desired in vain, but also most worthy of memory for all posterity of your renowned praise, which shines brightly elsewhere.

I indeed, as far as is in me, will not fail in my duty toward you in providing helpful hands to this business, whether by arranging instruments and means, or by prescribing those things which must be done, which I always reverently

->offer to you: although I do not deny that there are found in Italy, and even in your<-
->renowned city, those who could perform these very things and others pertaining to this<-
->much more expeditiously than I. For neither<-
->will Virgils <note>original: "Marones" - Referring to the poet Virgil (Publius Vergilius Maro), used here to mean great artists or scientists.</note> be lacking, where<-
->Maecenases <note>Gaius Maecenas was the legendary patron of Virgil and Horace; the name became a synonym for a wealthy benefactor of the arts and sciences.</note> are present.<-
