<meta>This page transitions from the specific measurements of astronomical instruments to the architectural and environmental requirements for a successful observatory. It continues the discussion of the "restoration" of astronomy by addressing the best geographic locations for observation.</meta>

->A P P E N D I X<-

# ON ARCHITECTURAL STRUCTURES
## Accommodating Astronomical Observations.

The descriptions of astronomical instruments recorded in this manner (as seen above) treat the mechanical part of the Art—which must be the very first of all, and without which the rest are attempted in vain—sufficiently and more than enough. They suffice for all kinds of uses with a variety and abundance that is not only pleasing but also useful and almost necessary. But if these instruments are to be applied aptly and without inconvenience to their exercise and the duty for which they are intended, there is required not only a suitable place, but also opportune buildings, in which this <term>pragmateia</term> <note>A Greek term for a systematic scientific study or professional business.</note> may be administered without any difficulties or disturbances. Indeed, let the **PLACE** be first of all elevated, from where it is granted to look freely at the Horizon all around, with no forests or mountains or other buildings standing in the way. It is also conducive for the place to be solitary and removed from the noise of the common people, so that one may enjoy Philosophical leisure <note>Tycho emphasizes "Otio Philosophico," the peace required for deep intellectual work.</note>, yet in such a way that necessities are at hand. Access should occasionally lie open to the Learned and the intelligent, but the promiscuous crowd should be kept away, as they neither understand such things nor hold them in the value they deserve. 

And although places existing in the Southern region of the earth might seem preferable to those verging toward the Poles and which are more northern—on account of a more upright position of the Sphere <note>In the South, the celestial equator is higher in the sky, making many observations easier.</note> and perhaps more frequent clear skies—nevertheless, since it is not convenient for everyone to change their Fatherland or the place where they live for that reason, nor is that sought-after condition and convenience perhaps granted in any Southern parts of the Earth (especially those unknown and foreign), it will certainly be necessary to be content with the opportunity of place and time that is offered and at hand. One must overcome difficulties and inconveniences, if any occur, with constant diligence, lest the same thing happen which <term>Seneca</term> <note>Lucius Annaeus Seneca (4 BCE – 65 CE), Roman Stoic philosopher.</note> truly says regarding time: **We have little, but we lose much.** 

In <term>Egypt</term>, indeed, and other places in <term>Africa</term> and <term>Asia</term> where the ancient astronomers lived, it would be granted to practice these things more closely because of a sky that is, as they say, more clear, and a Sphere less inclined. But since <term>Bellona</term> <note>The Roman goddess of war; here symbolizing the military conflicts of the Ottoman Empire.</note> has expelled almost all the liberal arts there through the <term>Turkish Empire</term>, and it is likely that Astronomy is in exile there or held in less value (unless by chance it is still practiced in some way by the Arabs). Regarding other parts of <term>Europe</term> which verge toward the South, better things would certainly be hoped for, provided they do not obey the Turk or the Muscovite—if indeed they do not turn away from the noble arts there. But since those kingdoms are also for the most part occupied by other things and care less for Astronomical matters (as far as is known), he who prefers those parts (even though more Southern) to those which verge somewhat toward the North seems likely to find a loss rather than a gain. 

Indeed, since observations in the winter time, when the nights are longer, are performed more conveniently and fully, the Northern coast has this prerogative: not only does it provide this, but moreover, with its intense frost—especially when the Northern winds are blowing—it so purifies and thins the air that for many continuous days it is most refined. Thus, the stars shine and twinkle <note>original: "scintillent," referring to the atmospheric effect of stars flickering.</note> as much as possible at night, whereas in Southern Regions at that time it very frequently either Rains, or the sky is otherwise covered with clouds and damp, and lies less open to the consideration of the heavenly bodies. 

The Prince of most praised memory, <term>William of Hesse</term> <note>William IV, Landgrave of Hesse-Kassel (1532–1592), a fellow astronomer and correspondent of Tycho.</note>, Landgrave, who was intensely addicted to Astronomical matters and not lightly colored by them, did indeed wish that I might obtain my habitation in a more Southern place of the earth, where I might more fully watch over the Heavenly bodies, as is clear from certain letters of his to me printed in the **First Volume of Astronomical Epistles, Page 23**. There is no doubt he did this with the best intention, and perhaps even to allure me thither. But I think that in <term>Denmark</term>, no fewer observations of the stars can be obtained than in <term>Hesse</term> or any other part of <term>Germany</term> (to say nothing now of other European kingdoms), except that some Stars rise in the more Southern regions which remain perpetually hidden in the North. This, however, is not of great importance, nor does it contribute much to the restoration of Astronomy. 

The twenty-one manuscript books in my possession can testify to this, containing the most accurate observations performed in Denmark over as many years; the likes of which I believe have scarcely been obtained by anyone else in the meantime (may envy be absent from the saying), even in a more southern Region. In these books, the locations and changes of all the Planets are explored frequently and in many ways every single year—not even <term>Mercury</term> appearing more rarely on account of its smaller divergence <term>elongation</term> <note>The angular distance of a planet from the Sun; Mercury is difficult to see because it stays close to the Sun's glare.</note> from the Sun. Furthermore, the positions of the fixed Stars, as many as are in any way open to sight, are most subtly noted. And afterward, they were transferred into a Canonical description, such as no one until now—even among those who formerly inhabited <term>Egypt</term> and the <term>Southern</term> and (as is thought) more serene places of the earth—is found to have provided, nor any of their successors, wherever on earth they lived. That I bring this forward not out of any arrogance or contempt for the ancients, but with the truth of the matter thus supporting me, I shall make clear shortly, if the Divine Power wills it. 

And let it be enough to have given this warning about choosing a place (in which, however, I permit everyone to abound in his own sense). The occasion itself will easily suggest more: and if all things cannot be had according to one's wish, convenience must nonetheless be accepted in whatever way it is offered, so that something rather than nothing may be done.

--- Page 100 ---
<meta>Continuing from the previous discussion on the ideal location for an observatory, Tycho now transitions from the geographical setting to the specific architectural requirements for the buildings themselves, focusing on stability and the protection of his precision instruments.</meta>

# BUILDINGS 

Furthermore, such buildings are to be constructed in a way that they serve observations conveniently, and that they have each instrument—according to its size and shape—firmly, properly, and suitably arranged. They must preserve the instruments in good repair <note>original: "sarta recta," a Latin legal phrase signifying that a building or object is kept in proper, functional condition.</note> from the damage caused by the air, winds, and rain. At the same time, coverings should be built over the instruments themselves in such a way that they can be opened with little effort when the situation requires it, and then closed again. Indeed, the material of the structures ought to be entirely of stone, whether of baked bricks or other quarried stone, and, where necessary, even marble or other stone columns. For the sake of stability, the instruments should be supported and everything fixed into a firm foundation, so that they do not wobble in any way or gradually acquire any instability. The coverings, however, should be made of some lighter framework, so that they may be opened and put back together again more easily. There should also be dwellings and heated rooms <note>original: "vaporaria," referring to steam baths or rooms heated by stoves, which were essential for the health and comfort of astronomers working through the cold Danish nights.</note> for those observers who keep watch over such things. And both these and other requirements must be arranged so harmoniously that no inconveniences, difficulties, or disturbances delay the work itself or make it tedious.

Anyone who has the funds available may, at their own discretion, invent and construct various forms of buildings for this purpose, according to the suitability of the place and the shape and number of the instruments. In this matter, I do not think anything specifically should be prescribed to anyone. If, however, someone wishes to know this design which we have chosen and completed according to architectural principles—having exhausted no small labor and expense on that island in the Danish Sound famous for its ships, Venusia <note>The Latinized name Tycho used for the island of Hven, located between modern-day Denmark and Sweden.</note> (commonly called Hven), and begun twenty-two years ago—well then, we shall provide that too. By this means, it can be established in which places and with what architectural forms those instruments (whose illustrations and descriptions we previously exhibited) were arranged. From this, anyone can then take what they wish and adapt it for their own use. For I hope that in their own time, there will be some who are touched by such deep
->concern, and who will strive with great<-
->effort to imitate or even surpass these things.<-

# THE CITADEL OF URANIBORG
## IN REGARD TO ITS TOTAL CAPACITY
### AND DESIGN.

<note>This page features a detailed woodcut plan of the Uraniborg observatory complex. The illustration shows the central residence and observatory building (marked 'A') situated within a circular courtyard. This courtyard is surrounded by meticulously arranged geometric gardens containing fruit trees and smaller auxiliary structures (marked 'M'). The entire complex is enclosed within a large square perimeter wall, rotated 45 degrees to form a diamond shape, with gatehouses (marked 'B', 'C', and 'D') located at the corners pointing toward the cardinal directions.</note>

<term>Citadel of Uraniborg<note>original: "Arcis Uraniburgi." Named for Urania, the Muse of Astronomy, this was the first custom-built scientific research institute in modern Europe. Tycho referred to it as a "citadel" or "castle" because of its fortified appearance and its symbolic role as a stronghold for the pursuit of celestial truth.</note></term>

<term>Design<note>original: "Designatio." This term encompasses the architectural layout, the geometric planning, and the functional arrangement of the entire site.</note></term>

--- Page 102 ---
<meta>Continuing from the previous page's discussion on the structural requirements for a successful observatory—namely stability, protection from the elements, and convenience for the observers—Tycho now presents the actual architectural realization of these principles: his "Citadel of the Skies," Uraniborg. Catchword: OR.</meta>

# EXPLANATION OF THE PLAN OF THE CITADEL OF URANIBORG,
## IN REGARD TO ITS TOTAL CAPACITY.

**F**REDERICK THE SECOND of most praiseworthy memory, King of Denmark and Norway, etc., yielding to no one in virtue, magnanimity, and integrity, who ten years ago—to the great grief not only of Denmark and his subjects but also of the neighboring regions—passed away at the age of fifty-four, being surely worthy of a longer life had it so seemed fit to GOD: This most excellent King (I say), since besides other heroic gifts he possessed a truly generous and inclined mind toward liberal studies, and had understood that **Astronomy** <note>original: "Astronomia"</note> holds the principal place among these, having always been held in the highest value by the greatest monarchs and kings—many of whom were laudably engaged in it and attained no small permanence of name thereby—he deemed it worthwhile that this science should also be illustriously cultivated and fostered in his own kingdom. Therefore, when he perceived that I was devoting my efforts to its restoration, and had been engaged in that arena for many years since my youth—so much so that for the sake of continuing this work and bringing it to its desired end, I had resolved to leave my fatherland for Germany, where I might have leisure for it more quietly and opportunely—he summoned me and, with merciful benevolence, exhorted me to carry out the same work here in Denmark. By royal favor, he granted me the use of the Island of **Hven** <note>original: "Venusia" or "Huenna"</note>, located in the famous Danish Sound, so that I might there apply myself to these celestial studies. I am often accustomed to recall the memory of this never-sufficiently-praised King’s counsel, since he deserved so exceptionally well not only of me, but of the entire field of Astronomy by this means. Wherefore it is fitting that all posterity, to whom (as I hope) the fruit of this work will one day reach, should celebrate the name of that most Serene and Excellent King with a grateful mind. 

Therefore, by the decree and merciful will of that most distinguished King, when buildings solidly and magnificently suited for Astronomical business were to be constructed by me on the said island, I chose a site especially elevated upon it (since the whole island itself stood high), near its very center. There I began to build from the foundations a Citadel named after the Heavens themselves, whose observations it was to serve, and called **Uraniborg** <note>literally "The Castle of Urania," the Muse of Astronomy</note> for that reason. Beginning in the year 1576, I completed it in successive stages. You see its elevation <note>original: "Orthographiam," referring to a scale drawing of the facade and layout</note> represented here according to its entire form and capacity, as far as could be done on a flat surface. The particular features to be explained in this plan are as follows, indicated by the letters marked in their specific places: 

**A.** The main house, built in the very center of the whole area, its four sides forming an equal square and facing the four quarters of the world most exactly, so that the two small towers face South and North, while both gates face East and West precisely. 
**E.** The Eastern Gate, constructed in rustic and **Tuscan** <note>a sturdy, plain style of classical architecture</note> work. 
**D.** The Western Gate, finished in the same style. These two gates had two large English watchdogs above them in a suitable little house, to signal the arrival of anyone from any direction by their barking. 
**B.** The Printing Shop, which in some way resembles the larger house in a smaller form. 
**C.** A small house for servants, also representing the larger house in its gables and certain other features. 
**F.** The interior face of the rampart. 
**G.** Its exterior form with its arrangement. 
**N.** Four paths leading toward both gates and both small houses, which run exactly toward the four quarters of the world. 
**O.** The four gates of the gardens. 
**M.** Four arbors <note>original: "Pergulae"</note> arranged in those semi-circular ramparts for the sake of pleasantness. 
**L.** Gardens of herbs and flowers. 
**H.** Gardens of trees of various kinds, numbering about three hundred, etc. 

And this is the description of the total capacity. The circuit of the rampart has a square figure with a semi-circle woven into the middle of each side, as is clear in the preceding design. Each side of the square contains 300 feet. The thickness of the rampart at its base is twenty feet. Its height is 22 feet. The interior diameter of the semi-circular rampart is about ninety feet. The house itself, situated in the middle, which is also exactly square as I said, has about 60 feet on each side. The height of the wall is 45 feet. The round towers attached on the South and North are 22 feet in diameter; the galleries <note>original: "ambitus"</note> adhering to them on the outside add another 10 feet. The porches at the East and West on the lower part of the square have 15 feet on each side. The total height of the house from the surface of the ground to the topmost weathercock <note>original: "Pegalum," likely referring to a decorative or functional summit piece</note> is 75 feet. The house also extends in every direction below ground, having subterranean buildings whose depth is 12 feet. Indeed, there are even certain other things below these. But since these are subterranean, they are not represented here, although they consist of many rooms and are ordered for various uses; they were completed at great expense, almost no less than the upper part of the house which stands above the level of the earth and lies open under the sky.

I shall now subjoin a fuller description and explanation of this main house situated in the center, so that all things may be more clearly visible; and I will exhibit there some of the subterranean and hidden things, as far as is possible to imitate such things on a flat surface. For to recount everything finished there, not only below but also above—though it might not be easily believed that this house contained such and so many things—would be both too long and perhaps tedious for some.

---

->*Sacred house of Urania, renowned watchtower of the Heavens,*<-
->*Founded on a high place, and secured by a rampart,*<-
->*Set about with trees and herbs in your gardens,*<-
->*You who surveyed all the stars through thrice-seven years,*<-
->*While you raised your august head to Olympus*<-
->*Do you now lie thus despised? Do you now rest thus bereaved?*<-
->*Perhaps it seemed so to the gods above, who care for divine things;*<-
->*Lest great gifts be confined within narrow bounds,*<-
->*Thus do all earthly things turn and change their seasons.*<-
->*Praise be to you alone, who turn the Heavens and the Stars.*<-

He concludes with a poignant poem reflecting on the observatory's decline after twenty-one years of active use.</summary>

# FLOOR PLAN AND ITS EXPLANATION
<note>original: "Ichnographia"; the horizontal ground plan of a structure</note>

**A** Eastern Door. **C.** Western Door. ☉. Four passages meeting at right angles, which were later reduced to three so that the winter dining room or heated room **D.** could be enlarged, and in its corner behind the furnace there was a small and secret spagyric <term>Spagyric: A term for alchemy, specifically the art of separating and recombining the essences of plants and minerals for medicine</term> laboratory, in which there were five distinct furnaces that served the pyronomic <term>Pyronomic: Relating to the scientific management or use of fire, especially in a laboratory</term> work ready at hand, so that it would not always be necessary to descend into the larger laboratory **B.** A fountain rotating a revolving water-wheel, which threw water upwards here and there as desired. **D.** That winter dining room. **E. F. G.** Guest rooms. **L.** Stairs for ascending to the upper floor. **H.** Kitchen. **K.** A cement-lined well 40 cubits <note>original: "ulnas"; a measurement of length based on the forearm</note> deep, serving the hydraulic mechanism and distributing water through siphons hidden within the walls to the individual rooms, both upper and lower. **P.** Stairs for descending into the Chemical laboratory. **T** The Library. **VV.** The large brass <note>original: "Orchalcius"; a copper alloy similar to brass or bronze</note> globe shown in the illustration. **V.** Four tables for students; four chimneys ascending from the lower laboratory as well as in the four corners of the rooms. **Y.** Beds arranged here and there in the same rooms. The observer will easily discern the other features by their own observation. All these things should be understood to be of such a size as to fit the foundation of the larger house depicted above, though they are shown here in nearly half the size for the sake of space.

# EXPLANATION OF THE PARTS OF THE LARGER AND PRINCIPAL HOUSE.

**T**his larger and principal building of the Citadel of Uraniborg <note>The "Citadel of the Heavens"</note> was begun to be built by me in the year 1576. Its first foundation stone was laid by the Magnificent Lord CHARLES DANZAY, who for many years was the Ambassador of the most Serene and Powerful King of the French to Denmark. He was a man second to none in integrity, virtue, and practical experience, and was moreover excellently steeped in the liberal arts. Because he followed me with a singular and constant affection as long as he lived (for he died eight years ago in his eighty-first year), he always favored me and my affairs. As soon as he learned that this house was to be built, he voluntarily offered to place the first corner stone there. Soon after, he saw to it that the following words were inscribed in a certain porphyry stone:

->WHILE FREDERICK II WAS REIGNING IN DENMARK,<-
->CHARLES DANZAY OF AQUITAINE, AMBASSADOR OF THE KING OF FRANCE TO DENMARK,<-
->PLACED THIS VOTIVE STONE FOR THE SAKE OF MEMORY<-
->AND A HAPPY OMEN FOR THIS HOUSE, BUILT BY THE NOBLE MAN<-
->TYCHO BRAHE OF KNUDSTRUP BY ROYAL DECREE<-
->FOR PHILOSOPHY AND ESPECIALLY<-
->FOR THE CONTEMPLATION OF THE STARS.<-
->IN THE YEAR 1576, ON THE SIXTH IDES OF AUGUST <note>August 8th</note>.<-

Indeed, when the day destined for the foundation arrived, the excellent Danzay arrived, accompanied by several noble men, as well as some learned men from our common friends, to be present for this act. On the eighth day of August, in the morning at sunrise—with Jupiter near the Heart of the Lion <note>original: "cor ♌"; the star Regulus</note> and the Moon occupying the western cardine <note>The western horizon</note> in Aquarius—he placed the stone in the presence of all of us, after first solemnly making an offering of various wines and praying for every prosperity, with the friends standing around in support. This stone was placed in the eastern corner of the house toward the South-East <note>original: "Notapelioten"</note>, as expressed in the accompanying illustration next to the letter F. Afterward, the whole house began to be built, and in successive years it was completed, although not a few or slight difficulties and delays crept in during the meantime, which it is not now necessary to discuss. The explanation of its elevation <note>original: "Orthographiæ"; the architectural drawing of the facade</note> assigned here is as follows:

**A.** The Eastern Gate, composed of Ionic and Doric work. **B.** The winter dining room. **F.** The foundation stone I mentioned, which the said French Ambassador placed. **G.** and **H.** Windows of the subterranean cellar. **C.** A chamber for guests; there are two similar ones toward the west. **M.** The Museum with the library. **L.** The subterranean and round chemical laboratory, containing 16 various furnaces destined for the spagyric <term>Spagyric: A term for alchemy, specifically the art of separating and recombining the essences of plants and minerals for medicine</term> art. **I.** The hole through which coals are lowered for the pyronomic <term>Pyronomic: The scientific management of fire, especially in a laboratory or furnace</term> works. **Z.** A subterranean cellar for storing wood. **q.** The kitchen. **D.** The chamber called "The Red Room." **α** <note>Greek letter alpha</note> An octagonal yellow chamber. **E.** The Blue Chamber. To these three corresponds a larger summer dining room toward the west, green in color and adorned on the ceiling with paintings of the principal herbs, from which a huge number of ships sailing past—especially in the summer time—is pleasantly viewed. **X.** Windows of the upper floor. **O.** The larger Southern Observatory, containing several distinguished and large astronomical instruments, especially the Azimuthal Semicircle, the Ptolemaic Rules, and the Brass Sextant for taking altitudes; also the medium Brass Azimuthal Quadrant. These were represented and explained in their own places above, specifically by numbers 8, 9, 4, and 3, retaining the same order in which the instruments are listed here. This should also be understood in the following descriptions (where necessary). **P.** A certain globe upon which an instrument is placed while observing the distances of the stars, to serve as a support so that it can be rotated in all directions; such a globe is also depicted above with the Sextant, number 16. A similar one corresponds toward the west. **Q.** An octagonal gallery in which the aforesaid globe is situated. **N.** The smaller Southern Observatory, containing the brass Equatorial Armillary Spheres indicated at number 12. **V, V.** The descent into the laboratory and the ascent into the observatory. **R.** The larger Northern Observatory, also filled with several large instruments, such as the Rules or the larger Brass Parallactic instrument, which also shows azimuths on the circumference of the wall, explained at number 10. There was also in the same tower a Sextant providing distances by a single observer, and that bipartite arc, as well as that instrument which was once used for observing the New Star <note>The supernova of 1572</note>; these three were previously explained by numbers 17 and 15. Also preserved there was a certain wooden parallactic instrument used by the great Copernicus, which was sent to me as a gift from Frombork <note>original: "V Varmiā"; Warmia, the region in Poland where Copernicus lived</note>, which I mentioned in its own place. **S.** The smaller Northern Observatory, containing other Equatorial Armillaries delineated at number 20. **T.** Another globe similar to the first, serving as a support for the Sextants; a similar one also corresponds toward the west. In the upper part of the house itself, where the round windows are seen, there are eight small rooms for <note>abbreviation for pro</note> students. The topmost octagonal chamber is just below the spire of the house, from which the view lies open on all sides, having a walkway around it—which they call a gallery—above the roof itself. **yy.** Octagonal structures in which carved images representing the four parts of the year <note>The Seasons</note> are seen. **ββ** <note>Greek letter beta</note> Seven chimneys ending in one toward the south, and another seven toward the north, so that all the chimneys of the whole house meet in two distinct places. **N.** The clock, whose bell, **5**, hangs above. **λ** <note>Greek letter lambda</note> A gilded Pegasus which, by a rotating index below the ceiling of the topmost chamber, shows from where the winds blow. And there, another index rotating on the same center shows the hours.

Such is the exterior face of the house, whether viewed from the East or the West; and if one compares the Southern part with the Northern, they will find the same form, so that all things correspond to each other and consist of a fitting symmetry, as is required in architectural works if they are to be made skillfully and in the proper manner. Furthermore, the subterranean parts, which are seen below marked with dotted lines, are to be understood thus: whatever is seen below the Southern Tower expresses the pyronomic laboratory, as far as was possible; where **1** is a round table situated in its center, on which those things to be prepared are placed. The number **2** represents the furnaces of various kinds arranged all around. There were 16 in number: three different kinds of baths <note>Likely "bains-marie" or water baths for gentle heating</note>, one digestion furnace in ashes, four large athanors <note>Self-feeding furnaces used for constant heat</note>, two small ones, two distillation furnaces in sand or ashes, one for a large vessel <note>original: "vesica"; a bladder-shaped flask</note> adapted with double pipes, another secret furnace with lamps, and two reverberatory furnaces, one of which reverberated directly and the other through a helical line both openly and closed. The greater part of the furnaces was made from stones dug from the earth brought from Norway, which they call Bergen stones; for these withstand the strongest fire and can be skillfully worked. But if everything that was in this laboratory were to be explained, it would not be finished in a few words. The things designated below the larger house itself indicate the subterranean cellars, storehouses, and pantry. Where the doors are, through which there is entry here and there, they are marked **3**; the columns placed for support are marked **4**. Below the Northern Tower next to number **5**, that concrete well 40 cubits deep is seen, which is mentioned later in the floor plan. At number **6** are concrete chests in which certain foodstuffs were preserved. Other things the figure partly expresses, and partly they are to be understood.

# ELEVATION OF STJERNEBORG <note>original: "Orthographia Stellæburgi"; an elevation is a scale drawing of the side or front of a building.</note>
## LOCATED OUTSIDE THE CITADEL OF URANIA. <note>Uraniborg</note>

---

## GROUND PLAN OF STJERNEBORG. <note>original: "Ichnographia Stellæburgi"; a ground plan or floor plan viewed from above.</note>

<note>Stjerneborg, or "Star Castle," was Tycho Brahe’s second observatory, built around 1584. While Uraniborg was a grand residence and laboratory, Stjerneborg was designed purely for observation. Tycho famously placed the instrument rooms underground so that the heavy brass instruments would be protected from the wind and mounted on solid foundations, ensuring the highest possible precision for his data.</note>

<margin>STEL. <note>The catchword, a 16th-century printing convention where the first word of the following page—here "Stellæburgi"—is printed at the bottom of the current page to ensure continuity.</note></margin>

<summary>This page features the architectural titles and diagrams for Stjerneborg, Tycho Brahe's innovative underground observatory designed for maximum instrument stability. It serves as a visual companion to the technical descriptions of his astronomical research facility on the island of Hven.</summary>
<keywords>Stjerneborg, Star Castle, elevation, ground plan, observatory, Tycho Brahe, Hven, scientific architecture, 16th-century astronomy</keywords>

--- Page 106 ---
<meta>This page continues the detailed description of Tycho Brahe’s astronomical complex on the island of Hven. While the previous page provided the architectural drawings (the elevation and ground plan) for Stjerneborg, this page provides the text-based "key" or explanation for those diagrams, describing the function of each room and the inscriptions found on site.</meta>

# EXPLANATION OF STJERNEBORG. <note>original: "Stellæburgi Explicatio"</note>

**E**ven though the towers facing South and North in the main house of the Citadel <note>Uraniborg</note>, with their various extensions, were sufficient for the convenient arrangement of many large instruments, I nonetheless gradually arranged for more to be built for specific reasons. These could not be contained within those spaces appropriately enough for each to be used without obstructing the others. Consequently, around the year 1584, on a certain hill outside the Citadel toward the South <margin>removed from the rampart by about 70 small paces</margin>, I ordered an underground observatory to be constructed with various crypts <note>underground vaulted rooms</note> built of solid masonry from bottom to top. This was done with no small labor and expense, partly so that I might steady some of the primary instruments firmly and safely, lest they be disturbed by any winds and thus serve their purpose more conveniently; and partly so that I might separate my students when many were present. I could assign some to the Citadel itself and others to these crypts to perform observations, so they would not get in each other’s way or compare their observations before I wished them to. I called this observatory **Stiernburg** in the Danish language, which sounds almost the same in German and is **Stellæburgum** <note>Star Castle</note> in Latin. You see its form and elevation here along with its ground plan. Their explanation is briefly indicated as follows: **A.** A portal composed of Ionic work, through which one descends into the crypts of this observatory; above it are three skillfully carved Crowned Lions, and on either side are porphyry stones displaying suitable inscriptions. These were included in the first book of my *Astronomical Letters* where this underground observatory is discussed, and it would be too long to repeat them all here. However, we will shortly provide the later one, which is more universal. **B.** A round ceiling, beneath which is a heated room <term>hypocaust<gloss>a room heated from below, often used in Roman and Renaissance architecture</term>, into which entry is open from all sides of the crypts. **C.** Crypt for the larger Equatorial Armillaries <note>rings used to measure coordinates</note> illustrated at number 14. **D.** Crypt for the great revolving Quadrant called number 6, expressed previously. **E.** Crypt for the Zodiacal Armillaries displayed at number 11. **F.** Crypt for the large Geometric Square containing a steel Quadrant inside, marked as number 7. **G.** Crypt for the Four-Cubit Sextant on its support and globe, which is represented at number 16. Let these be briefly noted regarding the crypts themselves. The inscription, however, of which I spoke, located on the other side of the portal toward the South and marked in golden letters on porphyry stone, is as follows:

-> TO GOD, THE BEST AND GREATEST, <-
-> AND SACRED TO POSTERITY. <-

> *Observing that ASTRONOMY, the oldest and most excellent of all sciences, had indeed been long and greatly cultivated but was not yet firmly established or freed from errors, he [Tycho] devised and constructed various exact instruments for the restoration and perfection of the science. These are sufficient for all kinds of observations of the celestial bodies. With incredible labor, diligence, and expense, he arranged them—partly in the nearby Citadel of URANIBORG, built for the same reason, and partly in these crypts for more stable and convenient use. Commending, dedicating, and consecrating this rarest and most precious treasure to you, O illustrious posterity who shall follow in every age, he asks that you preserve it perpetually for the glory of the eternal GOD, the architect of the admirable celestial machine, for the propagation of the divine art, and for the honor of the fatherland. Do not allow it to fall into ruin through age or other misfortune, nor to be moved elsewhere, nor violated in any way: if for nothing else, at least out of reverence for the avenging eye of the Author of the universe.*
>
> -> He who began and completed all these things on this island, having placed this monument, asks and entreats you, <-
>
> -> TYCHO BRAHE, SON OF OTTE. <-
> -> GREETINGS TO YOU WHO READ AND DO THESE THINGS, AND FAREWELL. <-

Outside, in the area of this Stjerneborg enclosure, are stone columns **H, I,** arranged on both the East and West sides for supporting Ptolemaic rulers <note>long rods used for measuring angular distances</note> as well as smaller portable armillaries when required. **K, L, N, O** are globes resting on their supports, sometimes arranged in the said area so that sextants may be tilted against them to aim at any position of the stars. **M** is a round stone table intended for setting up a portable quadrant and other smaller instruments when desired, so they may serve conveniently for observations. The observant and intelligent viewer will notice the rest for themselves.

## EXPLANATION OF THE GROUND PLAN. <note>original: "Ichnographiæ Expositio"</note>

**A** The vestibule through which one enters the observatory by steps. **B,** The square heated room. **C,** Crypt for the largest Equatorial Armillaries. **D,** For the Revolving Quadrant. **E,** For the Zodiacal Armillaries. **F,** For the large steel Quadrant which has a Geometric Square, also made of steel, on its exterior. **G,** For the Four-Cubit Sextant placed upon its revolving globe. **H, H,** Stone columns arranged on the western side. **I, I,** Other stone columns located toward the East. **K, L, N, T,** Globes placed outside for supporting the astronomical sextants. **M,** A round stone table. **O,** A bed, in which it was sometimes permitted for me to rest during observations if, by chance, clear weather was not granted continuously due to the intervention of clouds. **Q,** Another similar and larger one for the same use for the Students. **P,** The furnace. **V,** A table. **S,** The beginning of an underground passage which I once intended to lead beneath the rampart and the garden into the Citadel and into the chemical laboratory; for it was started, but not finished. The exterior area with its gallery has a length of seventy feet on each side of its square, facing the four quarters of the world. The diameter of the semicircle intercepted in the middle is twenty-four feet. In two angles of this square area, toward the Southeast <note>original: "Notapelicten"</note> and Southwest <note>original: "Notolybicum"</note>, are certain convenient cases in which that larger Semicircular instrument (used for taking distances of stars beyond the range of a quadrant, the drawing of which is not shown among the other instruments but is briefly indicated after them) was kept safe and dry, along with other sextants and portable tools of the same kind whose use is not always required. Likewise, I had decided to arrange certain other things at the Northeast <note>original: "Borrhapelioten"</note> and Northwest <note>original: "Borrolybicum"</note> corners in their own time. Let these things be said briefly about these underground structures. If I wished to explain everything individually, a long description would be needed. Since, moreover, we have assigned poems in golden letters here and there in the crypts, the same first book of *Letters* shows them, so it is not worth the effort to recall them here.

And let this be enough to have indicated concerning the astronomical buildings. Although it was not my intention to include here the island of the famous Danish Strait <note>The Øresund</note> in which we built these things, nonetheless, because I mentioned the place being elevated and situated apart, and since this very island has already been depicted in the fourth volume of the *Atlas of Cities* <note>original: "Theatri Urbium"</note> by the distinguished Mr. Georg Braun (though not accurately enough due to the painter’s neglect), I therefore thought it should be presented here a little more exactly.

<summary>This page explains the layout and purpose of Stjerneborg, Tycho Brahe's underground observatory, detailing the specific crypts for instruments and providing a solemn dedication to future generations. Tycho emphasizes that the underground design was chosen for stability, wind protection, and to keep his students from influencing each other's data.</summary>
<keywords>Stjerneborg, Tycho Brahe, astronomical instruments, observatory design, equatorial armillary, quadrant, sextant, Hven, scientific precision, Georg Braun, posterity</keywords>

TOPOGRAPHY OF THE ISLAND OF VENUSIA, COMMONLY CALLED HVEN.
† 2

# TOPOGRAPHY OF THE ISLAND OF VENUSIA, COMMONLY CALLED HVEN.

### EXPLANATION OF THIS TOPOGRAPHY.

**T**his island is situated in the most famous strait of the illustrious Kingdom of Denmark, which divides Scania <note>modern-day southern Sweden, then part of Denmark</note> from Zealand. It looks toward the capital, **Copenhagen**, to the Southwest at a distance of three miles; toward **Elsinore**, where the Royal Custom House is located, to the Northwest at a distance of two miles (both of which are in Zealand); toward **Helsingborg**, which is exactly to the North, also two miles away; and toward **Landskrona** to the Southeast, just one mile distant (both of which are located in Scania). The island itself is quite high, as if one were climbing a mountain, yet it is entirely flat on top. It measures 8,160 large paces in circumference. Near its center, where I built the Citadel of **Uraniborg**, the elevation of the Pole—or, what is the same thing, the latitude from the Equator—has been frequently determined by us with the utmost accuracy as 55 degrees, 54 1/2 minutes. We have taken its longitude to be 36 degrees, 45 minutes, having certainly respected the difference of meridians used by Ptolemy and Copernicus, and with as much diligence as possible; just as in this square map containing the layout of the island, these coordinates are subdivided into seconds of a single degree. Other things seen on the island itself are to be understood as follows: 

**A.** The Citadel of **Uraniborg**. 
**B.** **Stjerneborg** <note>original: "Stellæburgū", the Star Castle</note>. 
**C.** The farmstead intended for the estate's economy. 
**D.** The workshop for artisans, those fabricating astronomical instruments and other things. 
**E.** A windmill. 
**F.** A village containing about 40 dwellings for the country folk. 
**G.** A mill serving the manufacture of paper, while simultaneously grinding grain and additionally preparing various hides; one and the same massive wheel performs these three tasks both together and separately. 
**H.** The church. 
**I.** The judicial court of the villagers. 
**K, L, M.** Large fishponds, of which **L**, designated for the mill, is of profound depth and established by an embankment of incredible mass so that it might serve as a reservoir for the abundance of water needed for the mill. These and the remaining ponds, about 60 in number and containing a great abundance of fish of various kinds, are for the most part easily drained for the use of the said mill when necessary. 
**O.** A marshy meadow, rising with some alders. 
**T.** Pastures planted with shrubs in several places. 
**N.** A small grove of hazel trees shading the northern shore. 
**P, Q, R, S.** The ruins of four castles that once stood there. 

When King Frederick II of most blessed memory granted me this island for life and confirmed it with letters given on parchment, so that I might carry out astronomical work there with suitable leisure (as we have also noted previously), I expended immense labor and expense upon it to serve the will of that excellent King and the honor of the Fatherland. I did this not only by constructing splendid and solid buildings, as indicated, but also by collecting water from all sides into many ponds (where nothing of the sort existed before), so that I might establish a mill suitable for preparing paper right there on the seashore. This mill supplied the paper for my printing press, which was also developed there, so that it would not have to be sought from Germany with great difficulty.

**I** HAVE spent on all these things more than a ton of gold <term>ton of gold: a common expression at the time for 100,000 gold dalers, an enormous sum</term>. For whatever I was able to collect, whether from Royal grants or from my own annual income, I spent on those uses; and I shall say nothing of the immense hardships and labors I endured there for 21 years. From these things and others, any sensible person will easily be able to judge that I have deserted such a great island and my own sweetest Fatherland—and in it my many blood relatives and friends—only for the gravest of reasons, especially now in my fiftieth year and with a large family. What these reasons were, and how great they were, I choose to remain silent about here. Meanwhile, I am happy to hold my most Serene King Christian IV, my merciful Lord (who recently succeeded his father King Frederick of most blessed memory, the founder and promoter of these things), blameless. For I do not doubt that if those things which concern this business (perhaps not dishonorable to the Kingdom) had been presented to him in a timely and sufficient manner, he—endowed as he is with a heroic nature, a discerning mind, a generous spirit, and an excellent inclination toward the noble arts—would have mercifully and liberally preserved and perpetuated these studies, most worthy of Kings, in his Kingdom. But perhaps it was so destined by fate, so that the restoration of Astronomy might be more widely proclaimed and rendered more universal. Indeed, anyone will easily consider within themselves how seriously and earnestly the restoration of Astronomy is at my heart, since I have been willing to bravely bear so many labors and expenses, so many disturbances and adversities for its sake—to the point that I did not hesitate to leave even my Fatherland and everything most dear to me.

-> SO GREAT IS OUR LOVE TO UNFOLD THE PATTERNS OF THE STARS. <-
<note>original: "TANTVS AMOR NOBIS ASTRORVM EXPANGERE NORMAS."</note>

<meta>This page continues Tycho Brahe’s technical descriptions of his astronomical equipment. Having previously described the layout of his observatories on the island of Hven, he now provides a technical supplement explaining the mathematical precision of his instrument scales and the design of his sighting mechanisms.</meta>

# SUPPLEMENT ON THE SUBDIVISION AND SIGHTS OF INSTRUMENTS.

**S**ince in the previous sections, when I dealt with the instruments, I had not expressed the <term>transversal subdivision</term><note>A method of using diagonal lines to divide a scale into much smaller, more precise increments than could be marked directly.</note> and the <term>diopters</term><note>Sighting devices or "vanes" used to align the instrument with a star.</note> with figures—because they were not immediately at hand—it is pleasing to add that very thing here, so that this business may be more rightly understood. Therefore, the method of the division having transversal points is such as this figure expresses, in which every ten minutes are distinguished into ten equal intervals by small lines marked with points; and thus the <term>fiduciary edge</term><note>The straight, calibrated edge of a ruler or alignment tool.</note> passing any of these during an observation brings forth the very minute of the degree that was sought, or a proportional part of it, according as it is discerned to be removed from this or that point. I learned this method in my youth at Leipzig, adapted indeed for rectilinear parallelograms, to which it properly belongs. However, I afterwards applied it quite appropriately to the arcs on my instruments, just as I hinted ten years ago in our book on the *Comet of the Year 1577*, near the end of page 461, in these words:

> For although its demonstration properly belongs to rectilinear parallelograms, it is nevertheless applied conveniently even to arcs, since in such a tiny interval the line differs imperceptibly from a straight line, without any trace of error.

But so that I may also add the demonstration of this here—perhaps on account of certain <gloss>pseudo-scholars</gloss> who carp at things they do not sufficiently grasp—take it thus. In the assigned figure **A** is the center of the instrument, and its radius is **A.O**. Moreover, the small part **O.I** is assumed, in which that division is made by transversal lines in the proportion of 1 to 48, such as is most often used in my instruments. And since **A.I** is set at 10,000,000,000 parts of the <term>Great Table of Rheticus</term><note>Reference to Georg Joachim Rheticus's famous 16th-century trigonometric tables.</note>, the same **O.I** will be 208,333,333, as it is the forty-eighth part of the radius. Let arc **I.E** be 20′ and **I.V** be 10′; their sine is 29,088,779 (**Y.I**). The second sine <note>The versed sine or similar trigonometric value</note> of these is 42,308 (**V.Y**), which, added to **N.V** (which is equal to **O.I**), makes **N.Y** 208,375,641. In the triangle **N.Y.I**, which is right-angled at **Y**, two sides **N.Y** and **Y.I** are known; therefore the base **I.N** is given as 210,396,208, together with the angle **N.I.Y** 82 degrees, 3′, 10″, 47‴. When **Y.I.A** (89 degrees, 50′) is added to this, it makes **N.I.A** 171 degrees, 53′, 10″, 47‴. Let the base **I.N** in the right-angled triangle **N.Y.I** be divided into ten equal parts, so that 21,039,621 corresponds to one minute, represented by **I.B**; soon, in the oblique triangle **B.I.A**, two sides **I.B** and **I.A** (the radius) are given, together with the angle **B.I.A**, which is the same as the angle **N.I.A** found before. Therefore the angle **I.A.B** becomes known as 1′, 0″, 7‴, which ought to be exactly 1 minute; thus it is greater by only 7‴ <note>Thirds of a minute, or 1/3600th of a degree</note>—a truly imperceptible difference. Similarly, if **F.I** is assumed to be nine parts, they will be 189,316,587; and we will again have the triangle **F.I.A**, in which two sides are given as just mentioned, along with the radius **I.A** and the angle **F.I.A** comprised by them; and the angle **I.A.F** comes out to 9′, 0″, 1‴, which ought to be exactly 9′, falling short in the last minute by only 1″, 6‴. Furthermore, as the same holds near the middle as we have now shown at the extremities, it is found by the same reasoning as before: first, that the angle **I.A.H** is 5′, 3″, 11″, exceeding by 3″, 11‴. Second, the angle **N.A.H** is 4′, 56″, 55‴, falling short by 3″, 5‴. It is clear, therefore, that the maximum difference—whether additive or subtractive—in this <term>calculation</term> <note>original: "pragmateia", a Greek term for a formal study or systematic treatment</note> results in something slightly over 3 seconds; a degree of subtlety which the sharpness of sight is in no way sustained to discern in any instrument whatsoever, and which is in itself idle. Therefore, they look for <gloss>a knot in a bulrush</gloss> <note>A Latin idiom meaning to find a difficulty where there is none.</note> in vain, if any should presume to scoff at this sufficiently accurate form of distribution of ours. Moreover, in certain larger instruments where the radius is longer, we are accustomed to carry out this subdivision by only five minutes, and therefore that small difference under discussion becomes even more imperceptible, which is what we proposed to show.

We have devised this method for <term>sighting vanes</term><note>original: "Pinnacidiorum", the plates or vanes on an instrument used for sighting.</note> or diopters as most suitable for use: that four slits be made in the lower vane (closest to the eye) around all its sides, which are precisely analogous to the upper vane, and whose four sides, with respect to the fiduciary line, are equidistant and correspond to it. You see this represented in the attached figure, as far as could be done on a flat plane, where **A.B.C.D** designates that front vane moved close to the eye of the observer; the other, **E.F.G.H**, which is distant, at the circumference of the instrument **I**, marks the ruler to which these vanes are competently fixed at right angles. It will be necessary for the vane **F.G.H.E** to be entirely equal to the other **B.C.D.A**. The small plates applied to the lower vane from three sides, which are straight on the part where they are closest to it, can be tightened or loosened by certain pins to the vane itself, so that they create slits equal on all sides, which may also be widened or narrowed as use requires. This can be provided by a certain peculiar mechanism from the other and inner side of the vane, by a single screw turned around, so that all the slits are loosened or constricted together equally and with a uniform movement without labor or loss of time. The fourth slit, which is cut in that part of the vane where it adheres to the ruler, remains thus perpetually; it is represented a little above **B.A**, and another slit equidistant from the plane of the ruler is seen in the upper vane near **F.E**. Although that interior slit, no less than the other three mentioned before, can also be competently diminished or increased by the application of a small addition, as the work requires. 

**THE USE** is in taking the altitudes of the stars: the ruler **I** is raised or lowered until the star is seen through the slit **D.A** at the edge of the other vane **A.E**; and at the same instant, just as much of it is also seen through the slit **B.C** next to the other side **G.F**. Then there is no doubt that a central and exact <term>collimation</term><note>The alignment of the sight-line of the instrument with the celestial object.</note> of the star itself has been made. If, however, one wishes to obtain the <term>azimuths</term><note>The horizontal angle of a celestial object.</note> at the same time, one must attend through another slit next to **C.D** at the front side **G.H**, and together through the slit **G.H** at...

<margin>the other</margin>

<summary>This page provides a mathematical proof for the accuracy of Tycho Brahe's transversal (diagonal) scales, arguing that the error between a straight scale and a circular arc is too small for the human eye to detect. It also describes his innovative sighting vanes (diopters) which used adjustable slits and a screw mechanism to achieve perfect alignment with celestial bodies.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Tycho Brahe’s technical descriptions of his astronomical equipment. the other [marked] F. E., and in this way a very prompt observation of the stars is established. However, in the case of the Sun, the rays admitted through the round hole of the upper <term>sighting vane</term><note>Latin: *pinnacidium*. These are the adjustable plates or "sights" on an instrument used for alignment. Tycho's design used two: one near the eye and one further away.</note> fill a certain circle designated on the inner part of the lower one from every side, according to the amount of light which the Sun produces through that hole, and thus provide the data being sought. Finally, we also note that in certain instruments, that sighting vane which is distant from the eye consists of a cylindrical shape, and thus the matter comes to the same result, except that with the Sun, the shadow of the cylinder must be observed; so also in the <term>armillary spheres</term><note>Astronomical instruments composed of nested metal rings representing the great circles of the celestial sphere.</note> we apply a certain smooth axis, so that <term>collimation</term><note>The alignment of the line of sight with the center of the celestial object.</note> can be made around it from every side; for both cylinders and smooth axes have this particular advantage, that they suffice not just for one but even for two observers simultaneously. The rest, the figure itself, or rather the practice with such instruments, will teach more fully.

[A decorative woodcut floral ornament appears here]

This method of observation through equidistant slits in the other sighting vane, necessity itself taught me: for stars are perceived through holes in the otherwise usual manner with the greatest difficulty, especially in that vane which is furthest from the eye, unless it were sufficiently large. And if this size were granted, some small portion of a degree could be lost, since it is not known whether the alignment becomes perfectly central. This I truly wonder was not noticed by previous astronomers, and that this inconvenience was not otherwise provided for. When a certain distinguished mathematician <note>Likely Paul Wittich, a traveling scholar who visited Tycho at Hven in 1580 and helped spread his methods.</note> visited me some years ago, coming on a long journey for the sake of seeing my instruments, and had inspected this way of denoting the stars most conveniently through slits arranged thus, he leaped for joy, asserting that he had now received the thing for which he had panted in vain for many years prior; he congratulated himself that for that reason alone he had not come to Denmark in vain. And afterwards, coming to Kassel, he applied it in some way to the Landgrave’s instruments <note>Refers to Wilhelm IV, Landgrave of Hesse-Kassel, a fellow astronomer and correspondent of Tycho.</note>, as also the

<meta>This page transitions from the technical description of Tycho Brahe’s sighting mechanisms and astronomical instruments to a laudatory poem celebrating his achievements and his royal patronage under King Frederick II of Denmark. The previous page detailed the physical use of the instruments; this page serves as a literary tribute to the man who used them.</meta>

->To the truly Illustrious and Noble man<-

# TYCHO BRAHE, LORD
## of KNUDSTRUP, PRINCE OF ASTRONOMERS,
### A Three-Metre, Four-Stanza Ode <note>Latin: *Ode Tricolos Tetrastrophos*. This is a technical term for a poem using three different types of meter within a four-line stanza structure.</note>.

When the Roman soldier once harried the swollen Getae with arms, or the Pontic sea, and by the Ganges, and against the Medes, he carried his eagles and sharp spears: The gods bound the powerful goddess <note>Referring to Victoria, the personification of Victory.</note> with a shackle so she could stay in no place for long, lest—visiting the fierce Geloni or the Parthians conspicuous for their hostile arrows—she might, in an insolent game, carry them to the heights, having turned the wheel of Rhamnidis <note>A name for Nemesis, the goddess of retribution and fortune, often depicted with a wheel.</note> to the very bottom. Thus Victory, bound with an iron chain and deprived of her swift wings, was once forced to cherish the Athenian homes. Who does not know that Jupiter stood bound by a shackle at the purple altars of Tyre? Envy, a wasting poison creeping through the innermost marrow, bringing everything to itself and nothing to another, <margin>Envy.</margin> begrudged that a celestial divinity could be worshipped through all peoples.

But Denmark, the proud ruler of the Baltic sea, did not; she—though she could have held you with a more just bond, O **TYCHO**—would not at last give you up to the envious German shores. By making your country’s name perennial, you, skillful man, have granted it to fly conspicuously through all nations: For everyone, instructed by your guidance through the wandering stars and the once-inextricable kingdoms of heaven, has turned their eyes and mind toward them. And by a more just path, one may designate with more certainty whether the terrifying Comets bring anything to the world: what the position of the poles may be, and how the fates vary: Whether he inhabits the wide shore of Germany or France, or the ancient Quadi, Bohemia, and the Marcomanni, or the kingdoms of either Hesperia <note>Poetic term for the lands of the West, usually Italy or Spain.</note>: Grateful, he will say he owes these votive songs to you; the youth devoted to the study of <term>Urania</term><note>The Greek Muse of Astronomy.</note> and <term>Mathematics</term><note>Latin: *Mathesi*. In this period, the term encompassed astronomy, geometry, and astrology.</note> will everywhere speak of you with a lovely hymn of praise.

For under your lead, the more liberated student of <term>the Stagirite</term><note>A title for Aristotle, born in Stagira. The poem suggests Tycho’s observations corrected Aristotelian physics.</note> will judge that many things you have committed to paper will live forever, yet many things were also unknown to him. Casting aside the old cataract <note>Latin: *glaucoma*. Used metaphorically for the blindness caused by uncritical adherence to tradition.</note> by which sluggishness protects itself in the decrees of the ancients, as if fascinated by their authority. Nay, whatever the headlong desire for profit drives men to do—to commit a raft to the shipwrecking waves and to seek the gold-bearing regions of Guinea: Or what forces one to go with Portuguese galleys to the ports of Calicut, rich in spices, and to those of China, Quinsay <note>An old name for Hangzhou, China.</note>, and Japan. This very man, while he scrutinizes the stars better by your guidance and runs more surely through the sea by a path unknown to the ancients, pays back his thanks to you, having spoken them with a grateful murmur for your labor.

Then he speaks also of Denmark, the honored nurse—nay, the mother: He speaks of you, **FREDERICK** <note>King Frederick II of Denmark, Tycho’s royal patron.</note>, shining with the long robe of Kings: He sings that you cherished in your kingdom (not the son of Jove, the hero of Tiryns <note>Hercules, who famously held up the sky for Atlas.</note>, on whose neck both poles of the world once hung more firmly, when the son of Iapetus <note>Atlas.</note> released his weary neck to a substitute), but this man here, who restrained the unobserved signs and the perpetual orbs that were without a fixed law. Who, carried above the fiery citadels by the gifts of his genius, beheld in which orb the Sun <note>Latin: *Phoebus*.</note> and Moon <note>Latin: *Phoebe*.</note> remain fixed.

How for them the earthly machine provides a center for both; how Venus, following the <term>Cyllenian star</term><note>Mercury, named for Mount Cyllene, the birthplace of Hermes/Mercury.</note>, circles the fire-haired chariot <note>The Sun.</note> in a larger circuit. How the Sun, the arbiter of celestial orbs and king of the world, moderates the orbit of Mars and the chariot of Jupiter and that of the slow-moving old man <note>Saturn.</note>, like a heart and a center, with ethereal art. So that now neither the Copernican dogmas nor your cycles, O Ptolemy, fail us.

There are those who perhaps adorn the Danish kingdoms with the trophies and honors of Mars. There are those who perhaps decorate the King, being prompt in counsel and by hand: But **TYCHO**, bold by the sharpness of his mind, has included within your kingdom, O **FREDERICK**, by your art, the choirs of Urania who were long ago exiles, and the stars called down from heaven: He has led the fame of your name and scepter above the mortal race of men, through all the paths of the ethereal boundary. Who would believe it possible? **TYCHO** gave a terrestrial heaven to your people (let it be permitted for me to explain this miracle with a new word); he made the mind and the empire equal to Olympus. And whoever works at the sacred altars of Urania, being grateful and not stained by the gall of envy, will confess more things are owed to your labor.

<summary>This page contains a Neo-Latin laudatory poem dedicated to Tycho Brahe, praising his astronomical achievements and his patron, King Frederick II of Denmark. It highlights Tycho's "Tychonic system" of the universe, where the Sun orbits the Earth while other planets orbit the Sun, presenting it as a superior alternative to Ptolemy and Copernicus.</summary>
<keywords>Tycho Brahe, King Frederick II, Danish history, Tychonic system, History of Astronomy, Neo-Latin poetry, Urania, Aristotle, Navigation, Patronage</keywords>

--- Page 111 ---
<meta>This page continues the poetic tribute to the astronomer Tycho Brahe. While the previous page focused on Tycho’s departure from Denmark and the personification of "Envy" that follows great men, this page celebrates his arrival in the Holy Roman Empire (Germany) and his reception by Emperor Rudolph II. It places Tycho in a lineage of great scholars like Regiomontanus and highlights his dual mastery of astronomy and alchemy.</meta>

Nor will our **Germany** <term>Germania; the Holy Roman Empire</term>, ungrateful, allow you to be plucked by envious oblivion: For while she was too eager to see you, she lamented your absence for seven three-year cycles <note>Original: *septem trieterides*. A period of 21 years, referring to the time Tycho spent established at his observatory, Uraniborg.</note>. Saluting you gently now that you are finally restored to her after her prayers, she says: "May he be present, a guest sent to me not without the will of the Gods."

She grieves enough that you, an exile to the world, have endured the freezes of the stiff **Baltic** <term>Balthicæ; referring to the Baltic Sea region</term>; given enough to the icy Great Bear <note>Original: *Trioni*. Referring to the constellation Septentriones, a symbol for the North.</note> and the stubbornly cruel North Wind, while you cherished the sacred hearths of **Urania** <term>Uraniæ; the Muse of Astronomy</term>. May you be present with a favoring power, and display this beam of your serene countenance through the **German** <term>Teuthonicas; Teutonic or German</term> cities.

Not even Penelope rejoiced so to see the hardworking **Ulysses**, nor the boy **Telemachus** who first recognized his parent, or the trembling mothers; as our **Germany** rejoices that you have come into this realm of kings, where there is an illustrious theater for your virtue, and a patron who is owed to your genius.

When the kindness of the Emperor **RUDOLPH**, mixed with Majesty, hears that you have departed from a fatherland that did not sufficiently consider such great monuments of its own honor, and that the choir of **Urania** came with you: He will strive of his own accord to join this little laurel to his own Scepters and triumphs. And he will cherish you and your arts with a kindly face.

We know how the celestial offspring of the House of **Austria** <term>Austriæ; the Habsburg dynasty</term> joins both the Muses and iron-clad Mars in both seasons, being of noble spirit: so that just as he soon crushed the insolent pride of the **Thracian** leader <note>Original: *Threicii ducis*. A classical reference to the Ottoman Sultan/Turks, with whom the Habsburgs were at war.</note> with his soldiers, while he seeks to soften his labors, he may be refreshed in the gardens of **Urania**.

The cultured Academy of **Vienna** <term>Viennæ; the University of Vienna</term> will praise with me the twin **Alberts**; the father and the son <note>Likely referring to Albert of Saxony and subsequent scholars who made Vienna a center for mathematics.</note>, who, devoted to the stars, carried a mind burning with sacred fire above the heights: from which he bore the surname of **THE ASTRONOMER**. Let me not be silent, **Frederick**, of the race of **Ernest**, you the long glory of your people and of the **German** Scepter;

Under this patron, Johannes **Regiomontanus** <term>Regiomontius; the famous 15th-century astronomer Joannes Müller</term> shone, the glory of **Urania**: whom the Fates, and envious spite, removed from the earth, taken by a death all too hurried. He gave us triangles <note>Referring to his foundational work on trigonometry.</note>, he gave us polished Mechanics: indeed, **Nuremberg** <term>Noriberga</term> stood aghast at him building, and the Emperor stood amazed, while he watched a rustic piece of wood fly in the middle of the sky <note>Refers to a legendary mechanical eagle or "automaton" said to have been built by Regiomontanus.</note>. Believe it, posterity, that the parent fled with his son from the Cretan prisons through the void <note>A comparison to the flight of Daedalus and Icarus.</note>.

---

What can Daedalian hands not do? And that mind matured in sacred knowledge by Phoebus <note>Apollo, god of the sun and wisdom.</note>, into which a celestial Prometheus instilled a particle of more divine genius? O **TYCHO**, too blessed is the land which will see the miracles of your genius, and your arts of **Fire-mastery** <term>Pyronomas; a term for alchemy or chemical arts involving fire</term>, and your right hand driving diseases into their strongholds with Medicine.

You are indeed the prince of **Urania's** troop; nor are you less polished by the study of all noble sciences and arts. O happy is he, to whom it shall be given to compare the harmony of the earthly globe from your mouth with the sky! So that here and there the stars and their Planets may belong to each world. You, powerful in the **Chemical** art and in metals, often command the wool-spinning goddesses <note>The Fates (Parcae), who spin the thread of life.</note> to produce the cut threads of life; you often command the empty boat of **Charon** <term>Charontis; the ferryman of the underworld</term> to sail past.

To you, those whom the freezes of **Norway** <term>Norvagiæ</term> and the horrid North constellations press upon, and the **Danish** kingdoms together with the **Swedes**, will pay back thanks to you who deserve them (if indeed any ages will perhaps repay favor to the good, and if envy permits you to be heaped with honor according to your merits).

The slaves of the criminal **Tartarean** gout <note>Original: *Tartaræ... podagræ*. Gout was a common and painful affliction; "Tartarean" refers to the underworld, implying hellish pain.</note> will groan for you; everywhere those consumed by wasting disease will complain, wet with weeping, that you have departed from your ancestral realms. We, however, congratulating you from here, sing a votive melody, and we gaze upon your longed-for face, and kiss your hands so long desired.

O you seven columns of the Imperial diadem, O leaders and kings <note>Referring to the Seven Prince-Electors of the Holy Roman Empire.</note>, whom a celestial ardor cooks with a more noble fire: I call upon you in the name of the sciences, and with me the choir of **Urania**: and the whole crown of the **Hyades** <term>Hiantidum; likely the Hyades star cluster, though the spelling is unusual</term>, which must be restored under your auspices, requires help. Do not allow this glory to be snatched away from the borders of **Germany** by the Italian cities, or the distant **Britons**, or the western **Franks**.

From there, rival **France** <term>Gallia</term> watches you from afar with sideways glances; the **Italians** will want you to have been negligent of praise; **Holland**, fertile in men, notes you with a watchful, un-turned eye, as do the furthest **Britons**: the more northern **Scot** and the daring **Spaniards** <term>Ibéri</term>, swollen with the discovery of the Ocean.

Let them see what each one pants for; I trust to see **Germany** shining with that laurel of praise above all others, by the hospitality shown to **Urania**.

---

->placed this out of devoted worship and love.<-
